02
Block 2 · Foundation

Declarative Pipeline anatomy

Goal: be able to write the skeleton pipeline → agent → stages → stage → steps → post from memory, and know why each block exists and what order is legal.

2.1 Two flavours: Declarative vs Scripted

📐 Declarative Learn this

Starts with pipeline { }. Fixed, opinionated structure. Jenkins can validate it, visualise it, and Blue Ocean can render it. Easy to read, hard to abuse. Use this 95% of the time.

🐍 Scripted

Starts with node { }. Raw Groovy — loops, classes, try/catch anywhere. Maximum power, easy to make unreadable. Use it inside a script { } block when declarative genuinely can't express something.

🧠The mental model Declarative says what should happen ("run these four stages, then always clean up"). Scripted says how ("do this Groovy, step by step"). Declarative pipelines are still Groovy underneath, which is why a syntax slip produces Groovy stack traces.

2.2 The skeleton — every legal top-level block

groovyThe complete declarative skeleton (all sections, correct order)
pipeline {                 // 1. REQUIRED. The whole file lives inside it.

    agent any              // 2. REQUIRED. WHERE the pipeline runs.

    options {              // 3. optional. Pipeline-wide behaviour.
        timestamps()                                  // add timestamps to logs
        timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES')            // kill runaway builds
        buildDiscarder(logRotator(numToKeepStr: '30'))// keep disk sane
        disableConcurrentBuilds()                     // one build at a time
    }

    parameters {           // 4. optional. Inputs the user fills before "Build".
        choice(name: 'ENV', choices: ['dev','qa','prod'], description: 'Target')
        booleanParam(name: 'RUN_TESTS', defaultValue: true)
    }

    triggers {             // 5. optional. WHAT starts the pipeline.
        pollSCM('H/5 * * * *')   // check Git every 5 minutes
    }

    tools {                // 6. optional. Auto-install & put tools on PATH.
        nodejs 'node20'
    }

    environment {          // 7. optional. Variables for every step.
        APP_NAME = 'nodejs-app'
    }

    stages {               // 8. REQUIRED. The work.
        stage('Build') {
            steps {        //    REQUIRED inside a stage.
                echo "Building ${APP_NAME}"
                sh 'npm ci'
            }
        }
    }

    post {                 // 9. optional. Runs after stages, whatever happened.
        always   { echo 'always' }
        success  { echo 'green' }
        failure  { echo 'red' }
        cleanup  { echo 'last' }
    }
}
⚠️Four rules that cause 90% of "why won't my Jenkinsfile compile"
  1. pipeline, agent and stages are mandatory at the top level.
  2. Every stage must contain a steps block (or parallel/matrix/when variants).
  3. Order matters at the top level: agent → options → parameters → triggers → tools → environment → stages → post.
  4. stages can contain only stage blocks — no naked steps floating inside it.

2.3 agent — where the work happens

agent answers one question: on what machine, and inside what container, do these steps run? Jenkins allocates a workspace and an executor there.

FormMeaningWhen you use it
agent anyAny available node/executorLearning, single-server setups
agent noneNo global agent — each stage declares its ownMixing OSes/tools in one pipeline (Block 13)
agent { label 'docker' }Run only on nodes labelled dockerRouting builds to capable machines
agent { node { label 'linux'; customWorkspace '/data/ci' } }Node + custom workspace pathDisk or path constraints
agent { docker { image 'node:20-alpine' } }Run steps inside a containerZero-install toolchains (Block 13)
agent { dockerfile { filename 'Dockerfile.ci'; args '-v /tmp:/tmp' } }Build an image from a Dockerfile, run inside itReproducible build environments
groovyagent none + per-stage agents (the professional pattern)
pipeline {
    agent none                       // no machine reserved for the whole pipeline
    stages {
        stage('Test on Node 20') {
            agent { docker { image 'node:20-alpine' } }   // reserved just for this stage
            steps { sh 'node -v && npm test' }
        }
        stage('Build image') {
            agent { label 'docker' }                      // needs the docker socket
            steps { sh 'docker build -t app .' }
        }
    }
}
💡What agent really does It (1) picks a node by label, (2) grabs an executor slot, (3) creates/locks a workspace directory, and (4) for docker/dockerfile, starts a container and mounts that workspace inside it as the working directory. All later sh steps run there — which is why files created in one stage are visible in the next on the same agent.

2.4 stages, stage and steps

  • stages is a container — it holds one or more stage blocks and nothing else.
  • stage('Name') is a logical unit with a name shown in the UI. It is not a sandbox: it does not isolate files or change directories. It exists so you can see timing, pass/fail and rerun per unit.
  • steps holds the actual commands. Steps are things Jenkins knows how to run: sh, echo, git, junit, archiveArtifacts.
groovySteps you will use constantly
steps {
    echo 'Print a line to the console'                 // built-in
    sh 'npm ci'                                        // run a shell command (Linux/macOS)
    sh label: 'install deps', script: 'npm ci'         // with a nicer log label
    sh returnStdout: true, script: 'git rev-parse --short HEAD'   // capture output
    sh returnStatus: true, script: 'grep -q X file'    // get exit code without failing
    bat 'mvn test'                                     // Windows
    dir('python-app') { sh 'pytest' }                  // run inside a sub-directory
    deleteDir()                                        // wipe the workspace
    script {                                           // escape hatch: real Groovy
        def v = sh(script: 'node -v', returnStdout: true).trim()
        echo "Node version is ${v}"
    }
}

Single quotes vs double quotes — the #1 beginner bug

groovyGString interpolation
def TAG = 'v1.4'

echo "Tag is ${TAG}"    // ✅ double quotes  -> "Tag is v1.4"   (GString, interpolates)
echo 'Tag is ${TAG}'    // ❌ single quotes  -> "Tag is ${TAG}" (plain String, literal)

2.5 post — the cleanup & notification block

post runs after all stages finish. Conditions are evaluated in a fixed order, and multiple conditions can run (e.g. always and success both fire on a green build).

ConditionRuns whenTypical use
alwaysEvery time, no matter the resultPublish reports, clean workspace, notify
successAll stages passedSlack "green", tag image as stable
failureAny stage threw an errorAlert, attach log, open ticket
unstableBuild marked unstable (failed tests, quality gate)Warn but don't page anyone
abortedSomeone cancelled itRelease locks, scale down
changedResult differs from the previous build"Back to green" messages
fixedFailed before, success nowCelebrate, close incident
regressionSuccess before, failing nowEscalate immediately
unsuccessfulNot success (failure/unstable/aborted)Generic alerting
cleanupAlways, and last — after everything elseDelete temp files, stop containers

Block 11 goes deep on post. For now, just wire up always and failure.

2.6 ✍️ Your first real Jenkinsfiles (v2)

Replace nodejs-app/Jenkinsfile and python-app/Jenkinsfile with these, commit, push, and run. Nothing fancy yet — the point is that the structure works and the UI shows four stages.

groovynodejs-app/Jenkinsfile — v2
// ============================================================
//  nodejs-app/Jenkinsfile  --  v2  (Block 2: anatomy)
// ============================================================
pipeline {
    agent any

    options {
        timestamps()
        timeout(time: 15, unit: 'MINUTES')
    }

    environment {
        APP_NAME = 'nodejs-app'
    }

    stages {
        stage('Info') {
            steps {
                echo "Building ${APP_NAME} - build #${env.BUILD_NUMBER}"
                echo "Workspace: ${env.WORKSPACE}"
                sh 'node -v && npm -v'
            }
        }

        stage('Build') {
            steps {
                echo 'Block 5 will install dependencies here'
            }
        }

        stage('Test') {
            steps {
                echo 'Block 6 will run the Jest suite here'
            }
        }
    }

    post {
        always  { echo "Finished with status: ${currentBuild.currentResult}" }
        failure { echo 'Something broke - Block 11 will notify us properly' }
    }
}
groovypython-app/Jenkinsfile — v2
// ============================================================
//  python-app/Jenkinsfile  --  v2  (Block 2: anatomy)
// ============================================================
pipeline {
    agent any

    options {
        timestamps()
        timeout(time: 15, unit: 'MINUTES')
    }

    environment {
        APP_NAME = 'python-app'
    }

    stages {
        stage('Info') {
            steps {
                echo "Building ${APP_NAME} - build #${env.BUILD_NUMBER}"
                echo "Workspace: ${env.WORKSPACE}"
                sh 'python3 --version'
            }
        }

        stage('Build') {
            steps {
                echo 'Block 5 will create the venv and install deps here'
            }
        }

        stage('Test') {
            steps {
                echo 'Block 6 will run pytest here'
            }
        }
    }

    post {
        always  { echo "Finished with status: ${currentBuild.currentResult}" }
        failure { echo 'Something broke - Block 11 will notify us properly' }
    }
}
  1. Create the job: New Item → name nodejs-app → Pipeline → OK.
  2. Point it at Git: Pipeline section → Definition: Pipeline script from SCM → SCM: Git → Repository URL: your repo → Script Path: nodejs-app/Jenkinsfile.
  3. Save → Build Now. Watch the Stage View fill in. Click any stage → Logs to see only that stage's output.
  4. Do it again for python-app with Script Path python-app/Jenkinsfile.
📌NoteBoth jobs can share one Git repo — that's exactly how monorepos work. Each job reads a different Jenkinsfile path. We'll formalise this with Multibranch in Block 15.

2.7 ✅ Checklist

  • I can list the mandatory top-level blocks without looking.
  • Both jobs ran and showed 3 stages in the UI with per-stage timings.
  • I deliberately broke the syntax (removed steps) and read the resulting error.
  • I can explain what agent any actually allocates.
  • I know the difference between "${VAR}" and '${VAR}'.

2.8 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1Declarative vs Scripted pipeline — which do you use and why?
Declarative. It has a fixed structure Jenkins can validate and visualise, it's readable for the whole team, and it prevents the "clever Groovy nobody can maintain" problem. I drop into script { } only when I genuinely need Groovy logic that declarative can't express.
Q2What are the mandatory sections of a declarative pipeline?
pipeline at the root, and inside it agent and stages. Every stage must then contain a steps block.
Q3What does agent none mean?
No agent is allocated for the pipeline as a whole; each stage declares its own agent. It lets one pipeline run tests inside a Node container and build an image on a Docker host, without holding two executors for the whole run.
Q4Do stages run in parallel by default?
No — sequentially, in the order written. Parallelism is explicit with a parallel block (Block 21), and it still needs executors available.
Q5What's the difference between always and cleanup in post?
Both always run, but cleanup runs last, after every other post condition — including after a failure in always. Anything that must happen even if notifications break (removing temp files, stopping containers) belongs in cleanup.

2.9 🔥 Common errors

ErrorCauseFix
No such DSL method 'stage' found among stepsA stage outside stages, or steps written directly in stagesWrap stages in stages { }; every stage needs steps { }
Required context class hudson.FilePath is missingUsed a file step (e.g. sh) outside steps, typically in post at pipeline level without an agentPut the step inside a steps block, or add agent/node scope
Missing required parameter: "agent"You wrote agent with no valueUse agent any/agent none/agent { label '…' }
Variable prints as ${VAR} literallySingle-quoted stringUse double quotes for interpolation: "${VAR}"
WorkflowScript: 3: expecting '}', found 'stages'A missing brace or comma earlier in the fileRead the line number — the real mistake is usually a few lines above

2.10 Under the hood — how a declarative pipeline actually runs

This is the most useful piece of Jenkins theory there is, and it explains errors that otherwise look random.

  1. Parse. The declarative structure is validated first — which is why a missing steps block fails instantly with a structural message instead of a Groovy stack trace.
  2. CPS transformation. Your Groovy is rewritten into Continuation Passing Style so the pipeline can be paused and resumed — that's what lets a build survive a controller restart.
  3. Execution. Steps dispatch: sh forks a process on the agent, echo writes to the build log, junit parses files on the controller, docker.build talks to the daemon.
  4. Durability. Program state is flushed to jobs/*/builds/<n>/program.dat so a crash resumes from the last checkpoint.
🧠Why Groovy behaves oddly inside a pipeline Because of CPS, some ordinary Groovy is unsafe: closures that mutate state, loops over non-serialisable objects, and certain collection methods. Annotate pure helper functions with @NonCPS:
groovy@NonCPS — pure computation only, never steps
import com.cloudbees.groovy.cps.NonCPS

@NonCPS
String joinNames(List<String> names) { names.join(', ') }

pipeline {
    agent any
    stages {
        stage('Demo') {
            steps {
                script { echo joinNames(['jenkins', 'docker', 'sonar']) }
            }
        }
    }
}
Rule: an @NonCPS method must never call a pipeline step (sh, echo), because it cannot be suspended.

Complete options reference

OptionEffect
buildDiscarder(logRotator(numToKeepStr:'30', artifactNumToKeepStr:'5', daysToKeepStr:'60'))Caps history and artifacts — the disk safety net
disableConcurrentBuilds() / disableConcurrentBuilds(abortPrevious:true)Queue or cancel when a run is already in flight
timeout(time:30, unit:'MINUTES')Hard cap; result becomes ABORTED
retry(2)Rerun the whole pipeline on failure (prefer stage-level)
timestamps()Prefix every log line with elapsed and wall time
ansiColor('xterm')Render ANSI colour from npm/pytest output
skipDefaultCheckout(true)No implicit checkout; call checkout scm yourself
skipStagesAfterUnstable()Stop further stages once the build is unstable
quietPeriod(5)Wait N seconds before starting — coalesces commit bursts
checkoutToSubdirectory('src')Check out into a named subfolder
newContainerPerStage()With docker agents: a fresh container per stage
preserveStashes()Keep stashes after the run (debugging aid)

2.11 Lab — three deliberate syntax failures

🧪Lab 2.1 · Read the error, fix the error Run each in Replay and match the message to the cause.
groovyFault 1 — steps directly inside stages
stages {
    sh 'npm ci'      // ✗ No such DSL method 'sh' found among steps
}
groovyFault 2 — single quotes kill interpolation
environment { T = '${BUILD_NUMBER}' }
echo '${T}'      // prints literally: ${T}
echo "${T}"      // prints: 42
groovyFault 3 — post block with agent none
pipeline {
  agent none
  stages { stage('a') { agent any; steps { echo 'hi' } } }
  post { always { sh 'echo bye' } }   // ✗ Required context class hudson.FilePath is missing
}
// Fix:  post { always { node('any') { sh 'echo bye' } } }

2.12 Production notes — pipeline hygiene

HabitWhy it pays off
Every pipeline opens with options { timestamps() timeout() buildDiscarder() }Three lines prevent the three most common production incidents
Name stages after outcomes ("Test", "Push"), not tools ("npm")The stage view stays meaningful when you swap tooling
Keep logic out of script{} where declarative worksDeclarative is validated, visualised and safer
One Jenkinsfile per app, never one mega-pipelineSmall blast radius, clear ownership, faster runs
Label every meaningful sh with label:The console log stays readable at 2 a.m.

2.13 More interview Q&A

Q6What is CPS in Jenkins pipelines?
Continuation Passing Style: Groovy is transformed so execution can be paused and resumed, which is how a build survives a controller restart. It also explains why some ordinary Groovy misbehaves in pipelines and why pure helpers are annotated @NonCPS.
Q7Why do some Groovy methods fail inside a pipeline?
CPS-transformed code must be serialisable and resumable. Collection methods with closures, each over non-serialisable objects and mutating shared state can throw NotSerializableException. I move that logic into an @NonCPS method or a shared-library class under src/.
Q8Can a running pipeline survive a Jenkins restart?
Yes, up to the configured durability level: program state is written to disk, so after a restart the build resumes at the last checkpoint and in-flight steps are re-run. That is a direct consequence of the CPS design.
Q9When do you use script{}?
When declarative genuinely can't express the logic — building a dynamic parallel map, conditional loops, parsing JSON into variables, calling Groovy libraries. Everything else stays declarative so it can be validated and visualised.
Q10How do you keep Jenkinsfiles readable as they grow?
Move reusable logic to a shared library, keep stages short and outcome-named, use parameters instead of hard-coded values, and set currentBuild.description so the history page explains itself. Past roughly 150 lines, the repeated parts belong in vars/.
03
Block 3 · Foundation

Snippet Generator & syntax tooling

Goal: stop memorising step syntax. Generate it, validate it, lint it — and iterate with Replay so you never wait on a Git push to find out a step is wrong.

3.1 The four tools built into every Jenkins

🔧 Snippet Generator

/pipeline-syntax/ — pick a step, fill a form, get the exact Groovy. For steps.

📐 Declarative Directive Generator

/directive-generator/ — same idea, but for sections like options, when, triggers, parameters.

📖 Global Variable Reference

/pipeline-syntax/globals — every built-in variable (env, currentBuild, params, scm) with real values from your own controller.

🔁 Replay

On any finished build: edit the Jenkinsfile in the browser and rerun instantly, without a commit.

🔗How to reach them From any job: click Pipeline Syntax in the left menu. Or go direct: http://<jenkins>:8080/pipeline-syntax/. They're always available on the controller, even before you create a job.

3.2 Using the Snippet Generator (do this once, properly)

  1. Sample Step ▾ → choose the step, e.g. sh: Shell Script. The form changes to match.
  2. Fill the fields — for sh you get Script, plus advanced options like returnStdout and returnStatus.
  3. Click Generate Groovy. Copy the output into your Jenkinsfile's steps block.
groovyOutput of the generator for a few common steps
// 1. sh  ->  Shell Script
sh 'npm ci'

//    ...with advanced options ticked:
sh label: 'install dependencies', returnStdout: true, script: 'npm ci'

// 2. git  ->  Git  (Block 4)
git branch: 'main', credentialsId: 'github-ssh', url: 'git@github.com:you/repo.git'

// 3. checkout  ->  Check out from version control  (modern form, Block 4)
checkout scmGit(branches: [[name: '*/main']],
                extensions: [],
                userRemoteConfigs: [[credentialsId: 'github-ssh',
                                     url: 'git@github.com:you/repo.git']])

// 4. withCredentials  ->  Bind credentials to variables  (Block 8)
withCredentials([usernamePassword(credentialsId: 'dockerhub',
                                  passwordVariable: 'DOCKER_PASS',
                                  usernameVariable: 'DOCKER_USER')]) {
    sh 'echo $DOCKER_USER'
}

// 5. junit  ->  Publish JUnit test result report  (Block 6)
junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml', allowEmptyResults: true

// 6. archiveArtifacts  ->  Archive the artifacts  (Block 11)
archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'coverage/**', fingerprint: true

// 7. cleanWs  ->  Delete workspace when build is done  (Block 11)
cleanWs()
🎯The professional shortcut You will never remember checkout scmGit(...) by heart. Nobody does. What senior engineers remember is which steps exist and where to generate them. That's the skill. Use the generator for the exact syntax every single time.

3.3 Declarative Directive Generator — for blocks, not steps

When you need correct syntax for when, options, triggers, parameters or tools, use /directive-generator/. Choose the directive, fill the form, paste.

groovyGenerated declarative directives
// when: branch is main          (directive: when -> branch)
when {
    branch 'main'
}

// when: any of / all of / not   (directive: when -> allOf / anyOf / not)
when {
    allOf {
        branch 'main'
        expression { params.DEPLOY == true }
    }
}

// options: timeout + timestamps (directive: options -> timeout / timestamps)
options {
    timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES')
    timestamps()
}

// triggers: pollSCM             (directive: triggers -> pollSCM)
triggers {
    pollSCM('H/5 * * * *')
}

// parameters: choice            (directive: parameters -> choice)
parameters {
    choice choices: ['dev', 'qa', 'prod'], description: 'Target env', name: 'ENV'
}

3.4 Validate a Jenkinsfile without running it

Jenkins ships a REST endpoint that parses your Jenkinsfile and returns either "successfully validated" or the exact parse error. Wire it into your editor or a pre-commit hook.

bashscripts/validate-jenkinsfile.sh
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Validate a Jenkinsfile against the controller WITHOUT triggering a build.
# Usage: ./scripts/validate-jenkinsfile.sh nodejs-app/Jenkinsfile
set -euo pipefail
JENKINS_URL="${JENKINS_URL:-http://localhost:8080}"
USER="${JENKINS_USER:?set JENKINS_USER}"
TOKEN="${JENKINS_TOKEN:?set JENKINS_TOKEN}"   # Manage Jenkins -> Users -> API Token
FILE="${1:?path to Jenkinsfile}"

CRUMB=$(curl -s -u "$USER:$TOKEN" "$JENKINS_URL/crumbIssuer/api/xml?xpath=concat(//crumbRequestField,\":\",//crumb)")

curl -s -u "$USER:$TOKEN" -H "$CRUMB" \
     -F "jenkinsfile=<$FILE" \
     "$JENKINS_URL/pipeline-model-converter/validate" | tee /tmp/validate.out

grep -q "successfully validated" /tmp/validate.out \
  && echo "✅ Jenkinsfile is valid" \
  || { echo "❌ Jenkinsfile has errors (see above)"; exit 1; }
⚠️Validation is syntax-only This endpoint checks that the pipeline parses. It does not check that your credentials exist, that the branch is real, or that npm will succeed. Syntax valid ≠ build green.

3.5 Tooling around Jenkins

ToolWhat it gives you
Replay (built-in)Edit + rerun a failed build's Jenkinsfile in the browser. The fastest debug loop that exists.
VS Code · Jenkins Doc + Pipeline LinterSnippets, auto-complete and offline linting while you type.
npm-groovy-lintCLI linter for Groovy/Jenkinsfile: npx npm-groovy-lint --path Jenkinsfile
Jenkinsfile in pre-commitRun the validate endpoint before every commit so broken syntax never reaches CI.
Blue Ocean / Pipeline Graph ViewPer-stage logs, visual graph, rerun a single failed stage.

3.6 The debug loop I actually use

groovyThree tricks that save hours
// 1. Print everything the pipeline can see
sh 'env | sort'                       // shell environment
echo sh(script: 'printenv | sort', returnStdout: true)
echo "Params: ${params}"
echo "Env: ${env.getEnvironment()}"

// 2. Turn on shell tracing for ONE command (instead of the whole job)
sh 'set -x; npm ci'

// 3. Keep the workspace when a build fails, so you can inspect it
post {
    failure { archiveArtifacts artifacts: '**/*.log', allowEmptyArchive: true }
    // and NEVER cleanWs() while you are still debugging
}

3.7 ✅ Checklist

  • Opened /pipeline-syntax/ and generated an sh step by hand.
  • Generated a when { branch 'main' } directive from /directive-generator/.
  • Opened /pipeline-syntax/globals and found env.BUILD_URL.
  • Used Replay to change a stage name and rerun without committing.
  • Validated a Jenkinsfile with the pipeline-model-converter/validate endpoint.

3.8 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1How do you write a Jenkinsfile without memorising syntax?
I use the built-in Snippet Generator for steps and the Declarative Directive Generator for sections, validate with the pipeline-model-converter/validate REST endpoint, and iterate with Replay so I don't need a Git commit per attempt. What I do memorise is the catalogue of available steps.
Q2What is Replay and when is it useful?
Replay lets you edit the Jenkinsfile of a completed build directly in the browser and rerun it. It's ideal for debugging — you iterate in seconds without pushing commits. Once it's green you copy the final version back into the repo and commit.
Q3Someone says their Jenkinsfile "compiles" but the build fails. Explain.
Validation only proves the Groovy parses and the declarative structure is legal. Runtime problems — a missing credential ID, an unreachable branch, a failing npm ci — are discovered only when steps execute.

3.7 Under the hood — where the generator gets its list

Every Jenkins step is a Java class registered for discovery (@Extension + Describable with @DataBoundConstructor/@DataBoundSetter). The generator reads that metadata, renders a form, and serialises your inputs back to Groovy. Three consequences:

  • A step only appears if its plugin is installed. "No such DSL method" usually means a missing plugin, not bad syntax.
  • Not every step has a form. Older steps expose only raw parameters under Advanced.
  • Generated code is canonical. When unsure, generate — don't invent.

Catalogue of steps you should know exist

CategorySteps
Files & workspacedir · deleteDir · fileExists · readFile · writeFile · readJSON · writeJSON · stash · unstash · cleanWs
Shellsh · bat · powershell · returnStdout · returnStatus · label
SCMcheckout · git · scmGit · changelog
CredentialswithCredentials · sshagent · withVault (plugin)
Reportsjunit · recordCoverage · recordIssues · archiveArtifacts · publishHTML
Flowparallel · timeout · retry · waitUntil · sleep · lock · milestone · input · build
Resultserror · catchError · warnError · unstable
Dockerdocker.build · docker.withRegistry · image.inside/withRun/run/push/pull
NotifyslackSend · emailext · httpRequest

3.8 Lab — generate three steps you'll use every day

🧪Lab 3.1 · Snippet Generator drill Open /pipeline-syntax/, generate each of these, and compare with the canonical output.
groovy1) sh with a label and captured output
// Sample Step: sh: Shell Script
//   Script:  git rev-parse --short HEAD
//   Advanced -> [x] Return standard output
sh label: 'read short commit sha', returnStdout: true, script: 'git rev-parse --short HEAD'
groovy2) junit with tolerant settings
// Sample Step: junit: Publish JUnit test result report
//   Test report XMLs: **/reports/junit.xml
//   [x] Allow empty results   [x] Keep long stdout/stderr
junit allowEmptyResults: true, keepLongStdio: true, testResults: '**/reports/junit.xml'
groovy3) withCredentials (secret text)
// Sample Step: withCredentials: Bind credentials to variables
//   Bindings: Secret text -> Variable: SONAR_TOKEN, Credentials: sonar-token
withCredentials([string(credentialsId: 'sonar-token', variable: 'SONAR_TOKEN')]) {
    sh 'echo "token length: ${#SONAR_TOKEN}"'      // never echo the value itself
}
Then open /directive-generator/ and generate options → timeout and when → anyOf.

3.9 Editor & CLI tooling

bashLocal lint + a pre-commit hook
# Local Groovy lint (no Jenkins needed)
npx npm-groovy-lint --path nodejs-app/Jenkinsfile --failon warning

# Block broken syntax before it reaches CI
cat > .git/hooks/pre-commit <<'HOOK'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -e
for f in $(git diff --cached --name-only | grep -E 'Jenkinsfile$'); do
  echo "validating $f"
  ./scripts/validate-jenkinsfile.sh "$f"
done
HOOK
chmod +x .git/hooks/pre-commit
json.vscode/settings.json
{
  "files.associations": { "Jenkinsfile*": "groovy" },
  "editor.formatOnSave": false,
  "[groovy]": { "editor.tabSize": 4 }
}

3.10 More interview Q&A

Q4How do you lint a Jenkinsfile in CI itself?
I run npm-groovy-lint plus the Jenkins /pipeline-model-converter/validate endpoint in a lightweight "pipeline lint" job and in a pre-commit hook, so broken syntax is caught before it ever consumes an executor.
Q5Where do you find the list of available environment variables?
/pipeline-syntax/globals — the Global Variable Reference, which renders the real values from my controller including env, currentBuild, params, docker and scm.
Q6A step you generated isn't recognised at runtime. Why?
The generator lists steps from plugins installed on the controller that rendered it. If the plugin is missing where the pipeline runs, or was updated and the step renamed, you get "No such DSL method". I verify the plugin in Manage Jenkins → Plugins and check the changelog for renames.
04
Block 4 · Foundation

Git + GitHub integration

Goal: Jenkins pulls your code from GitHub automatically, authenticated, without a human typing a password — the single most fundamental CI capability.

4.1 Two ways to authenticate (learn both)

🔑 SSH key Preferred for servers

A key pair. Private key lives in the Jenkins credentials store; public key is registered on GitHub. No expiry, no scope juggling, works for deploy keys too.

🎟️ HTTPS + Personal Access Token

Uses a GitHub PAT as the password. Easy to rotate, easy to revoke, and required for the GitHub API (Block 15 multibranch, PR status).

4.2 Option A — SSH key (recommended)

  1. Generate the key as the jenkins user — not as root, or the file permissions will be wrong and ssh will silently refuse to use it.
bashOn the EC2 box — as the jenkins user
sudo -iu jenkins bash                       # become the jenkins user
mkdir -p ~/.ssh && chmod 700 ~/.ssh
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "jenkins@ci" -f ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 -N ""   # no passphrase
cat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub                   # 1) copy this to GitHub

# 2) TRUST github.com's host key -- without this the first checkout hangs or fails
ssh-keyscan -t rsa,ed25519 github.com >> ~/.ssh/known_hosts
chmod 600 ~/.ssh/known_hosts

# 3) prove it before touching Jenkins (fail here = fail in the pipeline)
ssh -T git@github.com                       # -> "Hi <you>! You've successfully authenticated"
cat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519                       # 4) copy this PRIVATE key into Jenkins
uiRegister the key in both places
GITHUB  (public key)
  Repo → Settings → Deploy keys → Add deploy key    (read-only, per repo)   - preferred
  or: GitHub → Settings → SSH and GPG keys → New SSH key   (all your repos)

JENKINS (private key)
  Manage Jenkins → Credentials → System → Global credentials → Add Credentials
    Kind         : SSH Username with private key
    ID           : github-ssh            <-- you will reference this exact string
    Username     : git
    Private Key  : Enter directly → paste the whole -----BEGIN ... KEY----- block
                   (include the BEGIN and END lines)
⚠️Deploy key vs account key A deploy key is scoped to one repository and can be read-only — perfect for a CI that only clones. An account key grants access to every repo you can see. In interviews, "we used per-repo read-only deploy keys" is the stronger answer.

4.3 Option B — HTTPS with a Personal Access Token

  1. GitHub → Settings → Developer settings → Personal access tokens → Tokens (classic) → Generate new token. Scopes: repo (private repos) and admin:repo_hook if you want Jenkins to create the webhook for you (Block 10).
  2. Jenkins → Credentials → System → Global → Add Credentials → Kind: Username with password → Username: your GitHub username → Password: the token → ID: github-pat.
🚨Never put a token in the Jenkinsfile A PAT in a Jenkinsfile is a credential committed to Git, visible in every fork, clone and CI log. Tokens go in the credentials store and are referenced by ID. We cover injection in Block 8.

4.4 The checkout step

groovyGenerated by the Snippet Generator → "checkout: Check out from version control"
checkout scmGit(
    branches: [[name: '*/main']],
    extensions: [
        cleanBeforeCheckout(),                       // wipe workspace before cloning
        [$class: 'CloneOption', shallow: true, depth: 1, noTags: false],
        [$class: 'SubmoduleOption', recursiveSubmodules: true]
    ],
    userRemoteConfigs: [[
        credentialsId: 'github-ssh',
        url: 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git'
    ]]
)
groovySimple, readable, enough for most repos
git branch: 'main',
    url: 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git',
    credentialsId: 'github-ssh'
groovyInherits whatever the job's SCM config says (essential for Multibranch, Block 15)
// Reads the SCM + branch from the job configuration.
// In a Multibranch Pipeline, `scm` is the branch being built --
// so you MUST use this form there, never hard-code `main`.
checkout scm

// ...and to control it (skip the automatic checkout, do it yourself):
options { skipDefaultCheckout(true) }
stages {
    stage('Checkout') {
        steps { checkout scm }
    }
}
ExtensionWhy you'd use it
cleanBeforeCheckout()Delete stale files before cloning. Prevents "it works on my machine" from leftover artifacts.
[$class:'CloneOption', shallow:true, depth:1]Shallow clone — much faster on big repos. Not for pipelines that need full history/tags.
[$class:'SparseCheckoutPaths']Clone only nodejs-app/** in a monorepo.
[$class:'LocalBranch']Check out a real local branch instead of detached HEAD (needed if you push tags back).

4.5 Useful Git facts inside a pipeline

groovyRead commit metadata for image tags and reports
script {
    env.GIT_COMMIT_SHORT = sh(script: 'git rev-parse --short HEAD', returnStdout: true).trim()
    env.GIT_BRANCH_NAME  = sh(script: 'git rev-parse --abbrev-ref HEAD', returnStdout: true).trim()
    env.GIT_AUTHOR       = sh(script: "git log -1 --pretty=format:'%an'", returnStdout: true).trim()
    env.GIT_MESSAGE      = sh(script: "git log -1 --pretty=format:'%s'", returnStdout: true).trim()
    echo "Building ${env.GIT_COMMIT_SHORT} on ${env.GIT_BRANCH_NAME} by ${env.GIT_AUTHOR}"
}

4.6 ✍️ Jenkinsfiles v4 — real checkout

groovynodejs-app/Jenkinsfile — v4
pipeline {
    agent any
    options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 20, unit: 'MINUTES') }

    environment {
        APP_NAME  = 'nodejs-app'
        REPO_URL  = 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git'
        GIT_CRED  = 'github-ssh'          // credential ID from Manage Jenkins
    }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) {                       // keep each app in its own folder
                    checkout scmGit(
                        branches: [[name: '*/main']],
                        extensions: [cleanBeforeCheckout()],
                        userRemoteConfigs: [[credentialsId: env.GIT_CRED, url: env.REPO_URL]]
                    )
                }
                script {
                    dir(APP_NAME) {
                        env.GIT_SHA = sh(script: 'git rev-parse --short HEAD', returnStdout: true).trim()
                    }
                    echo "Checked out ${env.GIT_SHA}"
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Build') { steps { echo 'Block 5' } }
        stage('Test')  { steps { echo 'Block 6' } }
    }

    post { always { echo "Result: ${currentBuild.currentResult}" } }
}
groovypython-app/Jenkinsfile — v4
pipeline {
    agent any
    options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 20, unit: 'MINUTES') }

    environment {
        APP_NAME  = 'python-app'
        REPO_URL  = 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git'
        GIT_CRED  = 'github-ssh'
    }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) {
                    checkout scmGit(
                        branches: [[name: '*/main']],
                        extensions: [cleanBeforeCheckout()],
                        userRemoteConfigs: [[credentialsId: env.GIT_CRED, url: env.REPO_URL]]
                    )
                }
                script {
                    dir(APP_NAME) {
                        env.GIT_SHA = sh(script: 'git rev-parse --short HEAD', returnStdout: true).trim()
                    }
                    echo "Checked out ${env.GIT_SHA}"
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Build') { steps { echo 'Block 5' } }
        stage('Test')  { steps { echo 'Block 6' } }
    }

    post { always { echo "Result: ${currentBuild.currentResult}" } }
}
📌From Block 15 onward Hard-coding the URL and branch is fine now, but Multibranch jobs inject the right branch automatically — that's when we switch to checkout scm. Both apps' final Jenkinsfiles use that form.

4.7 ✅ Checklist

  • ssh -T git@github.com succeeds when run as the jenkins user.
  • Credential github-ssh exists in Jenkins with ID spelled exactly as in the Jenkinsfile.
  • Both pipelines show a green Checkout stage that prints a commit SHA.
  • You know what changes when a branch is renamed (the branches value).

4.8 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1How does Jenkins authenticate to a private Git repo?
With a credential stored in the Jenkins credentials store and referenced by ID. Typically an SSH private key whose public half is registered as a GitHub deploy key, or a PAT over HTTPS. The private key never appears in the Jenkinsfile — only the credential ID does.
Q2checkout scm vs git ... — when do you use which?
checkout scm inherits the SCM and branch from the job configuration, so it's mandatory in Multibranch pipelines where the branch varies per build. An explicit git/checkout scmGit step is used when the pipeline must control URL, branch or clone extensions itself.
Q3Why use a shallow clone?
depth: 1 downloads only the latest commit, cutting clone time and disk on large repos. The trade-off is no history or tags, so it's unsuitable for pipelines that compute version numbers from history or push tags.
Q4How do you stop Jenkins from auto-checking out twice?
options { skipDefaultCheckout(true) } disables the implicit checkout, then I run checkout scm explicitly inside a stage — which gives control over ordering, extensions and workspace layout.

4.9 🔥 Common errors

ErrorCauseFix
Host key verification failedgithub.com not in jenkins user's known_hostssudo -iu jenkins ssh-keyscan github.com >> ~/.ssh/known_hosts
Permission denied (publickey)Public key not on GitHub, or private key missing BEGIN/END linesRe-copy id_ed25519.pub to GitHub; paste the full private key into the credential
Could not resolve credentialsIdTypo in the ID stringCredentials are matched by exact ID — copy it from the credentials list
WARNING: UNPROTECTED PRIVATE KEY FILEKey file permissions too openchmod 600 ~/.ssh/id_ed25519 as the jenkins user
Clone works as root but not in JenkinsKey lives in /root/.sshJenkins runs as jenkins; its keys must be in /var/lib/jenkins/.ssh

4.10 Under the hood — what "checkout" really does

The Git plugin keeps a real .git directory in the workspace: it fetches, checks out the ref, and optionally cleans. Three details that matter in practice:

ConceptWhat it meansWhy you care
Branch specifier*/main means "the main branch of any remote". Multibranch resolves */<branch> per build.A wrong specifier gives a silent "no changes".
Lightweight checkoutMultibranch reads the Jenkinsfile straight from the Git API, before allocating a workspace, to decide if a branch is buildable.Fast indexing — and why a Jenkinsfile syntax error appears at scan time.
ChangelogJenkins computes the commit range since the last successful build into currentBuild.changeSets.This is how you notify the person who broke it.
groovyExtracting authors, messages and changed files
script {
    def changes = currentBuild.changeSets.collectMany { cs ->
        cs.items.collect { c ->
            [ author: c.author.fullName,
              email : c.author.getProperty(hudson.tasks.Mailer.UserProperty)?.address,
              msg   : c.msg.take(120),
              files : c.affectedFiles.collect { f -> f.path } ]
        }
    }
    echo "This build contains ${changes.size()} commit(s)"
    changes.each { c -> echo "- ${c.author}: ${c.msg}" }
    env.CULPRIT_EMAILS = changes.collect { it.email }.findAll { it }.unique().join(',')
}

4.11 Choosing an authentication method

SituationUseReason
CI clones one repo, read-onlySSH deploy key (read-only)Smallest blast radius — cannot push even if leaked
CI clones many reposSSH key on a machine account, or a PATOne credential to rotate
Multibranch / Branch SourcePAT with repo + admin:repo_hookBranch discovery uses the REST API, not git
GitHub App availableGitHub App credentialsShort-lived tokens, granular permissions — best at scale
Pipeline pushes tags backDeploy key with write access, or PATRead-only keys are rejected

4.12 Lab — break the key, then repair it

🧪Lab 4.1 · The three failure modes of Git over SSH A. Wrong permissions.
bashReproduce then fix
sudo -iu jenkins chmod 644 ~/.ssh/id_ed25519     # break it
sudo -iu jenkins ssh -T git@github.com           # "UNPROTECTED PRIVATE KEY FILE"
sudo -iu jenkins chmod 600 ~/.ssh/id_ed25519     # fix it
B. Unknown host.
bashReproduce then fix
sudo -iu jenkins mv ~/.ssh/known_hosts /tmp/kh   # break it
sudo -iu jenkins ssh -T git@github.com           # "Host key verification failed"
sudo -iu jenkins ssh-keyscan -t rsa,ed25519 github.com >> ~/.ssh/known_hosts
C. Credential ID mismatch. Rename the Jenkins credential to github-ssh-typo and re-run: you get Could not resolve credentialsId. This proves credentials are matched by exact string, not by fuzzy name.

4.13 Monorepo patterns

Our repo holds two apps. Three ways to avoid building both on every commit:

groovyClone only the folder you need
checkout scmGit(
    branches: [[name: '*/main']],
    extensions: [[$class: 'SparseCheckoutPaths',
                  sparseCheckoutPaths: [[path: 'nodejs-app/']]]],
    userRemoteConfigs: [[credentialsId: 'github-ssh', url: env.REPO_URL]]
)
groovySkip the build when your folder didn't change
stage('Change detection') {
    agent { label 'linux' }
    steps {
        script {
            checkout scm
            def files = sh(script: 'git diff --name-only HEAD~1 HEAD', returnStdout: true).trim()
            env.CHANGED = files.readLines().any { it.startsWith('nodejs-app/') } ? 'true' : 'false'
            echo "nodejs-app changed: ${env.CHANGED}"
        }
    }
}
stage('Test') {
    when { expression { env.CHANGED == 'true' } }
    steps { dir('nodejs-app') { sh 'npm ci && npm test' } }
}

Caveat: HEAD~1 misses multi-commit pushes. Prefer git diff --name-only ${env.GIT_PREVIOUS_SUCCESSFUL_COMMIT} HEAD when that variable is set.

groovySimplest: one Multibranch job per app
# Job: nodejs-pipeline  -> Script Path: nodejs-app/Jenkinsfile
# Job: python-pipeline  -> Script Path: python-app/Jenkinsfile
#
# Each job still clones the repo (cheap for us) but executes only its own
# Jenkinsfile, so only that app is built, tested, scanned and pushed.

4.14 More interview Q&A

Q5How does Jenkins know who broke the build?
It computes the changelog between the last successful build and the current one and exposes it as currentBuild.changeSets, from which I read author, message and affected paths — that drives targeted notifications.
Q6Submodules, LFS and sparse checkouts?
All three are checkout extensions: [$class:'SubmoduleOption', recursiveSubmodules:true], Git LFS via the LFS pull extension, and SparseCheckoutPaths for monorepos. I generate them rather than hand-write the class names.
Q7Why would a branch not appear in a Multibranch scan?
Branch discovery disabled, the name filtered out by the include/exclude regex, no Jenkinsfile at the configured path, or the PAT lacks repo scope. The Scan Repository Log states which one it hit.
Q8How do you avoid building every app on every commit in a monorepo?
Sparse-checkout the relevant path, or compute changed paths from the changelog and gate stages with when. At larger scale, per-app jobs with path filters, or an orchestrator that fans out only to affected components.
05
Block 5 · Foundation

BUILD stage — Node & Python

Goal: install dependencies deterministically and produce a build artifact. Understand why deterministic matters more than fast, and how caching changes the game.

5.1 What "build" means per ecosystem

StackBuild =Deterministic commandLockfile (must be committed)
Node.jsInstall dependencies into node_modulesnpm cipackage-lock.json
PythonCreate a venv, install dependenciespip install -r requirements.txtrequirements.txt (pinned!) / poetry.lock
🚨npm install vs npm ci — a guaranteed interview question npm install reads package.json and may update the lockfile and install slightly newer versions. npm ci ("clean install") deletes node_modules, installs exactly what package-lock.json says, and fails if the two disagree. CI must be reproducible — so CI always uses npm ci. Same reasoning for Python: pin your versions and use pip install -r.

5.2 Why your build is slow, and the cache fix

🟩 Node

Downloads land in ~/.npm. Point npm_config_cache at a stable directory and npm ci becomes seconds on a warm agent.

🐍 Python

Wheels land in ~/.cache/pip. Keep it between builds and pip install skips the network entirely.

groovyPersistent caches on the agent
environment {
    npm_config_cache = "${env.WORKSPACE_TMP}/.npm"     // or a fixed path: /var/lib/jenkins/.npm
    PIP_CACHE_DIR    = "/var/lib/jenkins/.cache/pip"
}
⚠️Cache in $WORKSPACE only survives if you don't wipe it If you use cleanBeforeCheckout() (Block 4) or cleanWs(), anything cached inside the workspace is deleted. Use a path outside the workspace — e.g. /var/lib/jenkins/.npm — for caches you want to keep.

5.3 Exit codes: how Jenkins decides success

A shell step succeeds when it exits 0. Anything else fails the stage and the build. Three escape hatches:

groovyControlling failure
// 1. Non-zero is fine -- give me the code instead of failing
def rc = sh(script: 'npm audit --audit-level=high', returnStatus: true)
echo "npm audit exit code: ${rc}"

// 2. Capture output (trailing newline needs .trim())
def nodeVersion = sh(script: 'node -v', returnStdout: true).trim()

// 3. Force a failure with a clear message
sh script: 'test -f package.json', label: 'verify manifest exists'
if (!fileExists('package-lock.json')) {
    error 'package-lock.json is missing -- `npm ci` cannot guarantee reproducibility'
}

5.4 Stash & unstash — moving files between agents

If stage A runs on agent linux and stage B on agent docker, they do not share a workspace. stash saves files on the controller; unstash restores them wherever you need them.

groovystash / unstash
stage('Build') {
    agent { label 'linux' }
    steps {
        sh 'npm ci --omit=dev'
        stash name: 'app-source', includes: 'src/**,package*.json', useDefaultExcludes: true
    }
}
stage('Package') {
    agent { label 'docker' }
    steps {
        unstash 'app-source'          // files reappear here, on a different machine
        sh 'docker build -t app .'
    }
}

5.5 ✍️ Jenkinsfiles v5 — real build stage

groovynodejs-app/Jenkinsfile — v5 (Build)
pipeline {
    agent any
    options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 20, unit: 'MINUTES') }

    environment {
        APP_NAME       = 'nodejs-app'
        npm_config_cache = '/var/lib/jenkins/.npm'      // cache OUTSIDE the workspace
    }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) {
                    checkout scmGit(branches: [[name: '*/main']],
                        extensions: [cleanBeforeCheckout()],
                        userRemoteConfigs: [[credentialsId: 'github-ssh',
                            url: 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git']])
                }
            }
        }

        stage('Build') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) {
                    sh label: 'verify toolchain', script: 'node -v && npm -v'
                    sh label: 'install dependencies', script: 'npm ci'
                    // sanity gate: the app must actually load before we test it
                    sh label: 'smoke import', script: "node -e \"require('./src/app.js'); console.log('app loads OK')\""
                }
            }
        }

        stage('Test') { steps { echo 'Block 6' } }
    }

    post { always { echo "Result: ${currentBuild.currentResult}" } }
}
groovypython-app/Jenkinsfile — v5 (Build)
pipeline {
    agent any
    options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 20, unit: 'MINUTES') }

    environment {
        APP_NAME     = 'python-app'
        PIP_CACHE_DIR = '/var/lib/jenkins/.cache/pip'
        VENV_DIR     = 'venv'                    // venv INSIDE the workspace (cheap to rebuild)
    }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) {
                    checkout scmGit(branches: [[name: '*/main']],
                        extensions: [cleanBeforeCheckout()],
                        userRemoteConfigs: [[credentialsId: 'github-ssh',
                            url: 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git']])
                }
            }
        }

        stage('Build') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) {
                    sh label: 'verify toolchain', script: 'python3 --version'
                    sh label: 'create virtualenv', script: 'python3 -m venv $VENV_DIR'
                    sh label: 'install dependencies', script: '''
                        . $VENV_DIR/bin/activate
                        pip install --upgrade pip
                        pip install -r requirements-dev.txt
                    '''
                    sh label: 'smoke import', script: '. $VENV_DIR/bin/activate && python -c "import app; print(\'app loads OK\')"'
                }
            }
        }

        stage('Test') { steps { echo 'Block 6' } }
    }

    post { always { echo "Result: ${currentBuild.currentResult}" } }
}
💡Why the venv lives inside the workspace for Python A venv is cheap (milliseconds) and disposable — recreating it is faster and safer than sharing a mutable one between builds. Node's node_modules is the same idea; the expensive part is the download cache, and that's what we keep outside the workspace.

5.6 ✅ Checklist

  • package-lock.json is committed (Node) and requirements*.txt is pinned (Python).
  • Both pipelines show a green Build stage that prints the toolchain version.
  • I ran the build twice and the second run was faster (cache working).
  • I can explain in one sentence why CI uses npm ci.

5.7 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1What does your Build stage do?
It installs dependencies deterministically from a committed lockfile (npm ci / pinned requirements.txt), verifies the toolchain, and does a smoke check that the app imports. It produces no side effects outside the workspace, so any failure is safe to retry.
Q2How do you speed up slow CI builds?
Keep the package download cache outside the workspace so it survives cleanWs (npm_config_cache, PIP_CACHE_DIR), use shallow clones, cache base Docker layers with --cache-from, and split fast stages first so failures are discovered early.
Q3How do you share files between stages on different agents?
stash on the producing agent and unstash on the consuming one — the controller holds the files. Alternatively publish to an artifact repository (Nexus, S3, ECR) for anything you need to keep.
Q4Your build passes locally but fails in Jenkins. Walk me through it.
First reproduce as the Jenkins user with sudo -u jenkins …. Then compare: tool versions (node -v), lockfile committed, network access to the registry, filesystem permissions, and disk space. Then check the console log for the first + command line that failed — not the last.

5.8 Under the hood — determinism and caches

A build is deterministic when the same commit always produces the same result. Three enemies of determinism, and the fix for each:

EnemySymptomFix
Floating version ranges"express": "^4.19.2" resolves differently next weekCommit the lockfile; use npm ci / pinned requirements.txt
Unpinned base imagesFROM node:20 changes under youPin: FROM node:20.17-alpine3.20
System packages installed at build timeapt-get install curl picks a new versionPin versions, or bake them into a base image you control
Time/locale dependent outputSnapshot tests fail around midnightSet TZ and inject the clock in tests
Reproducibility is the whole point of CI. If a build can pass today and fail tomorrow on unchanged code, you don't have CI — you have a slot machine.Block 5

Where caches live, and how long they survive

CachePathSurvives cleanWs()?
npmnpm_config_cache=/var/lib/jenkins/.npm✅ outside the workspace
pipPIP_CACHE_DIR=/var/lib/jenkins/.cache/pip✅ outside the workspace
Docker layers/var/lib/docker✅ (until docker system prune)
Maven / Gradle~/.m2, ~/.gradle✅
node_modules / venvinside the workspace❌ deleted — that's fine, they're cheap

5.9 Lab — measure what caching actually buys you

🧪Lab 5.1 · Cold vs warm build
bashTime it yourself, as the jenkins user
sudo -iu jenkins bash -c '
  cd /tmp && rm -rf cachetest && mkdir cachetest && cd cachetest
  cp /var/lib/jenkins/jobs/nodejs-app/workspace/nodejs-app/package*.json . 2>/dev/null || true

  echo "--- COLD (empty cache) ---"
  rm -rf /tmp/coldcache && npm_config_cache=/tmp/coldcache npm ci --ignore-scripts
  echo "--- WARM (same cache again) ---"
  rm -rf node_modules && time npm_config_cache=/tmp/coldcache npm ci --ignore-scripts
'
Expect the warm run to be 3–10× faster. That ratio is exactly what your pipeline gains by pointing npm_config_cache at a path outside the workspace.

5.10 Multi-ecosystem build reference

TaskNode.jsPython
Install depsnpm cipython -m venv venv && . venv/bin/activate && pip install -r requirements-dev.txt
Prod-only installnpm ci --omit=devpip install -r requirements.txt
Show the treenpm ls --allpip list / pipdeptree
Smoke checknode -e "require('./src/app.js')"python -c "import app"
Produce an artifactnpm pack → *.tgzpip wheel . -w dist → *.whl
Cache dir~/.npm~/.cache/pip
Lockfilepackage-lock.jsonrequirements.txt (pinned) / poetry.lock
groovyPublishing a build artifact (npm pack / wheel)
stage('Package artifact') {
    steps {
        dir('nodejs-app') {
            sh 'npm pack'                                     // -> nodejs-app-1.0.0.tgz
            archiveArtifacts artifacts: '*.tgz', fingerprint: true
        }
    }
}

5.11 More interview Q&A

Q5What makes a CI build reproducible?
A committed lockfile, a deterministic install command (npm ci), pinned base images and pinned system packages, plus an isolated environment (container or venv) so the agent's global state can't leak in.
Q6Where should CI caches live?
Outside the workspace, at a stable absolute path like /var/lib/jenkins/.npm. Anything inside the workspace is destroyed by cleanWs() and cleanBeforeCheckout(), so it can't act as a cache.
Q7When would you use stash instead of just leaving files in the workspace?
When the next stage may run on a different agent — workspaces aren't shared across nodes, so stash copies the files via the controller. On a single agent, leaving them in place is faster.
Q8How do you fail fast in a build stage?
Put a cheap smoke check right after install (does the module import?), and rely on set -e semantics: any non-zero exit aborts the stage. I add label: to each sh so the console shows exactly which command failed.
06
Block 6 · Foundation

TEST stage + reports & quality gates

Goal: run the test suites, publish machine-readable results Jenkins can graph, and make coverage a real gate — not a number nobody reads.

6.1 Why reports matter more than logs

Green text in a console log is worthless to a team: you can't trend it, filter it, or see which test broke. JUnit XML turns test output into data Jenkins stores per build — a trend graph, a per-test history, and a "this test has been flaky for 3 days" signal.

6.2 Producing JUnit XML

jsonnodejs-app/package.json — add jest-junit + coverage reporters
"scripts": {
  "test":       "jest --ci --coverage",
  "test:junit": "jest --ci --coverage --reporters=default --reporters=jest-junit",
  "lint":       "eslint ."
},
"devDependencies": {
  "eslint": "^8.57.0",
  "jest": "^29.7.0",
  "jest-junit": "^16.0.0",
  "supertest": "^6.3.4"
},
"jest-junit": {
  "outputDirectory": "reports",
  "outputName": "junit.xml"
}
bashWhat it produces
reports/junit.xml          # JUnit XML  -> junit step
coverage/lcov.info         # LCOV       -> Coverage / SonarQube
coverage/cobertura-coverage.xml
inipython-app/pytest.ini — built-in JUnit + coverage, no plugin needed
[pytest]
testpaths = tests
addopts =
    --junitxml=reports/junit.xml
    --cov=app
    --cov-report=xml:coverage.xml
    --cov-report=term-missing
    --cov-report=html:htmlcov
bashWhat it produces
reports/junit.xml          # JUnit XML
coverage.xml               # Cobertura XML -> Coverage plugin + SonarQube
htmlcov/index.html         # browsable HTML report

6.3 Publishing the reports

groovyThe three publish steps
post {
    always {
        // 1. Test results -> per-test history, trend graph, flaky detection
        junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml',
              allowEmptyResults: true,
              skipPublishingChecks: false

        // 2. Coverage (modern "Coverage" plugin, replaces the old Cobertura plugin)
        recordCoverage tools: [[parser: 'COBERTURA', pattern: 'coverage/cobertura-coverage.xml']],
                       id: 'coverage', name: 'Code Coverage',
                       sourceCodeRetention: 'EVERY_BUILD'

        // 3. Keep the HTML report + artifacts for humans
        archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'reports/**,htmlcov/**,coverage/**',
                         allowEmptyArchive: true, fingerprint: true
    }
}
📦Plugin Install Coverage (coverage) — the modern successor to the Cobertura plugin. For Python point the parser at coverage.xml with parser: 'COBERTURA'; it's the same XML schema pytest-cov writes.

6.4 FAILURE vs UNSTABLE — the distinction interviewers probe

ResultMeansTypical causeBall colour
SUCCESSEverything passed—🔵 blue/green
UNSTABLEBuilt fine, but quality is below standardTests failed, coverage under threshold, quality gate red🟡 yellow
FAILURESomething threw an errorCompile error, missing file, non-zero exit code🔴 red
ABORTEDA human or timeout stopped itManual cancel, timeout, approval declined⚪ grey
groovyMarking a build unstable instead of failing it
// catchError: the stage turns red, the build becomes UNSTABLE and CONTINUES
stage('Tests') {
    steps {
        catchError(buildResult: 'UNSTABLE', stageResult: 'FAILURE') {
            sh 'npm test'
        }
    }
}

// Or keep the build green while flagging the stage (use sparingly!)
catchError(buildResult: 'SUCCESS', stageResult: 'UNSTABLE') { sh 'npm run lint' }

// Set it manually from Groovy
script {
    if (env.COVERAGE_PCT && env.COVERAGE_PCT.toInteger() < 70) {
        currentBuild.result = 'UNSTABLE'
        echo "Coverage ${env.COVERAGE_PCT}% is below the 70% gate"
    }
}

6.5 A real coverage gate

Don't just report coverage — enforce it, so the number can't rot. Both ecosystems can do it natively:

jsonnodejs-app/package.json → jest.coverageThreshold
"jest": {
  "testEnvironment": "node",
  "collectCoverageFrom": ["src/**/*.js"],
  "coverageReporters": ["text-summary", "lcov", "cobertura"],
  "coverageThreshold": {
    "global": { "lines": 70, "statements": 70, "branches": 50, "functions": 60 }
  }
}

Jest exits non-zero when the threshold is missed — one line, no extra plugin.

bashpytest --cov-fail-under
. venv/bin/activate
pytest --cov=app --cov-report=xml:coverage.xml \
       --cov-report=term-missing --cov-fail-under=70

Or move it into pytest.ini so nobody can forget it: addopts = … --cov-fail-under=70.

6.6 ✍️ Jenkinsfiles v6 — tests + reports

groovynodejs-app/Jenkinsfile — v6 (Test)
pipeline {
    agent any
    options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 20, unit: 'MINUTES') }

    environment { APP_NAME = 'nodejs-app'; npm_config_cache = '/var/lib/jenkins/.npm' }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            steps { dir(APP_NAME) { checkout scmGit(branches: [[name: '*/main']],
                        extensions: [cleanBeforeCheckout()],
                        userRemoteConfigs: [[credentialsId: 'github-ssh',
                            url: 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git']]) } }
        }
        stage('Build') {
            steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh 'npm ci' } }
        }
        stage('Test') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) {
                    // coverageThreshold in package.json enforces the quality gate
                    sh label: 'unit tests + coverage', script: 'npm run test:junit'
                }
            }
        }
    }

    post {
        always {
            dir(APP_NAME) {
                junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml', allowEmptyResults: true
                recordCoverage tools: [[parser: 'COBERTURA', pattern: 'coverage/cobertura-coverage.xml']],
                               sourceCodeRetention: 'LAST_BUILD'
                archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'reports/**,coverage/**', allowEmptyArchive: true
            }
            echo "Result: ${currentBuild.currentResult}"
        }
    }
}
groovypython-app/Jenkinsfile — v6 (Test)
pipeline {
    agent any
    options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 20, unit: 'MINUTES') }

    environment { APP_NAME = 'python-app'; PIP_CACHE_DIR = '/var/lib/jenkins/.cache/pip' }

                        extensions: [cleanBeforeCheckout()],

                            url: 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git']]) } }

                        python3 -m venv venv
                        . venv/bin/activate
                        pip install --upgrade pip
                        pip install -r requirements-dev.txt
                    ''' } }
        }
        stage('Test') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) {

                       script: '. venv/bin/activate && pytest --cov-fail-under=70'

}
📊What you should now see in the UI On the job page: a Test Result trend graph, a Coverage trend, per-test history ("this test failed 3 times last week"), and a link to the archived HTML report. That's the difference between "we run tests" and "we manage quality".

6.7 ✅ Checklist

  • Both jobs show Test Result and Coverage trends on the job page.
  • I broke a test on purpose and watched the build turn red with the failing test named.
  • I dropped coverage below the threshold and saw the build fail at the Test stage.
  • I can explain SUCCESS vs UNSTABLE vs FAILURE with an example of each.

6.8 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1How does Jenkins know a test failed?
Two ways. The shell step's non-zero exit code fails the build immediately, or the junit step parses the XML and, if any test case is a failure/error, marks the build UNSTABLE. Publishing XML gives far better feedback because Jenkins then knows which test and keeps per-test history.
Q2What is the difference between UNSTABLE and FAILURE?
FAILURE means the pipeline threw an error — compile error, missing file, non-zero exit. UNSTABLE means it completed but quality criteria weren't met: failing tests, coverage under threshold, or a SonarQube quality gate in red. UNSTABLE is "built, but don't ship it".
Q3How do you enforce a coverage gate?
Make the test command exit non-zero below the threshold — Jest's coverageThreshold or pytest's --cov-fail-under — and publish the XML so the trend is visible. Later (Block 16) SonarQube enforces the same idea across duplication, smells and vulnerabilities.
Q4How do you handle flaky tests in CI?
Publish JUnit XML so Jenkins tracks flakiness per test, quarantine the flaky test out of the blocking gate, and fix or delete it with a deadline. My rule: a flaky test is a bug in the test or the product — never something you just retry away.

6.9 🔥 Common errors

ErrorCauseFix
No test report files were foundWrong path — you published from the workspace root while tests wrote to nodejs-app/reports/Wrap the junit step in dir(APP_NAME) { }, or use **/reports/junit.xml
Tests pass locally, fail in JenkinsDev dependency missing, timezone, or a port already in useRun npm ci/pip install -r in CI exactly as locally; never bind fixed ports in tests
recordCoverage unknown stepCoverage plugin not installedInstall the Coverage plugin (or use the older cobertura step)
Coverage is 0%collectCoverageFrom/--cov points at the wrong pathPoint coverage at the code actually imported by the tests

6.10 Under the hood — what JUnit XML contains

The report is just XML. Understanding its shape lets you debug "why is my test result empty" in seconds.

xmlreports/junit.xml — the schema Jenkins parses
<testsuites name="jest tests">
  <testsuite name="items API" tests="3" failures="1" errors="0" skipped="0" time="0.412">
    <testcase name="GET /api/items lists seeded items" classname="items API" time="0.031"/>
    <testcase name="POST /api/items creates an item (201)" classname="items API" time="0.019">
      <failure message="expected 201, received 400">
        at Object.<anonymous> (tests/app.test.js:31:26)
      </failure>
    </testcase>
  </testsuite>
</testsuites>
  • Jenkins builds its per-test history from the name + classname pair. Rename a test and you break its history — which is why flaky-test detection needs stable test names.
  • Any <failure> or <error> child marks the build UNSTABLE (not FAILED), because the pipeline itself completed.
  • allowEmptyResults: true stops the step failing when the file is missing — essential when tests didn't run at all.

6.11 Lab — break the pipeline three ways

🧪Lab 6.1 · Failure, unstable, and silent success 1. A failing test. Change an assertion in tests/app.test.js, commit, run. The stage goes red, the build is UNSTABLE (because junit reported failures) — unless your sh also exits non-zero, in which case it's FAILURE. Understand which one you got and why.
groovyMaking the distinction explicit
// A: command fails -> stage red, build FAILED, later stages skipped
sh 'npm test'

// B: command fails -> stage red, build UNSTABLE, pipeline continues
catchError(buildResult: 'UNSTABLE', stageResult: 'FAILURE') { sh 'npm test' }

// C: report published, result decided by the JUnit content only
sh(script: 'npm test', returnStatus: true)     // swallow the exit code
junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml'         // UNSTABLE if any case failed
2. Coverage below the gate. Set --cov-fail-under=95 and watch the Test stage fail with a clear message.
3. Missing report. Point junit at the wrong path and see No test report files were found — then fix it with dir().

6.12 Choosing a coverage tool and parser

SourceReport fileParser for recordCoverage
Jestcoverage/cobertura-coverage.xmlCOBERTURA
Jest (alternative)coverage/lcov.infoJACOCO (LCOV) — used by Sonar
pytest-covcoverage.xmlCOBERTURA
Gocoverage.outGO_COV
Java / Maventarget/site/jacoco/jacoco.xmlJACOCO
groovyCoverage with an explicit quality gate
recordCoverage(
    tools: [[parser: 'COBERTURA', pattern: '**/coverage.xml']],
    id: 'cov', name: 'Backend coverage',
    sourceCodeRetention: 'EVERY_BUILD',
    qualityGates: [
        [threshold: 70.0, metric: 'LINE',     baseline: 'PROJECT', unstable: true],
        [threshold: 50.0, metric: 'BRANCH',   baseline: 'PROJECT', unstable: true]
    ]
)

With qualityGates the coverage plugin itself can mark the build unstable — useful when you want the gate enforced centrally rather than inside the test command.

6.13 Flaky tests — policy, not hope

PracticeDetail
Never retry a test awayA retry hides the bug and destroys signal. Fix or quarantine.
Quarantine with a deadlineMove the flaky test to a non-blocking suite with a ticket and an expiry date.
Common causes to checkShared mutable state, real time/sleep, fixed ports, network calls, test order dependence, unseeded randomness.
Parallel safetyUse EXECUTOR_NUMBER for any port or temp directory, so parallel builds don't collide.
Track itJUnit history shows "failed 4 of last 20" — that's your evidence for prioritising the fix.

6.14 More interview Q&A

Q5Why is my build UNSTABLE instead of FAILED?
Because something reported a quality problem rather than an error — most often the junit step finding failing test cases, or a coverage/quality gate. The pipeline ran to completion; it just didn't meet the standard. I use that distinction to alert differently.
Q6How do you handle flaky tests?
Publish JUnit XML so Jenkins tracks per-test history, then quarantine the flaky test out of the blocking suite with a ticket and a deadline, and fix the root cause — usually shared state, real time, a fixed port or network dependency. I never add a blanket retry.
Q7How do you make tests safe to run in parallel on one agent?
Never bind fixed ports or use fixed temp paths. I derive them from env.EXECUTOR_NUMBER or env.BUILD_TAG, and keep all state inside the build's own workspace or a container.
Q8Coverage dropped from 85% to 40% overnight with no test changes. Why?
Almost always the collectCoverageFrom / --cov path changed, or new files were added outside the instrumented directory. Coverage measures what you told it to measure, so a renamed source folder silently resets the denominator.
07
Block 7 · Real engineering

Environment variables & build parameters

Goal: control your pipeline's behaviour without editing code — the difference between a script that builds one thing and a pipeline a whole team can use.

7.1 Three levels of environment variables

LevelWhere it's setScopeUse for
1. GlobalManage Jenkins → System → Global properties → Environment variablesEvery job on the controllerCompany-wide things: REGISTRY, SLACK_CHANNEL, proxy settings
2. Pipelineenvironment { } in the JenkinsfileEvery step in that pipeline (or one stage)APP_NAME, IMAGE, tool flags
3. StepwithEnv([...]) { } or inline sh 'VAR=x cmd'Only inside that blockTemporary overrides, per-command config
groovyAll three in action
pipeline {
    agent any

    environment {
        APP_NAME   = 'nodejs-app'
        IMAGE      = "docker.io/<youruser>/${APP_NAME}"   // variables can reference each other
        BUILD_TS   = """${sh(returnStdout: true, script: 'date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S').trim()}"""
    }

    stages {
        stage('Demo') {
            environment {
                STAGE_ONLY = 'yes'                          // visible only in this stage
            }
            steps {
                echo "Global? ${env.REGISTRY}"              // set in Manage Jenkins
                echo "Pipeline: ${env.IMAGE} built at ${env.BUILD_TS}"
                echo "Stage: ${env.STAGE_ONLY}"

                withEnv(['NODE_ENV=ci', 'CI=true']) {        // block-scoped
                    sh 'echo $NODE_ENV $CI'
                }

                sh 'INLINE=yes; echo $INLINE'               // per-command
            }
        }
    }
}

7.2 Built-in variables you must know

VariableValueTypical use
env.BUILD_NUMBER42Image tags, artifact versions
env.BUILD_ID42 or timestampLegacy; prefer BUILD_NUMBER
env.BUILD_URLFull URL of this buildSlack/email notifications
env.JOB_NAME / JOB_URLJob identityNotifications, dashboards
env.WORKSPACEAbsolute path of the workspaceAbsolute paths in scripts
env.NODE_NAME / NODE_LABELSAgent name / labelsDebug "which machine ran this?"
env.EXECUTOR_NUMBER0, 1, …Unique ports/volumes per concurrent build
env.BUILD_TAGjenkins-${JOB_NAME}-${BUILD_NUMBER}Unique container names
env.BRANCH_NAMEBranch (Multibranch only)Branch-specific behaviour (Block 15)
env.CHANGE_IDPR number (Multibranch PR builds)PR comments & checks
env.STAGE_NAMECurrent stageDynamic log messages
🔍See every variable on your own controller Open http://<jenkins>:8080/pipeline-syntax/globals — it lists all built-ins with the real values your controller has right now. Or add a temporary stage: sh 'env | sort'.

7.3 Parameters — inputs before the build starts

DirectiveUI widgetRead as
string(name:'X', defaultValue:'…')Text boxparams.X (String)
text(name:'X', defaultValue:'…')Text areaString (multi-line)
booleanParam(name:'X', defaultValue:true)CheckboxBoolean
choice(name:'X', choices:['dev','qa','prod'])Drop-downString
password(name:'X', defaultValue:'…')Masked boxString — avoid, use credentials (Block 8)
file(name:'X')UploadFile in the workspace
groovyParameters + conditional behaviour
pipeline {
    agent any

    parameters {
        choice   name: 'ENV',        choices: ['dev', 'qa', 'prod'], description: 'Deploy target'
        booleanParam name: 'RUN_TESTS',   defaultValue: true,  description: 'Run the unit test suite'
        booleanParam name: 'PUSH_IMAGE',  defaultValue: false, description: 'Push to Docker Hub'
        string   name: 'IMAGE_TAG',  defaultValue: '', description: 'Override tag (default: build number)'
    }

    environment {
        // params is null on the very first build -> use the ?: fallback pattern
        TAG = "${params.IMAGE_TAG ?: env.BUILD_NUMBER}"
    }

    stages {
        stage('Test') {
            when { expression { params.RUN_TESTS } }
            steps { echo 'running tests' }
        }
        stage('Push') {
            when {
                allOf {
                    expression { params.PUSH_IMAGE }
                    anyOf { branch 'main'; expression { params.ENV == 'prod' } }
                }
            }
            steps { echo "pushing tag ${env.TAG} to ${params.ENV}" }
        }
    }
}
⚠️The first-build gotcha Parameters don't exist until the job has been built once — params.RUN_TESTS is null on build #1, so when { expression { params.RUN_TESTS } } is skipped. Always provide defaults and write params.X ?: 'fallback'. After the first build the "Build Now" button becomes "Build with Parameters".

7.4 Credentials inside environment{} (preview of Block 8)

groovyThe credentials() helper
environment {
    // Username/password  ->  *_USR and *_PSW
    DOCKER = credentials('dockerhub-creds')        // DOCKER_USR, DOCKER_PSW

    // Secret text        ->  variable holds the secret itself
    SONAR_TOKEN = credentials('sonar-token')

    // Secret file        ->  *_FILE (path to the temp file)
    KUBECONFIG = credentials('kubeconfig-file')    // KUBECONFIG_FILE
}

7.5 ✍️ Jenkinsfiles v7 — parameterized

groovynodejs-app/Jenkinsfile — v7
pipeline {
    agent any

    parameters {
        booleanParam name: 'RUN_TESTS', defaultValue: true,  description: 'Run Jest suite'
        choice       name: 'LOG_LEVEL', choices: ['info','debug'], description: 'Shell verbosity'
    }

    options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 20, unit: 'MINUTES') }

    environment {
        APP_NAME        = 'nodejs-app'
        npm_config_cache = '/var/lib/jenkins/.npm'
        TAG             = "${env.BUILD_NUMBER}-${env.GIT_COMMIT_SHORT ?: 'nogit'}"
    }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) { checkout scmGit(branches: [[name: '*/main']],
                        extensions: [cleanBeforeCheckout()],
                        userRemoteConfigs: [[credentialsId: 'github-ssh',
                            url: 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git']]) }
                script {
                    dir(APP_NAME) {
                        env.GIT_COMMIT_SHORT = sh(script:'git rev-parse --short HEAD', returnStdout:true).trim()
                    }
                    echo "Build ${env.BUILD_NUMBER} · commit ${env.GIT_COMMIT_SHORT} · ${params.LOG_LEVEL}"
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Build') { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh 'npm ci' } } }
        stage('Test')  {
            when { expression { params.RUN_TESTS } }
            steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh 'npm run test:junit' } }
        }
    }

    post {
        always {
            dir(APP_NAME) { junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml', allowEmptyResults: true }
            echo "Result: ${currentBuild.currentResult} · ${env.BUILD_URL}"
        }
    }
}

                        python3 -m venv $VENV
