7.6 ✅ Checklist

  • Added a global variable in Manage Jenkins and read it from a pipeline.
  • Both jobs now show "Build with Parameters" and honour the checkbox.
  • Printed the full environment with sh 'env | sort' once and recognised 10 built-ins.
  • I can explain the difference between env.X and params.X.

7.7 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1What's the difference between environment variables and parameters?
Environment variables are values the pipeline sets or inherits and that steps read (env.BUILD_NUMBER, REGISTRY). Parameters are inputs supplied by the user (or API trigger) before the build starts, read via params.X, and declared in a parameters block. Parameters become environment variables too, but only they show up in the "Build with Parameters" UI.
Q2How do you pass a value from one stage to the next?
Assign to env inside a script block: env.VERSION = sh(script:'…', returnStdout:true).trim(). Later stages read env.VERSION. Across agents I use stash/unstash or write a file, because the workspace isn't shared.
Q3Why would a parameterised stage be skipped on the first build?
Because params is empty until the job has run once and Jenkins has materialised the parameter definitions. I guard with defaults: params.X ?: 'fallback'.

7.8 Under the hood — the three scopes in practice

Variables resolve in a predictable order, and knowing it saves you from "my variable is empty":

textPrecedence (later wins)
1. Jenkins built-ins          (BUILD_NUMBER, JOB_NAME, WORKSPACE …)
2. Global properties          (Manage Jenkins -> System -> Global properties)
3. environment { }  at pipeline level
4. environment { }  at stage level      <- overrides pipeline-level
5. withEnv([...]) { }                   <- overrides everything for that block
6. inline shell:  VAR=x command         <- only that command
groovyPrecedence demo
pipeline {
    agent any
    environment { LOG = 'pipeline-level' }
    stages {
        stage('A') {
            environment { LOG = 'stage-level' }
            steps {
                echo "1: ${env.LOG}"                    // stage-level
                withEnv(['LOG=withEnv']) { echo "2: ${env.LOG}" }   // withEnv
                sh 'echo "3: $LOG"'                     // stage-level again
                sh 'LOG=inline; echo "4: $LOG"'         // inline
            }
        }
        stage('B') {
            steps { echo "5: ${env.LOG}" }              // pipeline-level
        }
    }
}
⚠️env.X vs X Inside sh, environment variables are shell variables ($LOG). In Groovy they're env.LOG or LOG — but plain LOG only works if Jenkins created it as a binding. Use env.LOG consistently; it is never ambiguous.

7.9 Parameter types in depth

groovyEvery parameter directive, with defaults and safe reads
parameters {
    string(name: 'VERSION',   defaultValue: '1.0.0',  description: 'Semver to release')
    text  (name: 'RELEASE_NOTES', defaultValue: '',   description: 'Markdown notes')
    booleanParam(name: 'RUN_E2E',  defaultValue: false, description: 'Run the e2e suite')
    choice(name: 'ENV', choices: ['dev','qa','prod'], description: 'Target environment')
    password(name: 'API_KEY', defaultValue: '', description: 'Avoid — use credentials')
    file  (name: 'CONFIG',    description: 'Upload a config file')
}

environment {
    // Safe read pattern: params is empty on the very first build
    TARGET  = "${params.ENV ?: 'dev'}"
    E2E     = "${params.RUN_E2E != null ? params.RUN_E2E : false}"
}
TypeGroovy type of params.XGotcha
string / textStringEmpty string, not null, when untouched
booleanParamBooleannull on the first build → use != false
choiceStringNewlines in choices are trimmed; keep it a list
passwordStringStored in build history — use credentials instead
fileString (filename in workspace)Uploaded per-build; not persisted

7.10 Lab — parameter-driven pipeline

🧪Lab 7.1 · Build with Parameters Add this to a scratch pipeline, run it once (creating the parameters), then run it again with different values and compare the log.
groovyObserve params vs env
pipeline {
    agent any
    parameters {
        choice name: 'ENV', choices: ['dev','qa','prod']
        booleanParam name: 'VERBOSE', defaultValue: false
    }
    stages {
        stage('Inspect') {
            steps {
                echo "params  = ${params}"
                echo "ENV     = ${params.ENV}   (type: ${params.ENV.getClass().simpleName})"
                echo "VERBOSE = ${params.VERBOSE}"
                echo "as env  = ${env.ENV}"        // parameters are exported to env too
                sh 'printenv | grep -E "^(ENV|VERBOSE)=" || true'
            }
        }
        stage('Verbose only') {
            when { expression { params.VERBOSE } }
            steps { echo 'running extra diagnostics' }
        }
    }
}
Now trigger it from the API and confirm the parameters arrive:
bashTrigger with parameters over HTTP
curl -X POST -u user:$TOKEN -H "$CRUMB" \
  "$JENKINS_URL/job/scratch/buildWithParameters?ENV=qa&VERBOSE=true"

7.11 Production notes

PracticeReason
Never put a secret in parametersValues are stored in plain text in build history and are visible to anyone with read access
Provide a default for every parameterAPI and upstream triggers may omit them
Use choice over free text for environmentsPrevents prod vs production typos reaching a deploy step
Log the effective configuration at the startecho "ENV=${params.ENV}" makes post-mortems trivial
Prefer the Active Choices plugin for dynamic listsFill choices from an API (e.g. list of available versions) instead of hard-coding

7.12 More interview Q&A

Q4Are parameters available as environment variables?
Yes — Jenkins exports parameters into the build environment, so params.ENV and env.ENV (and $ENV in shell) refer to the same value. The difference is that params is empty on the very first build before the definitions are materialised.
Q5How do you trigger a parameterised build from another pipeline?
build job: 'deploy', parameters: [string(name:'IMAGE_TAG', value: env.GIT_SHA)], wait: true, propagate: false — then inspect result.result and decide whether to fail, mark unstable or continue.
Q6How do you pass a computed value from stage 1 to stage 3?
Assign it to env inside a script block (env.VERSION = …). Later stages read env.VERSION. If the stages run on different agents I also stash the files, since the workspace isn't shared.
Q7How do you keep secrets out of parameters?
I never use password parameters for real secrets — they're persisted in the build record. Secrets live in the credentials store and are bound with withCredentials at the moment of use.
08
Block 8 · Real engineering

Credentials & secrets management

Goal: never write a secret in a Jenkinsfile again — and be able to explain to an interviewer exactly how Jenkins stores, injects and masks them.

8.1 How Jenkins stores credentials

Credentials live in the credentials store at $JENKINS_HOME/secrets/, encrypted with a master key (secrets/master.key + secrets/hudson.util.Secret). Pipelines never see the raw value in source — they reference an ID and Jenkins injects the value at runtime.

⚠️The disaster rule If you lose master.key, every stored credential becomes permanently unreadable. Back it up separately from the rest of JENKINS_HOME — ideally outside the server. (Block 22.)

8.2 Credential kinds — which to pick

KindHoldsTypical IDUsed for
Username with passworduser + password/tokendockerhub-credsDocker Hub, Artifactory, Git over HTTPS
Secret textOne stringsonar-tokenAPI tokens, Slack token, Sonar token
Secret fileA whole filekubeconfigkubeconfig, .npmrc, service-account JSON
SSH Username with private keyuser + private keygithub-ssh, deploy-keyGit over SSH, deploying over SSH
CertificateKeystore / cert + passwordsigning-certCode signing, mTLS

Scopes: System = only Jenkins itself can use it (safest, use for secrets). Global = also available to jobs. Store at the root for shared secrets, or inside a folder/domain so only that team's jobs can reach it (works beautifully with RBAC in Block 12).

8.3 withCredentials — the injection block

groovyAll the binding forms you'll need
// 1. Username + password  ->  two env vars
withCredentials([usernamePassword(credentialsId: 'dockerhub-creds',
                                  usernameVariable: 'REG_USER',
                                  passwordVariable: 'REG_PASS')]) {
    sh 'docker login -u "$REG_USER" -p "$REG_PASS"'
}

// 2. Secret text  ->  one env var
withCredentials([string(credentialsId: 'sonar-token', variable: 'SONAR_TOKEN')]) {
    sh 'sonar-scanner -Dsonar.login=$SONAR_TOKEN'
}

// 3. Secret file  ->  env var holding a PATH to a temp copy (deleted afterwards)
withCredentials([file(credentialsId: 'kubeconfig', variable: 'KUBECONFIG')]) {
    sh 'kubectl --kubeconfig=$KUBECONFIG get pods'
}

// 4. SSH key  ->  sshagent wrapper (safer than writing the key to disk)
sshagent(['deploy-key']) {
    sh 'ssh -o StrictHostKeyChecking=no ubuntu@host "docker pull app && docker compose up -d"'
}

// 5. Several at once
withCredentials([
    usernamePassword(credentialsId: 'dockerhub-creds', usernameVariable: 'U', passwordVariable: 'P'),
    string(credentialsId: 'slack-token', variable: 'SLACK')
]) {
    sh 'echo "using $U and $SLACK"'
}

8.4 Masking — what Jenkins protects, and what it doesn't

Jenkins replaces any occurrence of a bound secret's value in the console log with ****. But masking is string matching, not magic.

groovyMasking behaviour — and the holes
withCredentials([string(credentialsId: 'my-token', variable: 'TOK')]) {
    sh 'echo $TOK'            // ✅ printed as ****
    sh 'echo "$TOK" | base64' // ❌ LEAKS -- base64 no longer matches the raw string
    sh 'echo ${TOK:0:4}'      // ❌ LEAKS a substring
    sh 'echo $TOK > out.txt'  // ⚠️  secret now sits in the workspace -> archive it and it's public
    sh 'set +x; echo done'    // ✅ disable shell tracing around sensitive commands
}
🚨Secrets hygiene rules I follow
  • One credential per purpose per environment — never one shared secret.
  • Name IDs predictably: <system>-<env>-<kind> e.g. dockerhub-prod-push.
  • Never echo a secret, even "just to debug" — and never write one to the workspace.
  • Rotate on a schedule and immediately after anyone with access leaves.
  • Prefer short-lived tokens (OIDC, IAM roles) over long-lived passwords where possible.

8.5 Where secrets must NOT live

Anti-patternWhy it's wrongDo this instead
Hard-coded in JenkinsfileCommitted to Git; visible in every fork and cloneCredential ID + withCredentials
In environment {} as plain textSame problem, plus it's in the configcredentials('id') helper
Global env var in Manage JenkinsReadable by anyone who can configure jobsCredentials store, folder-scoped
In a Docker image layerExtractable by anyone who can pull the imageBuild args only at runtime, or mount at run time
In a build parameterStored in plain text in build historyCredential, or a password-masked parameter at worst

8.6 ✍️ Jenkinsfiles v8 — secrets wired in

groovynodejs-app/Jenkinsfile — v8 (secrets)
pipeline {
    agent any
    options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 20, unit: 'MINUTES') }

    environment {
        APP_NAME = 'nodejs-app'
        // NOTE: no secrets here -- only IDs and non-sensitive config
        REGISTRY_CREDENTIALS_ID = 'dockerhub-creds'
    }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { checkout scmGit(
                branches: [[name: '*/main']], extensions: [cleanBeforeCheckout()],
                userRemoteConfigs: [[credentialsId: 'github-ssh',
                    url: 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git']]) } } }

        stage('Build') { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh 'npm ci' } } }
        stage('Test')  { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh 'npm run test:junit' } } }

        stage('Verify registry access') {          // proving the credential works
            steps {
                withCredentials([usernamePassword(
                        credentialsId: env.REGISTRY_CREDENTIALS_ID,
                        usernameVariable: 'REG_USER',
                        passwordVariable: 'REG_PASS')]) {
                    sh '''
                        set +x                          # never trace the login command
                        echo "$REG_PASS" | docker login -u "$REG_USER" --password-stdin
                        docker logout
                    '''
                    echo "Logged in as $REG_USER"        // username is not secret
                }
            }
        }
    }

    post {
        always { dir(APP_NAME) { junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml', allowEmptyResults: true } }
        cleanup { sh 'docker logout || true' }
    }
}
groovypython-app/Jenkinsfile — v8 (secrets)
pipeline {
    agent any
    options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 20, unit: 'MINUTES') }

    environment {
        APP_NAME = 'python-app'
        REGISTRY_CREDENTIALS_ID = 'dockerhub-creds'
    }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { checkout scmGit(
                branches: [[name: '*/main']], extensions: [cleanBeforeCheckout()],
                userRemoteConfigs: [[credentialsId: 'github-ssh',
                    url: 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git']]) } } }

        stage('Build') { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh '''
                    python3 -m venv venv
                    . venv/bin/activate
                    pip install -r requirements-dev.txt
                ''' } } }

        stage('Test')  { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh '. venv/bin/activate && pytest --cov-fail-under=70' } } }

        stage('Verify registry access') {
            steps {
                withCredentials([usernamePassword(
                        credentialsId: env.REGISTRY_CREDENTIALS_ID,
                        usernameVariable: 'REG_USER',
                        passwordVariable: 'REG_PASS')]) {
                    sh '''
                        set +x
                        echo "$REG_PASS" | docker login -u "$REG_USER" --password-stdin
                        docker logout
                    '''
                    echo "Logged in as $REG_USER"
                }
            }
        }
    }

    post {
        always { dir(APP_NAME) { junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml', allowEmptyResults: true } }
        cleanup { sh 'docker logout || true' }
    }
}
🔑Create the credential first Manage Jenkins → Credentials → System → Global credentials → Add Credentials → Username with password → Username: your Docker Hub user → Password: a Docker Hub Access Token (Account Settings → Security → New Access Token, not your account password) → ID: dockerhub-creds.

8.7 ✅ Checklist

  • dockerhub-creds exists and the "Verify registry access" stage is green.
  • I confirmed the password never appears in the console log.
  • I know where master.key lives and why it matters.
  • No secret exists anywhere in either Jenkinsfile.

8.8 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1How do you manage secrets in Jenkins?
In the credentials store, encrypted at rest with the master key, referenced by ID. I bind them at runtime with withCredentials (or the credentials() helper in environment), so the value only exists in the process environment for the duration of the block and is masked in the console. Nothing sensitive goes into the Jenkinsfile or into job parameters.
Q2How does Jenkins mask secrets, and what are the limits?
It pattern-matches the secret's literal value in console output and replaces it with ****. It only works for values Jenkins knows it bound — so transforming the secret (base64, substrings, writing it to a file) defeats masking. That's why I avoid transforming secrets and use set +x around sensitive shell commands.
Q3What happens if you lose master.key?
Every credential becomes permanently undecryptable — you must recreate them all. So I back up master.key separately from JENKINS_HOME and treat it as the crown jewels.
Q4How would you give team A access to their secrets but not team B's?
Put team A's jobs in a Jenkins folder, store the credentials in that folder's domain, and grant the team's role permissions only on that folder (Role Strategy plugin, Block 12). Credentials are then invisible and unusable outside it.

8.9 Under the hood — how credentials are protected

  1. Storage. Credentials are stored in $JENKINS_HOME/credentials.xml (metadata) with secret values encrypted using the key derived from secrets/master.key and secrets/hudson.util.Secret.
  2. Reference. A pipeline names an ID. At runtime the Credentials Provider API resolves that ID to an object — and access can be restricted by folder/domain.
  3. Binding. withCredentials writes the value into the environment of the steps inside it (or a temp file for file()), then removes it.
  4. Masking. Jenkins registers the plaintext value with the log filter, so any occurrence in console output is replaced with ****.
🚨What masking does NOT cover
  • Transformed values: base64, substrings, reversed, URL-encoded.
  • Values written into files that you then archiveArtifacts — the file is downloadable by anyone with read access.
  • Values inside a stash, or baked into a Docker image layer.
  • Values printed by a tool that buffers output differently (rare, but real with some CLIs).

8.10 Credential hygiene checklist for a real team

RuleImplementation
One credential per purpose per environmentdockerhub-prod-push, dockerhub-dev-push — never one shared key
Predictable IDs<system>-<env>-<purpose>; the ID is part of your codebase
Least privilegeDocker Hub access tokens with only the scopes needed; read-only deploy keys
Folder scopingCredentials in the team's folder domain, invisible elsewhere
RotationQuarterly, and immediately on any offboarding or suspected leak
Prefer short-livedOIDC / IAM roles / GitHub App tokens over long-lived passwords where possible
AuditCredentials plugin logs usage; the Audit Trail plugin records who created or changed them

8.11 Lab — see masking work, then defeat it

🧪Lab 8.1 · Prove it yourself
groovyRun this once, then read the console output carefully
withCredentials([string(credentialsId: 'sonar-token', variable: 'SECRET')]) {
    sh 'echo "raw      : $SECRET"'              // masked  -> raw      : ****
    sh 'echo "base64   : $(echo -n $SECRET | base64)"'   // LEAKED (transformed)
    sh 'echo "substring: ${SECRET:0:4}"'        // LEAKED (partial)
    sh 'echo "$SECRET" > secret.txt'            // written to the workspace!
}
// afterwards:
sh 'cat secret.txt'                             // masked here too — but the FILE is archived below
archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'secret.txt'        // anyone with read access can download it
Takeaway: masking protects the console, not the artifact store. Never write a secret to the workspace, and never archive a file that contained one.

8.12 Creating credentials from code (JCasC / Groovy)

groovyScript Console — add credentials programmatically (idempotent)
import com.cloudbees.plugins.credentials.*
import com.cloudbees.plugins.credentials.common.*
import com.cloudbees.plugins.credentials.domains.*
import com.cloudbees.plugins.credentials.impl.*
import org.jenkinsci.plugins.plaincredentials.impl.*
import hudson.util.Secret

def store = Jenkins.instance.getExtensionList(
        'com.cloudbees.plugins.credentials.SystemCredentialsProvider')[0].getStore()
def domain = Domain.global()

def addIfMissing(id, cred) {
    if (store.getCredentials(domain).any { it.id == id }) {
        println "exists: ${id}"
    } else {
        store.addCredentials(domain, cred); println "added: ${id}"
    }
}

addIfMissing('dockerhub-creds',
    new UsernamePasswordCredentialsImpl(CredentialsScope.GLOBAL, 'dockerhub-creds',
        'Docker Hub push token', 'your-user', 'dckr_pat_xxxxxxxxxxxx'))

addIfMissing('sonar-token',
    new StringCredentialsImpl(CredentialsScope.GLOBAL, 'sonar-token',
        'SonarQube analysis token', Secret.fromString('squ_xxxxxxxxxxxx')))
📌Prefer JCasC for this Hard-coding secrets in a script is exactly what we're trying to avoid. In practice I put credentials in jenkins.yaml with values injected from environment variables or a secret manager (see Block 22), and only use the script console for one-off recovery.

8.13 More interview Q&A

Q5How would you migrate secrets from Jenkins to a vault (HashiCorp Vault / AWS Secrets Manager)?
Install the matching credentials provider plugin so the vault appears as another credentials provider — pipelines keep using credentialsId and nothing in the Jenkinsfile changes. Then rotate each secret, move it to the vault, and delete the local copy. I'd do it incrementally per team to keep the blast radius small.
Q6What is a credentials domain?
A scope within the credentials store. The global domain is visible everywhere; folder domains are visible only to items in that folder. Combined with folder-scoped roles, it's how you make secrets physically unreachable by other teams.
Q7Can a malicious Jenkinsfile read credentials?
Yes, if it can run on the controller or use a credential in scope — which is exactly why Jenkinsfiles are code-reviewed, why shared libraries are access-controlled, and why credentials should be folder-scoped and least-privilege. On untrusted branches I restrict which credentials are available to that job.
Q8How do you rotate a credential without downtime?
Create the new credential with a new ID, update the pipeline to read the new ID, deploy, verify, then delete the old one and revoke it at the provider. Having the ID in the Jenkinsfile (not the value) is what makes this a config change instead of an incident.
09
Block 9 · Real engineering

Docker build & push to Docker Hub

Goal: turn tested code into a versioned, immutable container image in a registry — the artifact every modern deployment consumes.

9.1 Concepts: image, tag, registry, digest

🏷️ Tag

A mutable pointer to an image: myapp:1.4.2. latest is a tag, not magic — and a dangerous one in production.

🔒 Digest

Immutable content hash: myapp@sha256:9f…. What you actually deploy when you need reproducibility.

📦 Registry

Storage + API: Docker Hub, ECR, GHCR, Harbor. Jenkins authenticates, then pushes layers.

🧱 Layer

Each Dockerfile instruction = a layer. Layers are cached, which is why instruction order decides build speed.

bashInstall the plugin + give Jenkins docker access
# Plugins: "Docker Pipeline" (docker-workflow) + "Docker Commons" (docker-commons)
#   Manage Jenkins -> Plugins -> Available -> Docker Pipeline

# On the EC2 box (already done by ec2-install.sh step 6/7):
sudo usermod -aG docker jenkins
sudo systemctl restart jenkins

# Verify from Jenkins' point of view:
sudo -iu jenkins docker info
sudo -iu jenkins docker run --rm hello-world

9.2 A tagging strategy that won't bite you

TagExamplePurposeGood practice?
Build numbernodejs-app:42Traceable back to a Jenkins build✅
Git SHAnodejs-app:1a2b3c4Traceable back to exact code✅ best
Semantic versionnodejs-app:1.4.2Human releases✅
latestnodejs-app:latestConvenience for dev only⚠️ never deploy from it
Mutable env tagnodejs-app:prodQuick rollback pointer⚠️ only with a digest recorded
🏆The pattern I use Push three tags per build: the immutable <git-sha> (what you deploy), <build-number> (traceable to CI), and latest (dev convenience only). Deployments always reference the SHA or digest, so "what is running in prod?" is never a guess.

9.3 Two ways to build & push

groovyDocker Pipeline DSL — handles login/logout for you
stage('Docker Build & Push') {
    steps {
        script {
            def imageName = "docker.io/${DOCKER_USER}/nodejs-app"     // no tag here
            def app = docker.build("${imageName}:${env.GIT_SHA}", "--pull --no-cache .")

            docker.withRegistry('https://index.docker.io/v1/', 'dockerhub-creds') {
                app.push()                       // pushes :<git-sha>
                app.push(env.BUILD_NUMBER)       // pushes :<build-number>
                if (env.BRANCH_NAME == 'main') {
                    app.push('latest')           // only main moves :latest
                }
            }
            env.IMAGE_DIGEST = sh(script: "docker inspect --format='{{index .RepoDigests 0}}' ${imageName}:${env.GIT_SHA}",
                                  returnStdout: true).trim()
            echo "Pushed ${env.IMAGE_DIGEST}"
        }
    }
}
groovyFull control — useful when you need buildx, secrets or --cache-from
stage('Docker Build & Push') {
    steps {
        withCredentials([usernamePassword(credentialsId: 'dockerhub-creds',
                                          usernameVariable: 'REG_USER',
                                          passwordVariable: 'REG_PASS')]) {
            sh '''
                set -e
                echo "$REG_PASS" | docker login -u "$REG_USER" --password-stdin

                docker build \
                  --pull \
                  --build-arg BUILD_ENV=ci \
                  --tag $IMAGE:$GIT_SHA \
                  --tag $IMAGE:$BUILD_NUMBER \
                  .

                docker push $IMAGE:$GIT_SHA
                docker push $IMAGE:$BUILD_NUMBER
                docker logout
            '''
        }
    }
}

9.4 Faster, smaller, safer images

TechniqueHowGain
Correct layer orderCopy package*.json / requirements.txt first, install, then copy sourceDependency layers stay cached across code changes
.dockerignoreExclude node_modules, .git, coverageSmaller context, faster builds, no secret leakage
Multi-stage buildBuild in one stage, ship a slim runtime stageImages 5–10× smaller, fewer CVEs
Non-root userUSER appuserPasses CIS benchmark & Trivy checks (Block 18)
--pullAlways refresh base imagesPicks up upstream security patches
--cache-fromPull the previous image and use its layersBig speed-up on ephemeral agents
Pin base imagesFROM node:20.17-alpine3.20Reproducible builds, no surprise breakage
🚨Security reality: mounting /var/run/docker.sock Our learning setup gives the jenkins user access to the host Docker socket. That is root-equivalent — any job, and anyone who can create one, can own the host. Production alternatives:
  • Kaniko / Buildah — build images in userspace without a docker daemon.
  • Rootless BuildKit — a daemon that runs unprivileged.
  • Docker-in-Docker (dind) — a real daemon in a container; still privileged, but isolated from the host.
  • Ephemeral cloud agents — a fresh VM per build, destroyed afterwards.
Say this in interviews: it shows you understand the risk, not just the syntax.

9.5 Smoke-test the image before you push it

groovyRun the container, hit /health, then tear it down
stage('Container smoke test') {
    steps {
        script {
            def img = docker.build("${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA}", '.')
            img.inside("--rm -p ${3000 + env.EXECUTOR_NUMBER.toInteger()}:3000".toString() + ' -e PORT=3000') {
                // 'inside' runs the container, mounts the workspace, waits for health
                retry(10) {
                    sleep 3
                    sh "curl -fsS http://localhost:${3000 + env.EXECUTOR_NUMBER.toInteger()}/health"
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
💡Why the port maths? env.EXECUTOR_NUMBER gives each concurrent build a different port, so two builds running at once don't fight over 3000. That detail alone prevents a whole class of "it fails when two people push" bugs.

9.6 ✍️ Jenkinsfiles v9 — build & push

groovynodejs-app/Jenkinsfile — v9
pipeline {
    agent any
    options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES'); disableConcurrentBuilds() }

    environment {
        APP_NAME    = 'nodejs-app'
        DOCKER_USER = '<your-dockerhub-user>'
        IMAGE       = "${DOCKER_USER}/${APP_NAME}"
        REGISTRY    = 'https://index.docker.io/v1/'
        CREDS       = 'dockerhub-creds'
    }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) { checkout scmGit(branches: [[name: '*/main']],
                        extensions: [cleanBeforeCheckout()],
                        userRemoteConfigs: [[credentialsId: 'github-ssh',
                            url: 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git']]) }
                script {
                    dir(APP_NAME) {
                        env.GIT_SHA = sh(script:'git rev-parse --short HEAD', returnStdout:true).trim()
                    }
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Build')  { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh 'npm ci' } } }
        stage('Test')   { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh 'npm run test:junit' } } }

        stage('Docker Build') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) {
                    script {
                        app = docker.build("${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA}", "--pull .")
                    }
                }
            }
        }

        stage('Container Smoke Test') {
            steps {
                script {
                    app.inside('-p 3100:3000 -e PORT=3000') {
                        retry(10) {
                            sleep 3
                            sh 'curl -fsS http://localhost:3100/health || exit 1'
                        }
                    }
                }
            }
        }

        stage('Push') {
            when { branch 'main' }
            steps {
                script {
                    docker.withRegistry(env.REGISTRY, env.CREDS) {
                        app.push(env.GIT_SHA)
                        app.push(env.BUILD_NUMBER)
                        app.push('latest')
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    post {
        always {
            dir(APP_NAME) { junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml', allowEmptyResults: true }
            sh 'docker image prune -f || true'
        }
        success { echo "✅ Pushed ${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA}" }
    }
}
groovypython-app/Jenkinsfile — v9
pipeline {
    agent any
    options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES'); disableConcurrentBuilds() }

    environment {
        APP_NAME    = 'python-app'

        REGISTRY    = 'https://index.docker.io/v1/'
        CREDS       = 'dockerhub-creds'
    }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) { checkout scmGit(branches: [[name: '*/main']],
                        extensions: [cleanBeforeCheckout()],
                        userRemoteConfigs: [[credentialsId: 'github-ssh',
                            url: 'git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-zero-to-hero.git']]) }
                script {
                    dir(APP_NAME) {
                        env.GIT_SHA = sh(script:'git rev-parse --short HEAD', returnStdout:true).trim()
                    }
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Build')  { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh '''
                    python3 -m venv venv
                    . venv/bin/activate
                    pip install -r requirements-dev.txt
                ''' } } }
        stage('Test')   { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh '. venv/bin/activate && pytest --cov-fail-under=70' } } }

        stage('Docker Build') {
            steps {
                dir(APP_NAME) { script { app = docker.build("${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA}", "--pull .") } }
