                }
            }
        }

        stage('Push') {
            when { branch 'main' }
            steps {
                script {
                    docker.withRegistry(env.REGISTRY, env.CREDS) {
                        app.push(env.GIT_SHA)
                        app.push(env.BUILD_NUMBER)
                        app.push('latest')
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    post {
        always {
            dir(APP_NAME) { junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml', allowEmptyResults: true }
            sh 'docker image prune -f || true'
        }
        success { echo "✅ Pushed ${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA}" }
    }
}

9.7 ✅ Checklist

  • "Docker Pipeline" plugin installed; sudo -u jenkins docker info works.
  • Both pipelines produce an image tagged with the Git SHA and push three tags.
  • I can see the image on hub.docker.com with the correct tags.
  • I pulled the pushed image on another machine and hit /health successfully.
  • I can explain why latest must not be used for production deploys.

9.8 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1How does Jenkins build and push a Docker image?
With the Docker Pipeline plugin: docker.build("image:tag", "--pull .") creates the image and docker.withRegistry(url, credentialsId) { img.push() } authenticates and pushes. Credentials come from the Jenkins store, so no password ever appears in the pipeline or logs.
Q2What's your image tagging strategy?
Immutable Git SHA as the primary tag, plus the build number for traceability to CI, and latest for developer convenience only. Deploys always reference the SHA or the digest so the running artifact is unambiguous and rollback is trivial.
Q3Is giving Jenkins access to the Docker socket safe?
No — socket access is effectively root on the host, so any job (and anyone who can create one) could take over the machine. For production I'd use Kaniko or rootless BuildKit to build without a privileged daemon, or run each build on an ephemeral agent that is destroyed afterwards.
Q4How do you keep Docker builds fast?
Order Dockerfile instructions so dependency layers change least often, keep a proper .dockerignore, reuse the previous image's layers with --cache-from, and use multi-stage builds so the pushed image contains only what it needs to run.

9.9 🔥 Common errors

ErrorCauseFix
docker: command not foundDocker not installed, or not on PATH for the jenkins userInstall docker-ce; verify with sudo -iu jenkins docker -v
Cannot connect to the Docker daemonjenkins not in the docker group / daemon stoppedusermod -aG docker jenkins + restart both services
denied: requested access to the resource is deniedWrong repo name, not logged in, or token lacks write scopeCheck DOCKER_USER matches your namespace; re-login with an access token
docker build hangs on apt-getNo network from the build context / DNSPass --network=host, or fix the agent's DNS
Image works locally, crashes in CIArchitecture mismatch (arm64 vs amd64)Build for the target platform: docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64

9.10 Under the hood — layers, cache and the build context

Every Dockerfile instruction creates a layer identified by a content hash. Docker reuses a cached layer when the instruction and its inputs are unchanged — which is why instruction order dominates build time.

bashInspect what your image is really made of
docker history user/nodejs-app:abc1234          # layer list + sizes
docker images --format '{{.Repository}}:{{.Tag}}	{{.Size}}'
docker inspect user/nodejs-app:abc1234 --format '{{.Id}} {{.RepoTags}} {{index .RepoDigests 0}}'
docker scout quickview user/nodejs-app:abc1234  # (or) trivy image ...
⚠️The build context trap docker build . sends the entire directory to the daemon — including .git, node_modules and coverage unless you have a .dockerignore. A 500 MB context can add minutes to every build and, worse, can leak files into image layers.

9.11 Multi-stage, distroless and size

Base imageTypical sizeWhen
node:20~1.1 GBAlmost never in production
node:20-slim~240 MBGood default
node:20-alpine~180 MBSmallest; watch for musl/glibc edge cases
gcr.io/distroless/nodejs20~120 MBNo shell, no package manager — smallest attack surface
python:3.12-slim~150 MBOur Python default (multi-stage with a venv)
dockerfileDistroless runtime stage — no shell, no package manager, tiny surface
# ---- build stage ----
FROM node:20-alpine AS build
WORKDIR /app
COPY package*.json ./
RUN npm ci
COPY . .
RUN npm run build && npm prune --omit=dev

# ---- runtime stage ----
FROM gcr.io/distroless/nodejs20-debian12:nonroot
WORKDIR /app
ENV NODE_ENV=production PORT=3000
COPY --from=build /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY --from=build /app/dist ./dist
USER nonroot
EXPOSE 3000
CMD ["dist/server.js"]
💡Trade-off to mention in interviews Distroless images have no shell, so you cannot docker exec into them to debug and HEALTHCHECK CMD with shell syntax won't work. Teams that adopt them usually pair them with good observability (metrics, traces, structured logs) because live debugging inside the container is no longer an option.

9.12 Lab — a failing image, diagnosed properly

🧪Lab 9.1 · Build succeeds, container exits immediately Symptom: docker build is green; the container exits with code 1 and no useful log.
bashDiagnose in this order
docker run --rm user/nodejs-app:abc1234          # read the error
docker logs $(docker ps -lq)                     # logs of the last container

# 1) Is the entrypoint even present?
docker run --rm --entrypoint sh user/nodejs-app:abc1234 -c 'ls -la /app'

# 2) Is it a permissions problem? (non-root user can't read a file)
docker run --rm user/nodejs-app:abc1234 id
docker run --rm --entrypoint sh user/nodejs-app:abc1234 -c 'ls -la /app/node_modules | head'

# 3) Is the port/env wrong?
docker run --rm -e PORT=3000 -p 3000:3000 user/nodejs-app:abc1234

# 4) Does it start at all when you override the command?
docker run --rm -it --entrypoint sh user/nodejs-app:abc1234
Most common causes: app listens on 127.0.0.1 instead of 0.0.0.0 (unreachable from outside the container); USER can't read a file copied as root; missing NODE_ENV or PORT; distroless image with a shell-form CMD.

9.13 Registry reference

RegistrywithRegistry URLAuth
Docker Hubhttps://index.docker.io/v1/Username + access token
GitHub (GHCR)https://ghcr.ioUsername + PAT (write:packages)
AWS ECRhttps://<acct>.dkr.ecr.<region>.amazonaws.comecr:region credential, or IAM on the agent
GitLabhttps://registry.gitlab.comDeploy token / CI job token
Self-hosted Harborhttps://harbor.example.comRobot account

9.14 More interview Q&A

Q5Why does my Docker build take 10 minutes when nothing changed?
Almost always cache invalidation: the manifest is copied after the source, so npm ci re-runs every time; or --no-cache/--pull is forced; or the build context is huge. I check docker history, move the dependency layer up, and add a .dockerignore.
Q6How do you get a reproducible image?
Pin the base image by tag and ideally digest (FROM node:20.17-alpine3.20@sha256:…), install from a lockfile, avoid latest, and set a fixed build environment. Then reference the pushed image by digest in deployments.
Q7What is a dangling image, and why does my disk fill up?
A dangling image is a layer set no longer referenced by any tag — every rebuild leaves some. Prune them on a schedule (docker image prune -f) and cap build history; on a busy CI host this is the single biggest disk consumer.
Q8Multi-arch builds in CI?
With buildx: docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64,linux/arm64 -t img:tag --push. It needs QEMU emulation (slow) or native builders (fast). I'd run amd64 on every commit and the full multi-arch manifest on release branches only.
Q9How do you pass secrets into a build without leaking them into layers?
Use BuildKit secret mounts: RUN --mount=type=secret,id=npmrc,target=/root/.npmrc npm ci with --secret id=npmrc,src=…. Never ARG SECRET or COPY a secret — both persist in the image history.
10
Block 10 · Real engineering

Webhooks & automatic triggers

Goal: push code and see Jenkins start building within seconds — no human, no polling. Then understand every other trigger so you can pick the right one.

10.1 Every way a build can start

TriggerHowLatencyWhen to use
ManualBuild Now buttoninstantLearning, manual releases
Webhook (push) ⭐GitHub POSTs to Jenkins1–3 sThe default for CI
SCM pollingJenkins asks Git every N minutesup to N minWhen Jenkins can't be reached from the internet
Crontriggers { cron('H 2 * * *') }scheduledNightly builds, weekly security scans
Upstream / downstreambuild job: 'other-pipeline'instantPipeline chaining (build → deploy)
Remote / APIHTTP POST with a tokeninstantExternal systems, GitHub Actions, cron boxes
🧠Webhook vs Polling — the classic interview question Polling = Jenkins asks "anything new?" every few minutes: always works (even behind NAT/firewall), but wastes API calls and delays builds. Webhooks = Git tells Jenkins the instant something happens: fast and efficient, but Jenkins must be reachable by GitHub. Rule of thumb: webhooks in production, polling as the fallback when the network forbids webhooks.

10.2 Setting up the GitHub webhook

  1. Install the GitHub plugin (Manage Jenkins → Plugins → GitHub, id github). It creates the /github-webhook/ endpoint.
  2. Enable the trigger in the job: job → Configure → Build Triggers → tick GitHub hook trigger for GITScm polling. (For Multibranch: tick Scan by webhook and set a trigger token.)
  3. Create the webhook on GitHub: Repo → Settings → Webhooks → Add webhook:
    Payload URL: http://<JENKINS-IP>:8080/github-webhook/ (note the trailing slash)
    Content type: application/json · Secret: optional but recommended
    Events: Just the push event (add Pull requests for Block 15)
  4. Test it: push a commit. In GitHub → Webhooks → Recent Deliveries you should see a green 200. In Jenkins a new build appears within seconds.
groovytriggers {} in the Jenkinsfile — everything declarative
pipeline {
    agent any

    triggers {
        pollSCM('H/5 * * * *')          // poll every 5 minutes (H = hashed/staggered)
        cron('H 2 * * *')               // nightly at ~02:00, spread load with H
        // githubPush()                 // requires the GitHub plugin + job trigger ticked
        // upstream(upstreamProjects: 'library-pipeline', threshold: hudson.model.Result.SUCCESS)
    }
    ...
}
⚠️The triggers block doesn't create the webhook triggers { pollSCM(...) } only updates the job's internal configuration when the Jenkinsfile runs. The GitHub-side webhook must be created once, by hand (or by the GitHub Branch Source plugin in Block 15). If pushes do nothing, check GitHub → Recent Deliveries first.

10.3 Cron syntax, Jenkins style

cronMINUTE HOUR DAY-OF-MONTH MONTH DAY-OF-WEEK
H/15 * * * *     every 15 minutes
H 2 * * *        once a day around 02:00
H 2 * * 1-5      weekdays around 02:00
H(0-30) 9 * * *  between 09:00 and 09:30
0 3 1 * *        the 1st of the month at 03:00 sharp
💡Always use H (hash) H is a pseudo-random value derived from the job name. Ten jobs with 0 2 * * * all fire at exactly 02:00 and stampede the controller; H 2 * * * spreads them across the hour. Using H is a small habit that marks you as someone who has run Jenkins at scale.

10.4 Triggering from outside (API / remote token)

bashRemote trigger with an auth token
# 1. In the job: Build Triggers -> "Trigger builds remotely" -> Token: BUILD_ME
# 2. Create an API token: Manage Jenkins -> your user -> Security -> API Token
# 3. Fetch a CSRF crumb, then POST:
CRUMB=$(curl -s -u user:$TOKEN "$JENKINS_URL/crumbIssuer/api/xml?xpath=concat(//crumbRequestField,\":\",//crumb)")
curl -X POST -u user:$TOKEN -H "$CRUMB" \
     "$JENKINS_URL/job/nodejs-app/build?token=BUILD_ME"

# ...with parameters:
curl -X POST -u user:$TOKEN -H "$CRUMB" \
     "$JENKINS_URL/job/nodejs-app/buildWithParameters?token=BUILD_ME&ENV=qa&RUN_TESTS=true"

10.5 Chaining pipelines

groovyStart another job from a pipeline
// fire-and-forget
build job: 'python-app', parameters: [booleanParam(name: 'RUN_TESTS', value: true)], wait: false

// wait and use the result
def result = build job: 'deploy-pipeline',
                   parameters: [string(name: 'IMAGE_TAG', value: env.GIT_SHA)],
                   propagate: false, wait: true
echo "Deploy job finished: ${result.result}"
if (result.result != 'SUCCESS') { error "Deployment failed" }

10.6 ✍️ Jenkinsfiles v10 — triggered

groovyAdd to BOTH apps (identical block)
pipeline {
    agent any

    triggers {
        // Primary: the GitHub webhook (job must have "GitHub hook trigger for GITScm polling" ticked)
        // Fallback: poll every 10 minutes in case the webhook is down
        pollSCM('H/10 * * * *')
        // Nightly full run (also re-runs security scans in later blocks)
        cron('H 3 * * *')
    }

    options {
        timestamps()
        timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES')
        buildDiscarder(logRotator(numToKeepStr: '30', artifactNumToKeepStr: '5'))
        disableConcurrentBuilds()
    }
    ...
}

10.7 ✅ Checklist

  • GitHub webhook shows a green 200 in Recent Deliveries.
  • A git push starts a build within ~10 seconds — no clicking.
  • Nightly cron is configured and I understand what H does.
  • I triggered a build from curl with a token and a crumb.

10.8 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1Webhook vs SCM polling — which do you choose and why?
Webhooks: instant, no wasted API calls, and they also deliver PR events. The requirement is that GitHub can reach Jenkins. If the controller is behind a firewall or on a private network, polling is the pragmatic fallback — I'd poll every 5–10 minutes and accept the latency.
Q2A webhook is configured but nothing happens. How do you debug?
Start at GitHub → Webhooks → Recent Deliveries: a 200 means GitHub delivered it, so the problem is on the Jenkins side (job trigger not ticked, wrong branch filter, multibranch scan not configured). A 403 is usually a missing CSRF crumb; a timeout means the security group blocks GitHub. Then check /var/log/jenkins/jenkins.log and the job's Git polling log.
Q3What is the H in H/5 * * * *?
A hash of the job name that Jenkins substitutes for a stable pseudo-random number. It staggers schedules so hundreds of jobs don't fire on the same minute and stampede the controller.
Q4How do you start pipeline B when pipeline A finishes?
With the build job: step, passing parameters and choosing wait/propagate. Or declaratively with triggers { upstream(...) }. I set propagate: false when I want to handle the downstream result myself (for example to mark the build unstable instead of failed).

10.9 🔥 Common errors

SymptomCauseFix
Webhook returns 302Missing trailing slash on /github-webhook/Add the slash
Webhook returns 403CSRF protection, or anonymous has no Job/Build permissionSend the crumb header; grant the hook user minimal build rights
Delivery shows 200 but no buildJob trigger not enabled, or branch filter mismatchTick "GitHub hook trigger for GITScm polling"; check the branch specifier
Webhook times outSecurity group / firewall blocks GitHubAllow GitHub's IP ranges, or put Jenkins behind a reverse proxy, or fall back to polling
Polling log says "no changes"Wrong branch specifier (*/master vs */main)Match the real default branch name

10.10 Under the hood — what a webhook delivery actually is

GitHub sends an HTTP POST to /github-webhook/ with a JSON body describing the event and an X-GitHub-Event header (push, pull_request, …). The GitHub plugin:

  1. Verifies the payload (optionally with the HMAC secret you configured — always set it).
  2. Finds jobs whose SCM URL matches the repository.
  3. For classic jobs, triggers a build if GitHub hook trigger for GITScm polling is enabled; for Multibranch, triggers a repository scan.
  4. Returns 200. Any other code shows up red in GitHub's Recent Deliveries.
bashReplay a delivery by hand (great for debugging)
# Take the payload from GitHub -> Webhooks -> Recent Deliveries -> Request
curl -X POST http://<jenkins>:8080/github-webhook/ \
     -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
     -H "X-GitHub-Event: push" \
     -H "X-Hub-Signature-256: sha256=..." \
     --data @payload.json -i

10.11 Trigger decision matrix

ScenarioBest triggerWhy
Every push should buildWebhook (push event)Instant, no polling cost
Pull requests need validationWebhook + PR discovery (Multibranch)Builds the merge result, reports status to the PR
Jenkins behind a firewallpollSCMOutbound only — works without inbound access
Nightly full scancron('H 3 * * *')Expensive jobs off the critical path
Shared library changed → rebuild consumersupstream() or build job:Explicit dependency
External system (Jira, cron box, GitHub Actions)Remote token + APIAuthenticated, fire-and-forget
Release to productionManual + input approvalHuman in the loop

10.12 Lab — webhook forensics

🧪Lab 10.1 · Diagnose a webhook that does nothing Work top-down; stop at the first failure.
bashThe decision tree
# 1. Did GitHub deliver it?  (GitHub -> Webhooks -> Recent Deliveries)
#      200/204  -> Jenkins received it: problem is inside Jenkins
#      302      -> missing trailing slash on /github-webhook/
#      403      -> CSRF crumb missing, or anonymous lacks Job/Build
#      timeout  -> security group / firewall blocks GitHub

# 2. Can GitHub reach you at all?
curl -sS -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}\n" http://<jenkins>:8080/github-webhook/
# (a bare GET normally returns 405 or 302 -- that means you ARE reachable)

# 3. Is the Jenkins-side trigger enabled?
#    job -> Configure -> Build Triggers -> [x] GitHub hook trigger for GITScm polling

# 4. Does the repo URL in the job match the payload's repository URL exactly?
#    (ssh vs https, trailing .git, case) -- they must match

# 5. Still nothing? Watch the log while you push:
sudo tail -f /var/log/jenkins/jenkins.log | grep -i github

10.13 Cron reference

cronMINUTE HOUR DOM MONTH DOW — with H for staggering
H/5  * * * *     every 5 minutes   (H2/5 = at 2,7,12,...)
H    2 * * *     once daily ~02:00
H    2 * * 1-5   weekdays ~02:00
H(0-30) 9 * * *  somewhere between 09:00 and 09:30
0    3 1 * *     03:00 on the 1st of each month
H    H/4 * * *   every 4 hours
# Jenkins tip: H is a hash of the job name -> stable but staggered.

10.14 More interview Q&A

Q5How do you secure a webhook endpoint?
Set the webhook secret so Jenkins can verify the HMAC signature, allow only GitHub's IP ranges at the firewall (or put a proxy in front), use HTTPS, and grant the minimal permission needed to trigger builds rather than admin rights.
Q6Push events fire builds, but PR events do nothing. What's missing?
The webhook must subscribe to Pull requests, and the Multibranch job needs a PR discovery behaviour configured (e.g. "Discover pull requests from origin"). With those, Jenkins builds PR-<n> and exposes CHANGE_ID.
Q7How do you avoid a storm of builds when 10 commits are pushed quickly?
Enable the quiet period (a few seconds) so Jenkins coalesces changes, and use disableConcurrentBuilds(abortPrevious:true) on expensive jobs. For very busy repos, a branch-level strategy that builds only the latest commit is the answer.
Q8How do you rebuild only what a commit affected in a multi-service repo?
Compute changed paths from the changelog or use path filters in the branch source, then gate each service's stages with when. At scale I'd split into per-service jobs so the trigger itself is already scoped.
11
Block 11 · Real engineering

post{} mastery — cleanup, artifacts & signals

Goal: make every build finish responsibly: reports published, artifacts kept, workspaces cleaned, and the right people told the right thing.

11.1 Every condition, and the order they run in

OrderConditionFires whenPut here
1alwaysEvery runPublish tests & coverage, record results
2changedResult differs from the previous build"Status changed" notices
3abortedCancelled (manual or timeout)Release locks/resources
4regressionWas succeeding, now failingEscalate: "you broke main"
5fixedWas failing, now succeedingClose the incident, celebrate
6success / failure / unstable / unsuccessfulResult-specificNotifications, tagging, rollbacks
7cleanupAlways, and lastDelete temp files, stop containers, prune images
⚠️Two things beginners get wrong
  • All matching conditions run — they are not else if. A green build runs always and success.
  • cleanup runs after everything, even if a step inside always throws. Anything that must not be skipped goes there.

11.2 post at stage level too

groovyPer-stage post — surgical reactions
stage('Container Smoke Test') {
    steps { script { app.inside('-p 3100:3000') { sh 'curl -fsS localhost:3100/health' } } }
    post {
        failure {
            // only this stage failed -> give the on-call engineer the container logs
            sh 'docker logs $(docker ps -lq) || true'
            archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'container.log', allowEmptyArchive: true
        }
    }
}

11.3 The currentBuild object

groovyEverything useful about "this build"
post {
    always {
        script {
            echo "result        : ${currentBuild.currentResult}"   // SUCCESS / FAILURE / UNSTABLE / ABORTED
            echo "final result  : ${currentBuild.result}"          // null until set
            echo "duration      : ${currentBuild.durationString}"
            echo "url           : ${currentBuild.absoluteUrl}"
            echo "previous      : ${currentBuild.previousBuild?.result}"
            echo "changed files : ${currentBuild.changeSets}"      // who broke it, and which files

            // make the build history readable at a glance
            currentBuild.description = "${params.ENV ?: 'dev'} · ${env.GIT_SHA}"
            currentBuild.displayName = "#${env.BUILD_NUMBER} ${env.GIT_SHA}"
        }
    }
    failure {
        script {
            def culprits = currentBuild.changeSets.collectMany { it.items.collect { it.author.fullName } }.unique()
            echo "Notify: ${culprits.join(', ')}"
        }
    }
}

11.4 Workspace cleanup — the disk discipline

groovyThree levels of tidiness
post {
    cleanup {
        // 1. Fast: wipe the workspace (requires the Workspace Cleanup plugin)
        cleanWs()

        // 2. Targeted: keep the workspace, remove the heavy stuff
        // deleteDir()                        // nuke everything
        // sh 'rm -rf nodejs-app/venv'        // just the venv

        // 3. Reclaim Docker disk (the biggest offender on a CI box)
        sh '''
            docker image prune -f || true
            docker container prune -f || true
            docker builder prune -f || true
        '''
    }
}
⚠️Never cleanWs() while debugging A wiped workspace destroys the evidence you need. Clean up only once the pipeline is stable — or use the patterns option to keep logs: cleanWs(patterns: [[pattern: '**/*.log', type: 'INCLUDE']]).

11.5 Failing loudly, but correctly

groovyResult control patterns
// A. Keep going, but remember that something went wrong
catchError(buildResult: 'UNSTABLE', stageResult: 'FAILURE') {
    sh 'npm run lint'
}

// B. Retry a flaky network step, then give up properly
retry(3) { sh 'docker pull node:20-alpine' }

// C. Retry with a growing delay (needs a script block)
script {
    def attempts = 3
    for (int i = 1; i <= attempts; i++) {
        try { sh 'curl -fsS https://registry.npmjs.org/'; break }
        catch (err) {
            if (i == attempts) { error "Registry unreachable after ${attempts} attempts" }
            sleep(time: i * 5, unit: 'SECONDS')
        }
    }
}

// D. Soft-fail a stage but keep the pipeline green (use with care!)
warnError('Lint found issues') { sh 'npm run lint' }   // Pipeline: Basic Steps 2.290+

11.6 ✍️ Jenkinsfiles v11 — the professional post

groovynodejs-app/Jenkinsfile — v11 (post block)
pipeline {
    agent any
    options {
        timestamps(); timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES')
        buildDiscarder(logRotator(numToKeepStr: '30', artifactNumToKeepStr: '5'))
        disableConcurrentBuilds()
    }
    environment { APP_NAME = 'nodejs-app'; IMAGE = '<user>/nodejs-app' }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') { /* ...as before... */ }
        stage('Build')    { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh 'npm ci' } } }
        stage('Test')     { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh 'npm run test:junit' } } }
        stage('Docker Build') { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { script { app = docker.build("${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA}", '--pull .') } } } }
        stage('Push') {
            when { branch 'main' }
            steps { script { docker.withRegistry('https://index.docker.io/v1/','dockerhub-creds') { app.push(env.GIT_SHA) } } }
        }
    }

    post {
        always {
            script {
                // readable build history
                currentBuild.description = "${env.GIT_SHA ?: 'unknown'}"
            }
            dir(APP_NAME) {
                junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml', allowEmptyResults: true
                recordCoverage tools: [[parser: 'COBERTURA', pattern: 'coverage/cobertura-coverage.xml']],
                               sourceCodeRetention: 'LAST_BUILD'
            }
            archiveArtifacts artifacts: '**/reports/**,**/coverage/**', allowEmptyArchive: true
        }
        success  { echo "✅ ${APP_NAME} ${env.GIT_SHA} built and pushed" }
        unstable { echo "⚠️  Built, but quality gates were not met: ${env.BUILD_URL}" }
        failure  {
            script {
                def culprits = currentBuild.changeSets
                        .collectMany { it.items.collect { it.author.fullName } }.unique()
                echo "❌ FAILED — last commit by: ${culprits.join(', ') ?: 'unknown'}"
            }
        }
        fixed    { echo "🎉 Back to green after a failure" }
        regression { echo "🔥 Regression: this used to pass — investigate ${env.BUILD_URL}" }
        cleanup {
            sh 'docker image prune -f || true'
            cleanWs()
        }
    }
}
groovypython-app/Jenkinsfile — v11 (post block)
pipeline {
    agent any
    options {
        timestamps(); timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES')
        buildDiscarder(logRotator(numToKeepStr: '30', artifactNumToKeepStr: '5'))
        disableConcurrentBuilds()
    }
    environment { APP_NAME = 'python-app'; IMAGE = '<user>/python-app' }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') { /* ...as before... */ }
        stage('Build')    { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh '''
                    python3 -m venv venv; . venv/bin/activate
                    pip install -r requirements-dev.txt
                ''' } } }
        stage('Test')     { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { sh '. venv/bin/activate && pytest --cov-fail-under=70' } } }
        stage('Docker Build') { steps { dir(APP_NAME) { script { app = docker.build("${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA}", '--pull .') } } } }
        stage('Push') {
            when { branch 'main' }
            steps { script { docker.withRegistry('https://index.docker.io/v1/','dockerhub-creds') { app.push(env.GIT_SHA) } } }
        }
    }

    post {
        always {
            script { currentBuild.description = "${env.GIT_SHA ?: 'unknown'}" }
            dir(APP_NAME) {
                junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml', allowEmptyResults: true
                recordCoverage tools: [[parser: 'COBERTURA', pattern: 'coverage.xml']],
                               sourceCodeRetention: 'LAST_BUILD'
            }
            archiveArtifacts artifacts: '**/reports/**,**/htmlcov/**', allowEmptyArchive: true
        }
        success  { echo "✅ ${APP_NAME} ${env.GIT_SHA} built and pushed" }
        unstable { echo "⚠️  Built, but quality gates were not met: ${env.BUILD_URL}" }
        failure  {
            script {
                def culprits = currentBuild.changeSets
                        .collectMany { it.items.collect { it.author.fullName } }.unique()
                echo "❌ FAILED — last commit by: ${culprits.join(', ') ?: 'unknown'}"
            }
        }
        fixed    { echo "🎉 Back to green after a failure" }
        regression { echo "🔥 Regression: this used to pass — investigate ${env.BUILD_URL}" }
        cleanup {
            sh 'rm -rf python-app/venv || true'
            sh 'docker image prune -f || true'
            cleanWs()
        }
    }
}

11.7 ✅ Checklist

  • I forced a failure and saw always, failure and cleanup all run in the right order.
  • Build history shows a readable description with the commit SHA.
  • Artifacts (JUnit XML, coverage) are downloadable from the build page.
  • Disk usage stopped growing between builds (cleanup works).

11.8 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1What goes in always vs cleanup?
always for things that are part of the result — publishing test reports, coverage and artifacts. cleanup for things that must happen no matter what, including if always itself throws: deleting temp files, stopping containers, pruning images. cleanup always runs last.
Q2How do you notify only when a build breaks — and only the person who broke it?
In post { failure { } } I read currentBuild.changeSets to extract the commit authors of this build, then message them (Slack DM or email). I use regression for "main was green, now it's broken" and fixed to close the loop.
Q3How do you stop a stage failing the whole pipeline?
catchError(buildResult:'UNSTABLE', stageResult:'FAILURE') — the stage is marked red, the build is marked unstable, and the pipeline continues. I use it for advisory checks (lint, optional scans), never for tests that guard a release.
Q4How do you stop Jenkins running out of disk?
Three layers: buildDiscarder(logRotator(...)) to cap build history, cleanWs() in post { cleanup } for workspaces, and regular docker image/container/builder prune — Docker layers are usually the biggest consumer on a CI machine.

11.9 Under the hood — build results in detail

ResultSet bySubsequent stagesTypical action
SUCCESSNothing failedRunDeploy / notify green
UNSTABLEjunit failures, quality gate, catchError, unstable()Run (unless skipStagesAfterUnstable())Warn, don't page
FAILURENon-zero sh exit, error(), exceptionSkippedAlert the author
ABORTEDUser cancel, timeout, declined inputSkippedRelease resources
NOT_BUILTStage skipped by when—None
groovyReading and setting results deliberately
post {
    always {
        script {
            echo "currentResult   : ${currentBuild.currentResult}"   // live value
            echo "result          : ${currentBuild.result}"          // final value (null if unset)
            echo "durationString  : ${currentBuild.durationString}"
            echo "previous result : ${currentBuild.previousBuild?.result}"
            echo "build url       : ${currentBuild.absoluteUrl}"

            // Per-stage results (useful for a summary table in notifications)
            def stages = currentBuild.rawBuild.getExecution().getStages()
                          .collect { "${it.getName()}: ${it.getStatus()}" }
            echo stages.join('
')
        }
    }
}

11.10 Lab — the ordering experiment

🧪Lab 11.1 · Prove the post order yourself Run this on a pipeline both ways (success and forced failure) and read the console carefully.
groovyWhich conditions fire, and in what order?
pipeline {
    agent any
    parameters { booleanParam name: 'FAIL', defaultValue: false }
    stages {
        stage('work') {
            steps {
                script { if (params.FAIL) { error 'deliberate failure' } else { echo 'ok' } }
            }
        }
    }
    post {
        always      { echo 'ORDER 1: always' }
        changed     { echo 'ORDER 2: changed' }
        aborted     { echo 'ORDER 3: aborted' }
        regression  { echo 'ORDER 4: regression' }
        fixed       { echo 'ORDER 5: fixed' }
        success     { echo 'ORDER 6: success' }
        failure     { echo 'ORDER 6: failure' }
        unstable    { echo 'ORDER 6: unstable' }
        cleanup     { echo 'ORDER 7: cleanup (always last)' }
    }
}
Run it green, then with FAIL=true (regression), then green again (fixed). You now have first-hand evidence for the ordering table.

11.11 Notification payload design

💡Every alert should answer five questions What broke · Which commit/branch · Who is responsible · Where to look (deep link) · What to do next. If a message can't answer all five, people will ignore it.
groovyA well-formed failure message
post {
    failure {
        script {
            def c = currentBuild.changeSets.collectMany { it.items }.take(3)
            def who = c.collect { it.author.fullName }.unique().join(', ') ?: 'unknown'
            slackSend(channel: '#ci', color: 'danger', message: """
                :x: *${env.JOB_NAME}* #${env.BUILD_NUMBER} FAILED
                • Branch : ${env.BRANCH_NAME ?: 'n/a'}  • Commit: ${env.GIT_SHA ?: 'n/a'}
                • Stage  : ${env.STAGE_NAME}
                • Author : ${who}
                • <${env.BUILD_URL}|Console log>  • <${env.BUILD_URL}console|Full output>
            """.stripIndent().trim())
        }
    }
}

11.12 More interview Q&A

Q5How do you make sure a cleanup step always runs?
Put it in post { cleanup { } }, which runs after every other post condition — including if a step inside always throws. If the pipeline uses agent none, wrap it in node('label') { } so it has somewhere to execute.
Q6How do you attach build artifacts to a failure notification?
archiveArtifacts in post { always }, then link ${env.BUILD_URL}artifact/ in the message, or use emailext attachLog: true to include the console output. Deep links matter more than attachments — nobody downloads a file to find out what happened.
Q7What should never be in a post block?
Anything that can fail the build for a non-build reason. Notification and cleanup steps must be wrapped so that a Slack outage or a missing file can't turn a green build red.
Q8How do you record who approved or who broke a build for audits?
Capture the approver with submitterParameter on input and store it in currentBuild.description; derive the culprit from currentBuild.changeSets. Both end up in the build record, which I retain for the audit window with buildDiscarder.
12
Block 12 · Real engineering

RBAC, users & permissions

Goal: turn "anyone can do anything" into least privilege: the right people can do the right things, and nobody can read production secrets. This is the block that separates an admin from a user.

12.1 Authentication vs Authorization

🔐 Security Realm (authentication)

Who are you? Jenkins' own user database, LDAP/Active Directory, GitHub OAuth, SAML, SSO.

🛂 Authorization Strategy (authorization)

What may you do? Matrix-based, Project-based Matrix, or Role-Based Strategy.

Find both under Manage Jenkins → Security.

12.2 Why Role-Based beats Matrix at scale

StrategyHow it worksProblem
Anyone can do anythingNo restrictionsNever acceptable beyond a laptop
Logged-in users can do anythingOne checkbox per permissionEvery user is effectively admin
Matrix-based securityA grid of users × ~50 permissionsEdit 20 users' grids individually — unmaintainable
Project-based MatrixMatrix per jobBetter, but still per-job editing
Role-Based Strategy ⭐Define roles, assign users to rolesNeeds the plugin — and is what real companies use

12.3 Setting up Role-Based Authorization Strategy

  1. Install the plugin: Role-based Authorization Strategy (role-strategy).
  2. Manage Jenkins → Security → Authorization → select Role-Based Strategy → Save.
  3. Manage Jenkins → Security → Manage and Assign Roles appears. Configure three role types:
Role typeControlsExamples
Global rolesController-wide powersadmin (Overall/Administer) · readonly (Overall/Read) · developer (Overall/Read + Job/Read)
Item rolesJobs, by name patternnodejs-team → pattern nodejs-.* with Build/Cancel/Configure · python-team → python-.*
Agent rolesWhich agents a role may usecan-use-linux → pattern linux-.*
uiA sensible three-role setup for this project
Manage Roles
  GLOBAL
    admin       : Overall → Administer, Read · Credentials → Create/Delete/ManageDomains
                  (keep this to 1-2 people!)
    developer   : Overall → Read · Job → Read · View → Read
    readonly    : Overall → Read

  ITEM
    nodejs-team : Pattern  nodejs-.*   → Job: Build, Cancel, Read, Workspace, Configure
    python-team : Pattern  python-.*   → Job: Build, Cancel, Read, Workspace, Configure
    viewer      : Pattern  .*          → Job: Read

  AGENT
    linux-users : Pattern  linux-.*    → Agent: Build, Connect

Assign Roles
  you          → admin, nodejs-team, python-team
  teammate-a   → developer, nodejs-team
  teammate-b   → developer, python-team
  intern       → readonly, viewer
💡Item patterns are regex, and they're your whole security model nodejs-.* matches every job whose name starts with nodejs-. Combine that with Folders (New Item → Folder) and you get per-team namespaces where credentials can also be scoped — a team literally cannot see another team's secrets.

12.4 The hardening checklist

SettingWhereWhy
Disable anonymous readSecurity → AuthorizationOtherwise anyone on the network sees job names, logs and repo URLs
Enable CSRF protectionSecurity → CSRF Protection (default on)Stops cross-site request forgery against build endpoints
Agent → Controller Access ControlSecurity → AgentsStops a compromised agent reading credentials or calling controller APIs
Use API tokens, not passwordsUser → Security → API TokenRevocable, scoped, and not your account password
Script Approval reviewSecurity → In-process Script ApprovalReview every Groovy signature a pipeline wants to call
HTTPS via reverse proxyNginx + Let's EncryptNever send Jenkins credentials over plain HTTP
Audit trailPlugins: Audit Trail, Job Config HistoryWho changed what, and when — required in regulated teams
🚨Locked yourself out? Recovery If you enable Role-Based Strategy and remove your own admin rights, Jenkins will not let you back in through the UI. Recovery (needs server access):
bashEmergency admin recovery
# 1. Stop Jenkins
sudo systemctl stop jenkins

# 2. Edit the global config: disable authorization entirely
sudo vim /var/lib/jenkins/config.xml
#    <useSecurity>true</useSecurity>   ->   <useSecurity>false</useSecurity>
#    (also remove the <authorizationStrategy> block if you like)

# 3. Start, log in as admin, re-configure roles properly, re-enable security
sudo systemctl start jenkins
Always keep a second admin account. This is the #1 self-inflicted Jenkins outage.

12.5 RBAC in the real world — the story to tell

🎤How to describe it in an interview "Controllers used the Role-Based Authorization Strategy plugin. We had a global readonly role for everyone, a developer role with build and workspace access, and item roles scoped by folder pattern so the Node team could only configure nodejs-* jobs. Credentials were stored in folder domains, so a team physically couldn't use another team's secrets. Anonymous access was off, agents had restricted controller access, and every admin action was captured in an audit trail."

12.6 ✅ Checklist

  • Role-Strategy plugin installed and active.
  • Created readonly, developer and admin global roles.
  • Created two item roles with regex patterns and tested them with a second user account.
  • Anonymous read is off; you can prove it in a private/incognito window.
  • You know the config.xml lockout recovery procedure.

12.7 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1How do you implement RBAC in Jenkins?
With the Role-based Authorization Strategy plugin: global roles for controller-wide permissions, item roles scoped by job-name pattern or folder, and agent roles restricting which agents a user's builds may run on. Users are assigned to roles, and I follow least privilege — most people get read + build, a few get configure, and admin is limited to one or two people.
Q2How do you stop one team seeing another team's credentials?
Put each team's jobs in a folder, store their credentials in that folder's credentials domain, and grant the team's item role only on that folder pattern. Credentials outside the folder are neither visible nor referenceable by their pipelines.
Q3What did you lock down on a production controller?
Anonymous access off, CSRF on, agent→controller access control on, HTTPS via a reverse proxy, admins limited to a named few, API tokens instead of passwords, and an audit-trail plugin so configuration changes are attributable.
Q4You're locked out of Jenkins after a permissions change. What now?
I have server access, so I stop Jenkins, set <useSecurity>false</useSecurity> in /var/lib/jenkins/config.xml, restart, log back in as admin, fix the roles, and re-enable security. To prevent it happening I always keep a second admin account and test role changes with a non-admin user first.
Q5What's the difference between authentication and authorization here?
Authentication (the security realm) decides who you are — local database, LDAP, GitHub OAuth. Authorization (the strategy) decides what you may do — matrix or role-based permissions. They're configured independently in Manage Jenkins → Security.

12.8 Under the hood — how authorization is evaluated

Jenkins asks, for every action, "does this user hold permission X on item Y?" The AuthorizationStrategy answers using the ACL attached to the item. With Role Strategy, item roles contribute ACL entries matched by job-name regex, which is why folder structure is a security control, not just tidiness.

ConceptMeaning
PermissionAn atomic capability: Job/Build, Job/Configure, Credentials/Create, Overall/Administer, Run/Delete
RoleA named bundle of permissions (global / item / agent)
AssignmentUser or group → role. Groups come from the security realm (LDAP, GitHub orgs, SAML)
Item patternRegex over the full item name, e.g. nodejs-.* or team-a/.* for folders
FolderA namespace that scopes both item roles and credentials
groovyScript Console — audit who has what
import com.michelin.cio.hudson.plugins.rolestrategy.*
import jenkins.model.Jenkins

def strategy = Jenkins.instance.getAuthorizationStrategy()
if (strategy instanceof RoleBasedAuthorizationStrategy) {
    strategy.getRoleMaps().each { type, map ->
        println "=== ${type} ==="
        map.getRoles().each { role ->
            println "  ${role.name}  pattern=${role.pattern}"
            println "    perms : ${role.permissions.collect { it.name }.join(', ')}"
            println "    users : ${map.getAssignedSIDs(role.name).join(', ')}"
        }
    }
}

12.9 A complete hardening runbook

  1. Enable security with a real realm (local DB for labs, LDAP/OIDC/SAML in companies).
  2. Set Role-Based Strategy; create readonly, developer, admin global roles.
  3. Create folders per team and item roles scoped to <folder>/.*.
  4. Move credentials into folder domains; delete the global duplicates.
  5. Turn off anonymous access; verify in a private window.
  6. Enable Agent → Controller Access Control so agents can't read credentials or call controller APIs.
  7. Put HTTPS in front; bind 8080 to localhost.
  8. Issue API tokens for automation; never embed account passwords.
  9. Install an audit trail (Audit Trail + Job Config History).
  10. Keep a second admin account so one mistake can't lock you out.
⚠️The one that surprises people Overall/Read is required for almost anything to be visible at all — including for the github-webhook/ endpoint to work when using authenticated triggers. Grant it to a dedicated ci-trigger service account rather than to anonymous.

12.10 Lab — verify your RBAC like an attacker

🧪Lab 12.1 · Test with a second account Create user intern with only readonly, then log in as them (private window) and confirm:
bashWhat an unprivileged user must NOT be able to do
# 1. Anonymous sees nothing
curl -s -o /dev/null -w "anon: %{http_code}\n" http://<jenkins>:8080/

# 2. A read-only user cannot build
curl -s -o /dev/null -w "intern build: %{http_code}\n" \
     -u intern:pass -X POST http://<jenkins>:8080/job/nodejs-app/build

# 3. A read-only user cannot read credentials
curl -s -o /dev/null -w "intern creds: %{http_code}\n" \
     -u intern:pass http://<jenkins>:8080/credentials/store/system/domain/_/api/json

# Expected: 403 (or 401) on 1-3, never 200
Then in the UI confirm the intern can see nodejs-app but cannot click Build Now, and cannot see the Script Console.

12.11 More interview Q&A

Q6How do you integrate Jenkins with LDAP or SSO?
Change the Security Realm: LDAP plugin with a server URL, bind DN and user search base, or SAML/OIDC plugins for SSO. Authentication then delegates to the directory while authorization stays role-based, with groups from the directory mapped to Jenkins roles.
Q7What is Agent → Controller Access Control?
A security subsystem that restricts what an agent process can ask the controller to do — for example reading credentials or calling remoting APIs. Without it, anyone who can run a build on an agent effectively has controller-level access, which is a serious risk in shared environments.
Q8How do you give a contractor temporary access?
Create a named account, assign the least-privilege role on the specific folder, and set a calendar reminder or an expiry process to remove it. I never share accounts, and I review the user list quarterly.
Q9How do you prove compliance (who changed what)?
The Audit Trail plugin records authentication and configuration events, and Job Config History keeps every version of every job configuration — so I can show who changed a pipeline definition and when, which is what auditors ask for.
Q10Jenkins is fully open on a new install. What do you do on day one?
Run the setup wizard with my own admin account (never the default), disable anonymous access, enable role-based authorization, create folders and scoped credentials, enable agent→controller access control, and put it behind HTTPS.
13
Block 13 · Real engineering

Nodes, agents, labels & tool installers

Goal: stop running builds on the controller. Add agents, route work with labels, and let Jenkins install toolchains for you.

13.1 First: set controller executors to 0

🚨The production rule Manage Jenkins → Nodes → Built-In Node → Configure → # of executors → 0. Builds on the controller can: exhaust memory and take the UI down, read every credential on the box, and break Jenkins for everyone. A controller orchestrates; agents execute.

13.2 Adding a permanent agent over SSH

  1. Prepare the agent machine (Ubuntu): install Java, git and whatever your builds need. Create the same user you'll connect as.
bashOn the new agent VM
sudo apt-get update -y
sudo apt-get install -y openjdk-21-jre-headless git docker.io
sudo useradd -m -s /bin/bash jenkins
sudo mkdir -p /home/jenkins/agent && sudo chown -R jenkins:jenkins /home/jenkins/agent

# The controller connects with an SSH key -> put its PUBLIC key here:
sudo -iu jenkins bash -c 'mkdir -p ~/.ssh && chmod 700 ~/.ssh && \
  echo "ssh-ed25519 AAAA... jenkins@controller" > ~/.ssh/authorized_keys && \
  chmod 600 ~/.ssh/authorized_keys'
  1. Store the private key in Jenkins: Credentials → SSH Username with private key → ID agent-key.
  2. Register the node: Manage Jenkins → Nodes → New Node → name linux-1 → Permanent Agent:
    Remote root directory: /home/jenkins/agent
    Labels: linux docker node20 (space-separated)
    Usage: Only build jobs with label expressions matching this node
    Launch method: Launch agents via SSH → Host: private IP → Credentials: agent-key → Host Key Verification: Non verifying (or Known hosts file for production)
  3. Save and watch it connect. If it fails, click the node → Log — the launch log tells you exactly why.

13.3 Labels — how work is routed

groovyLabel expressions
agent { label 'docker' }                 // any agent labelled docker
agent { label 'linux && node20' }        // both labels
agent { label 'docker || aws' }          // either
agent { label 'linux && !experimental' } // linux, but not the experimental box
agent { label "${params.AGENT_LABEL}" }  // chosen at build time
🏷️Label naming conventions that scale Label by capability, not by hostname: linux, windows, docker, node20, python3.12, arm64, gpu. Then when a machine dies you replace it with another carrying the same labels and no pipeline changes. If you label by host (agent-7), every pipeline is hard-wired to a box.

13.4 Tool installers — no more "works on my machine"

Install the NodeJS plugin, then Manage Jenkins → Tools → NodeJS installations → Add NodeJS → name node20 → version NodeJS 20.x. Pipelines then reference it by name:

groovytools {} — auto-install + PATH injection
pipeline {
    agent { label 'linux' }

    tools {
        nodejs 'node20'          // name must match Manage Jenkins -> Tools
        // jdk   'jdk21'
        // maven 'maven3'
        // git   'Default'
    }

    stages {
        stage('Verify') {
            steps { sh 'node -v && npm -v' }   // PATH points at the installed tool
        }
    }
}
⚠️Installers download on every fresh agent Auto-installers fetch the toolchain on first use — great for correctness, slow on ephemeral agents. At scale, bake tools into the agent image (Packer/Ansible) and just declare them, or use Docker agents (next section).

13.5 Docker agents — the cleanest build environment

groovyRun stages inside containers
pipeline {
    agent none
    stages {
        stage('Node tests') {
            agent {
                docker {
                    image 'node:20-alpine'
                    args  '-v /var/lib/jenkins/.npm:/root/.npm'   // persist the npm cache
                    reuseNode true                                // stay on the same node/workspace
                }
            }
            steps { sh 'node -v && npm ci && npm run test:junit' }
        }

        stage('Python tests') {
            agent { docker { image 'python:3.12-slim'; reuseNode true } }
            steps { sh 'python -m venv venv && . venv/bin/activate && pip install -r requirements-dev.txt && pytest' }
        }

        stage('Build image') {
            agent { label 'docker' }          // needs the docker socket -> host agent
            steps { sh 'docker build -t app:$GIT_SHA .' }
        }
    }
}
🔁reuseNode matters By default a docker agent allocates a new workspace on a possibly different node. reuseNode true keeps the same node and workspace, so files from earlier stages are still there — usually what you want inside a single pipeline.

13.6 Which agent strategy when?

ApproachProsConsBest for
Built-in nodeZero setupInsecure, doesn't scaleLearning only
Permanent SSH agentsPersistent caches, predictableYou patch and maintain themSmall stable teams
Docker agentsPerfectly reproducible toolchainsNeeds docker on the host; socket riskPolyglot repos (ours!)
Cloud / KubernetesAuto-scales, ephemeral, isolatedSetup complexity, cold startsLarge teams, bursty load

13.7 ✍️ Jenkinsfiles v13 — labelled + containerised

groovyPattern to add to BOTH apps
pipeline {
    agent none                       // nothing reserved for the whole run

    options { timestamps(); timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES'); disableConcurrentBuilds() }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            agent { label 'linux' }
            steps { checkout scm }   // now correct: Multibranch supplies scm (Block 15)
        }

        // --- Node version of the test stage ---
        stage('Test (Node 20)') {
            agent { docker { image 'node:20-alpine'; args '-v /var/lib/jenkins/.npm:/root/.npm'; reuseNode true } }
            steps { sh 'npm ci && npm run test:junit' }
        }

        stage('Package & push') {
            agent { label 'docker' }
            steps {
                script {
                    def img = docker.build("${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA}", '--pull .')
                    docker.withRegistry('https://index.docker.io/v1/', 'dockerhub-creds') {
                        img.push(env.GIT_SHA)
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }

    post {
        always { junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml', allowEmptyResults: true }
        cleanup { node('linux') { cleanWs() } }   // post has no agent -> ask for one
    }
}
⚠️The post-with-agent none trap When the pipeline uses agent none, a top-level post block has no node, so steps like sh or cleanWs fail with "Required context class hudson.FilePath is missing". Wrap them in node('label') { … } — as above.

13.8 ✅ Checklist

  • Controller executors set to 0 (or at least you know why you haven't yet).
  • One SSH agent connected, with meaningful labels.
  • NodeJS tool installer configured and referenced by tools { nodejs 'node20' }.
  • A stage ran inside node:20-alpine and you saw Jenkins pull the image.

13.9 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1Why shouldn't builds run on the controller?
Because the controller holds all credentials and serves the UI. A build running there can read every secret, consume all memory and take CI down for the whole company. I set the built-in node's executors to 0 and run everything on agents.
Q2How does Jenkins decide which agent runs a build?
By label expression. The pipeline declares agent { label 'docker && linux' }; Jenkins picks any connected agent whose labels satisfy it and has a free executor. If none qualifies the build waits in the queue.
Q3Permanent agents vs Docker agents vs Kubernetes?
Permanent agents are simple and give warm caches but need maintenance. Docker agents give a reproducible, version-pinned toolchain per stage with no host pollution — ideal for polyglot repos. Kubernetes agents auto-scale and are fully ephemeral, at the cost of setup complexity and cold-start time.
Q4An agent keeps going offline. How do you investigate?
Check the node's launch log in Jenkins first, then from the controller: network reachability, SSH credentials, Java present on the agent, disk space in the remote root directory, and clock skew. For flapping agents I look at journalctl on the agent and the controller's jenkins.log.
Q5What does the tools block do?
It references a tool configured under Manage Jenkins → Tools by name, installs it on the agent if needed, and prepends it to PATH for the duration of the pipeline — so node -v resolves to the exact version the project wants.

13.10 Under the hood — how an agent connects

Launch methodWho initiatesRequirementsTypical use
SSH (outbound)Controller → agentSSH server + Java on agent; key credential in JenkinsPermanent VMs, on-prem
Inbound / JNLPAgent → controllerPort 50000 (or WebSocket); agent jar + secretAgents behind NAT
Docker / KubernetesController → cloud APIDocker host or k8s cluster credentialsEphemeral, auto-scaling
bashInbound (JNLP) agent — for agents that can't be reached inbound
# On the agent: download the launcher and connect out to the controller
curl -sSLO http://<jenkins>:8080/jnlpJars/agent.jar
java -jar agent.jar \
  -url http://<jenkins>:8080/ \
  -secret <SECRET_FROM_NODE_PAGE> \
  -name linux-2 \
  -workDir /home/jenkins/agent &

# As a systemd unit so it survives reboots:
sudo tee /etc/systemd/system/jenkins-agent.service >/dev/null <<'UNIT'
[Unit]
Description=Jenkins Inbound Agent
After=network.target
[Service]
User=jenkins
ExecStart=/usr/bin/java -jar /opt/agent/agent.jar -url http://<jenkins>:8080/ -secret <SECRET> -name linux-2 -workDir /home/jenkins/agent
Restart=always
[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target
UNIT
sudo systemctl enable --now jenkins-agent

13.11 Tool installers vs baked images

ApproachProsConsChoose when
Auto-installer (tools{})Zero agent prep; version controlled in the pipelineDownloads on cold agents; slower first buildSmall teams, few toolchains
Baked agent image (Packer/Ansible)Fast, reproducible, no network at build timeYou maintain the imageScale, air-gapped, strict reproducibility
Docker agent (agent { docker })Exact toolchain per stage, zero host pollutionNeeds Docker on the host; socket riskPolyglot repos — our choice
groovyDocker agent with a persistent cache mount and a sidecar network
stage('Integration tests') {
    agent {
        docker {
            image 'node:20-alpine'
            args  '-v /var/lib/jenkins/.npm:/root/.npm ' +
                  '--network ci-net ' +                  // reach a sidecar by name
                  '-e TZ=UTC'
            reuseNode true
        }
    }
    steps {
        sh 'npm ci && npm run test:integration'
    }
}

13.12 Lab — capacity planning on one machine

🧪Lab 13.1 · How many executors should this box have?
bashMeasure, then decide
nproc                                  # vCPUs
free -g                                # RAM
# Rule of thumb for build agents:
#   executors ~= min(vCPU, floor(RAM_GB / 2))
#   4 vCPU / 8 GB  -> 4 executors (if builds are CPU bound, start with 2-3)
# A controller needs ~1 GB + 1 GB per 100 jobs for the UI alone.
Then deliberately over-subscribe: set 8 executors on a 2-CPU box, run 4 builds at once, and watch the queue and load average. You will feel why "more executors" is not automatically "faster".

13.13 Kubernetes agents (the scale answer)

groovyEphemeral pod agent — one pod per build, destroyed after
pipeline {
    agent {
        kubernetes {
            label 'nodejs-agent'
            defaultContainer 'node'
            yaml '''
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
spec:
  containers:
  - name: node
    image: node:20-alpine
    command: ['cat']
    tty: true
    resources:
      requests: { cpu: "500m", memory: "1Gi" }
      limits:   { cpu: "2",    memory: "2Gi" }
  - name: dind
    image: docker:24-dind
    securityContext: { privileged: true }
'''
        }
    }
    stages {
        stage('Test')  { steps { container('node') { sh 'npm ci && npm test' } } }
        stage('Build') { steps { container('dind') { sh 'docker build -t app:$GIT_SHA .' } } }
    }
}
💡Why this matters at scale Every build gets a clean, disposable environment — no workspace drift, no "someone installed something weird on agent 3". The cost is pod start-up latency (typically 5–20 s) and the need to cache dependencies in a PVC or registry.

13.14 More interview Q&A

Q6SSH agent vs JNLP agent?
SSH agents are launched by the controller, so the agent needs a reachable SSH server — simple and firewall-friendly if the controller can reach it. JNLP/inbound agents dial out to the controller, which is the right choice when the agent sits behind NAT or a firewall. Both carry the same remoting traffic.
Q7How many executors per agent?
Roughly one per vCPU, bounded by RAM (≈2 GB per concurrent build for typical Node/Python work), and validated by watching load average and build duration. Over-subscribing makes every build slower and increases flakiness from resource contention.
Q8How do you keep agent toolchains consistent?
I don't rely on whatever happens to be installed: either the pipeline declares agent { docker { image 'node:20.17-alpine3.20' } } so the toolchain is pinned per stage, or the agent image is built by Packer/Ansible and version-controlled. Ad-hoc installs on long-lived agents always drift.
Q9What happens to running builds when an agent dies?
The remoting channel breaks, the build is marked as failed (or retried if you configured agent-loss retry conditions), and the queue reassigns waiting builds to another agent with the same label. With durable pipelines the state is preserved up to the last checkpoint.
Q10How would you autoscale Jenkins?
Kubernetes agents (one pod per build) or a cloud plugin (EC2, Azure VM agents) that provisions on demand and terminates idle agents. I'd set idle-retention to a few minutes, cap the maximum instances to control cost, and keep one small always-on agent for fast feedback.

pipeline {

        kubernetes {
