    tty: true
    resources:
      requests: { cpu: "500m", memory: "1Gi" }
      limits:   { cpu: "2",    memory: "2Gi" }
  - name: dind
    image: docker:24-dind
    securityContext: { privileged: true }
'''
        }
    }
    stages {
        stage('Test')  { steps { container('node') { sh 'npm ci && npm test' } } }
        stage('Build') { steps { container('dind') { sh 'docker build -t app:$GIT_SHA .' } } }
    }
}
💡Why this matters at scale Every build gets a clean, disposable environment — no workspace drift, no "someone installed something weird on agent 3". The cost is pod start-up latency (typically 5–20 s) and the need to cache dependencies in a PVC or registry.

13.14 More interview Q&A

Q6SSH agent vs JNLP agent?
SSH agents are launched by the controller, so the agent needs a reachable SSH server — simple and firewall-friendly if the controller can reach it. JNLP/inbound agents dial out to the controller, which is the right choice when the agent sits behind NAT or a firewall. Both carry the same remoting traffic.
Q7How many executors per agent?
Roughly one per vCPU, bounded by RAM (≈2 GB per concurrent build for typical Node/Python work), and validated by watching load average and build duration. Over-subscribing makes every build slower and increases flakiness from resource contention.
Q8How do you keep agent toolchains consistent?
I don't rely on whatever happens to be installed: either the pipeline declares agent { docker { image 'node:20.17-alpine3.20' } } so the toolchain is pinned per stage, or the agent image is built by Packer/Ansible and version-controlled. Ad-hoc installs on long-lived agents always drift.
Q9What happens to running builds when an agent dies?
The remoting channel breaks, the build is marked as failed (or retried if you configured agent-loss retry conditions), and the queue reassigns waiting builds to another agent with the same label. With durable pipelines the state is preserved up to the last checkpoint.
Q10How would you autoscale Jenkins?
Kubernetes agents (one pod per build) or a cloud plugin (EC2, Azure VM agents) that provisions on demand and terminates idle agents. I'd set idle-retention to a few minutes, cap the maximum instances to control cost, and keep one small always-on agent for fast feedback.
14
Block 14 · Real engineering

Shared Libraries — stop repeating yourself

Goal: move duplicated pipeline logic into one versioned, testable library that both apps use. This is the single biggest signal of a senior Jenkins engineer.

14.1 The problem and the fix

By now nodejs-app/Jenkinsfile and python-app/Jenkinsfile are 90% identical: checkout, build, test, docker build, push, notify. If you need to change the tag strategy you must edit every file — and you will forget one. A shared library is a Git repo of Groovy code that pipelines import.

treejenkins-shared-library/ (its own Git repo)
jenkins-shared-library/
├── vars/                       <-- every file becomes a GLOBAL STEP by filename
│   ├── buildAndPushImage.groovy      -> step: buildAndPushImage(...)
│   ├── runTests.groovy               -> step: runTests(...)
│   ├── notifyBuild.groovy            -> step: notifyBuild(...)
│   └── standardPipeline.groovy       -> step: standardPipeline { ... }   (full template)
├── src/
│   └── com/example/ci/
│       └── Docker.groovy       <-- real Groovy classes: import com.example.ci.Docker
└── resources/
    └── scripts/deploy.sh       <-- non-Groovy files, loaded with libraryResource
DirectoryBecomesUse for
vars/A global step named after the file90% of your library — pipeline-facing steps
src/Regular Groovy classes you importComplex logic, parsing, API clients
resources/Files loaded with libraryResourceShell scripts, JSON templates, config

14.2 Registering the library

Manage Jenkins → System → Global Pipeline Libraries → Add:

uiGlobal Pipeline Library configuration
Name              : jenkins-shared-library     <-- the name pipelines reference
Default version   : main                        <-- branch/tag (@main, @v1.2.0)
Load implicitly   : ☐  (leave OFF: pipelines opt in explicitly)
Allow default version to be overridden : ☑
Include @Library changes in job recent changes : ☑

Retrieval method  : Modern SCM
  Source Code Management : Git
    Project Repository   : git@github.com:<you>/jenkins-shared-library.git
    Credentials          : github-ssh

14.3 Writing your first steps

groovyvars/notifyBuild.groovy — one file = one step
// vars/notifyBuild.groovy
// Called from a pipeline as:  notifyBuild('SUCCESS')
def call(String buildStatus = 'STARTED') {
    def color  = buildStatus == 'SUCCESS'  ? 'good'    :
                 buildStatus == 'UNSTABLE' ? 'warning' :
                 buildStatus == 'FAILURE'  ? 'danger'  : 'good'

    def subject = "${buildStatus}: ${env.JOB_NAME} #${env.BUILD_NUMBER}"
    def summary = "${subject}\nCommit: ${env.GIT_COMMIT ?: 'n/a'}\nMore: ${env.BUILD_URL}"

    echo summary                          // always visible in the console

    // Send to Slack only if the plugin + credential exist (safe on any controller)
    try {
        withCredentials([string(credentialsId: 'slack-token', variable: 'SLACK_TOKEN')]) {
            slackSend channel: '#ci', color: color, message: summary, tokenCredentialId: 'slack-bot'
        }
    } catch (err) {
        echo "Slack notification skipped: ${err.message}"
    }
}
groovyvars/buildAndPushImage.groovy
// vars/buildAndPushImage.groovy
// Usage:  buildAndPushImage(image: 'user/app', tag: env.GIT_SHA, context: 'nodejs-app')
def call(Map config = [:]) {
    def image   = config.image   ?: error('buildAndPushImage: `image` is required')
    def tag     = config.tag     ?: env.GIT_SHA ?: env.BUILD_NUMBER
    def context = config.context ?: '.'
    def pushLatest = config.get('pushLatest', false)
    def extraTags  = config.get('extraTags', [])

    dir(context) {
        def img = docker.build("${image}:${tag}", '--pull .')

        docker.withRegistry('https://index.docker.io/v1/', 'dockerhub-creds') {
            img.push(tag)
            extraTags.each { t -> img.push(t) }
            if (pushLatest) { img.push('latest') }
        }

        def digest = sh(script: "docker inspect --format='{{index .RepoDigests 0}}' ${image}:${tag}",
                        returnStdout: true).trim()
        echo "Pushed ${digest}"
        return "${image}:${tag}"
    }
}
groovyvars/runTests.groovy — one step for both ecosystems
// vars/runTests.groovy
// Usage:  runTests(type: 'node', minCoverage: 70)
//         runTests(type: 'python', minCoverage: 80)
def call(Map config = [:]) {
    def type = config.type ?: error('runTests: `type` must be "node" or "python"')
    def min  = config.get('minCoverage', 70)

    try {
        if (type == 'node') {
            sh label: 'jest', script: 'npm run test:junit'
        } else if (type == 'python') {
            sh label: 'pytest', script: ". venv/bin/activate && pytest --cov-fail-under=${min}"
        } else {
            error "Unknown project type: ${type}"
        }
    } finally {
        // always publish, even if the tests failed -- that's the whole point
        junit testResults: 'reports/junit.xml', allowEmptyResults: true
    }
}

14.4 The big one: a full pipeline template

groovyvars/standardPipeline.groovy — a declarative wrapper
// vars/standardPipeline.groovy
// A "template method": the library owns the shape, each app fills in the details.
//
// Usage from a Jenkinsfile:
//   standardPipeline {
//       type      = 'node'            // or 'python'
//       image     = 'user/nodejs-app'
//       context   = 'nodejs-app'
//       minCoverage = 70
//   }
def call(Map config, Closure body = null) {
    def type    = config.type ?: 'node'
    def image   = config.image ?: error('standardPipeline: `image` required')
    def context = config.context ?: '.'
    def min     = config.get('minCoverage', 70)
    def agent   = type == 'node' ? 'node:20-alpine' : 'python:3.12-slim'

    pipeline {
        agent none

        options {
            timestamps()
            timeout(time: 40, unit: 'MINUTES')
            buildDiscarder(logRotator(numToKeepStr: '50', artifactNumToKeepStr: '10'))
            disableConcurrentBuilds()
        }

        stages {
            stage('Checkout') {
                agent { label 'linux' }
                steps {
                    script {
                        checkout scm
                        env.GIT_SHA = sh(script: 'git rev-parse --short HEAD', returnStdout: true).trim()
                        currentBuild.description = env.GIT_SHA
                    }
                }
            }

            stage('Build') {
                agent { docker { image "${agent}"; reuseNode true } }
                steps {
                    dir(context) {
                        sh type == 'node' ? 'npm ci'
                                          : 'python -m venv venv && . venv/bin/activate && pip install -r requirements-dev.txt'
                    }
                }
            }

            stage('Test') {
                agent { docker { image "${agent}"; reuseNode true } }
                steps {
                    dir(context) { runTests(type: type, minCoverage: min) }
                }
            }

            stage('Package & Push') {
                agent { label 'docker' }
                when { anyOf { branch 'main'; branch 'develop' } }
                steps {
                    buildAndPushImage(image: image, tag: env.GIT_SHA, context: context,
                                      pushLatest: (env.BRANCH_NAME == 'main'),
                                      extraTags: [env.BUILD_NUMBER])
                }
            }
        }

        post {
            always  { script { notifyBuild(currentBuild.currentResult) } }
            cleanup { node('linux') { cleanWs() } }
        }
    }
}
groovyThe Jenkinsfiles become tiny
// nodejs-app/Jenkinsfile
@Library('jenkins-shared-library@main') _
standardPipeline {
    type        = 'node'
    image       = '<user>/nodejs-app'
    context     = 'nodejs-app'
    minCoverage = 70
}

// python-app/Jenkinsfile
@Library('jenkins-shared-library@main') _
standardPipeline {
    type        = 'python'
    image       = '<user>/python-app'
    context     = 'python-app'
    minCoverage = 70
}
🧠Why the trailing underscore? @Library('name') _ is the correct syntax when you are not importing a class — the bare _ is a placeholder for "no import statement". Without it Groovy parses the annotation incorrectly. Also note: @Library must be on its own line above everything else (only comments may precede it).

14.5 Versioning & safety

PracticeWhy
Pin the version: @Library('lib@v1.4.0') _Branches move. A tag gives reproducible builds; use @main only in development.
Test library changes on a branch firstPoint one test pipeline at @feature-branch before merging to main.
Unit-test with JenkinsPipelineUnitA JUnit-based framework that runs your vars against a mocked Jenkins. Real teams do this.
Keep vars/ thinBusiness logic belongs in src/ classes, which are far easier to unit test.
Remember the trust boundaryLibrary code runs outside the Groovy sandbox — it can do anything on the controller. Review library PRs like production code, and restrict who can push to the library repo.

14.6 ✅ Checklist

  • Created the shared-library repo with vars/ and registered it in Jenkins.
  • Both Jenkinsfiles shrank to ~8 lines and still build, test and push.
  • Pinned a tag (@v1.0.0) and confirmed the pipeline uses it.
  • I can explain what @Library('x') _ does and why the underscore is there.

14.7 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1What is a Jenkins shared library and why use one?
A Git repository of Groovy code that pipelines import with @Library('name@version') _. Files in vars/ become global steps, src/ holds classes, and resources/ holds non-Groovy files. It removes duplication across dozens of Jenkinsfiles, so a change to tagging, notifications or scanning is made once, reviewed once, and adopted everywhere.
Q2How do you version shared libraries safely?
Tag releases and reference a tag (@v1.4.0) from production pipelines, so a change to main can't break them. New versions are validated by pointing a test pipeline at the branch first, and by unit tests with JenkinsPipelineUnit.
Q3What are the security implications?
Shared library code runs outside the Groovy sandbox with full controller privileges, so anyone who can merge to the library repo effectively has admin on Jenkins. I'd restrict write access, require code review, and keep secrets handling in the credentials store rather than in library code.
Q4How do you test a shared library?
With JenkinsPipelineUnit — a JUnit framework that mocks the Jenkins runtime so I can assert that steps were called with the right arguments. I keep logic in src/ classes where possible, because plain Groovy classes are much easier to unit test than pipeline scripts.

14.8 Under the hood — how a library is loaded

  1. Resolution. The library name (jenkins-shared-library) is looked up in Global Pipeline Libraries → the SCM is checked out to $JENKINS_HOME/workflow-libs/<name>/ at the requested version.
  2. Loading. Files in vars/ are compiled into global CPS-transformed scripts: vars/foo.groovy with a call() method becomes the step foo(...). Classes in src/ are loaded by the Groovy classloader and available via import.
  3. Sandbox. Library code runs outside the Groovy sandbox — it has full controller privileges. That's the security trade-off for its power.
  4. Caching. The checkout is cached per version; changing @main means new commits are pulled on the next run.
🚨Three consequences you must internalise
  • Library code can do anything — restrict who can merge to it.
  • @main means a bad merge breaks every consumer at once. Pin tags in production.
  • Steps defined in vars/ run in CPS: keep them thin and put heavy logic in src/.

14.9 Writing a library step properly

groovyvars/deployCompose.groovy — docs, defaults, validation, return value
// vars/deployCompose.groovy
//
// Deploy a compose stack over SSH.
//
//   deployCompose(host: 'staging.example.com',
//                 stackPath: '/opt/nodejs-app',
//                 image: 'user/nodejs-app',
//                 tag: env.GIT_SHA,
//                 port: 3000,
//                 healthPath: '/health')
//
def call(Map config) {
    // ---- 1. validate inputs loudly -------------------------------------
    ['host', 'stackPath', 'image', 'tag'].each { k ->
        if (!config[k]) { error "deployCompose: '${k}' is required" }
    }
    def port       = config.get('port', 3000)
    def healthPath = config.get('healthPath', '/health')
    def credId     = config.get('credentialsId', 'deploy-key')
    def user       = config.get('user', 'ubuntu')

    // ---- 2. do the work -------------------------------------------------
    sshagent(credentials: [credId]) {
        sh """
            ssh -o StrictHostKeyChecking=no ${user}@${config.host} '
                set -e
                cd ${config.stackPath}
                export IMAGE_REPO=${config.image} IMAGE_TAG=${config.tag}
                docker compose pull
                docker compose up -d --remove-orphans
                curl -fsS --retry 20 --retry-delay 3 http://localhost:${port}${healthPath}
            '
        """
    }

    // ---- 3. return something useful ------------------------------------
    return "${config.image}:${config.tag}@${config.host}"
}
groovysrc/com/example/ci/DeployTarget.groovy — pure logic, easy to test
package com.example.ci

/** Value object describing where an app can be deployed. */
class DeployTarget implements Serializable {
    String host
    String path
    String user = 'ubuntu'
    int    port
    String healthPath = '/health'

    String composeCommand(String repo, String tag) {
        "cd ${path} && IMAGE_REPO=${repo} IMAGE_TAG=${tag} docker compose up -d --remove-orphans"
    }

    String healthUrl() { "http://localhost:${port}${healthPath}" }

    static DeployTarget nodejs(String envName) {
        envName == 'prod'
            ? new DeployTarget(host: 'prod.example.com',   path: '/opt/nodejs-app', port: 3000)
            : new DeployTarget(host: 'staging.example.com', path: '/opt/nodejs-app', port: 3000)
    }
}

14.10 Unit testing the library (JenkinsPipelineUnit)

groovybuild.gradle
plugins { id 'groovy' }
repositories { mavenCentral() }
dependencies {
    testImplementation 'org.jenkins-ci.main:jenkins-core:2.452'
    testImplementation 'com.lesfurets:jenkins-pipeline-unit:1.14'
    testImplementation 'org.spockframework:spock-core:2.3-groovy-3.0'
}
groovytest/com/example/RunTestsSpec.groovy
package com.example

import com.lesfurets.jenkins.unit.BasePipelineTest
import org.junit.Before
import org.junit.Test

class RunTestsSpec extends BasePipelineTest {

    @Override @Before
    void setUp() {
        super.setUp()
        binding.setVariable('env', [BRANCH_NAME: 'main', BUILD_NUMBER: '7'])
    }

    @Test void 'runs npm test for node projects'() {
        def script = loadScript('vars/runTests.groovy')
        script.call(type: 'node', minCoverage: 70)
        assert helper.callStack.findAll { it.methodName == 'sh' }
                .any { it.toString().contains('npm run test:junit') }
        printCallStack()
    }

    @Test void 'rejects an unknown project type'() {
        def script = loadScript('vars/runTests.groovy')
        try {
            script.call(type: 'ruby')
            assert false : 'should have thrown'
        } catch (Exception e) {
            assert e.message.contains('Unknown project type')
        }
    }
}
💡Worth ten minutes of your time Being able to say "our shared library has unit tests that run in CI" is a genuine differentiator. Very few Jenkins engineers do it, and interviewers notice immediately.

14.11 More interview Q&A

Q5How do you structure a shared library for a large organisation?
vars/ for the thin, stable, documented step API; src/ for the real logic as testable classes; resources/ for scripts and templates. Semantic-versioned releases pinned by consumers, unit tests with JenkinsPipelineUnit, and a CHANGELOG. Breaking changes go in a new major version — never silently on main.
Q6How do you roll out a breaking library change?
Add the new version alongside the old (a new step name or a new major tag), migrate a pilot team first, keep both working for one release cycle, then update everyone and remove the old path. Because consumers pin versions, the migration is a coordinated set of pull requests rather than an outage.
Q7Can a shared library call another shared library?
Yes — a library can depend on another via its own @Library annotation or through configured library dependencies. I keep the dependency graph shallow (one or two levels) because deep chains are hard to reason about and to version.
Q8Why does my library step not see env or sh?
Steps in vars/ run as CPS scripts that delegate to the surrounding pipeline, so they must be called from inside a pipeline context. Pure classes in src/ have no pipeline context at all — pass what they need as arguments, which is also exactly what makes them testable.
Q9How do you document a shared library?
A README.md in the library repo plus a Javadoc-style comment block at the top of every vars/ file showing usage and parameters. Jenkins also renders a sibling vars/<step>.txt as inline help in the Snippet Generator, so I add one for each step.

14.8 Under the hood — how a library is loaded

  1. Resolution. The library name (jenkins-shared-library) is looked up in Global Pipeline Libraries → the SCM is checked out to $JENKINS_HOME/workflow-libs/<name>/ at the requested version.
  2. Loading. Files in vars/ are compiled into global CPS-transformed scripts: vars/foo.groovy with a call() method becomes the step foo(...). Classes in src/ are loaded by the Groovy classloader and available via import.
  3. Sandbox. Library code runs outside the Groovy sandbox — it has full controller privileges. That's the security trade-off for its power.
  4. Caching. The checkout is cached per version; changing @main means new commits are pulled on the next run.
🚨Three consequences you must internalise
  • Library code can do anything — restrict who can merge to it.
  • @main means a bad merge breaks every consumer at once. Pin tags in production.
  • Steps defined in vars/ run in CPS: keep them thin and put heavy logic in src/.

15
Block 15 · Real engineering

Multibranch Pipelines

Goal: one job definition that automatically builds every branch and pull request — with the Jenkinsfile living in the branch, so CI evolves with the code.

15.1 What changes compared to a normal Pipeline job

Pipeline jobMultibranch Pipeline
JobsOne job, one branch (hard-coded)One folder, auto-created sub-job per branch/PR
BranchFixed in job configDiscovered by scanning the repo
JenkinsfileAny path you nameIn the repo root by default (configurable)
New branchSomeone creates a job by handAppears automatically on the next scan
Deleted branchJob lingers foreverRemoved by the orphaned-item strategy
PR validationManual setupBuilt-in (Branch Source plugin)

15.2 Creating one (GitHub Branch Source)

  1. Install GitHub Branch Source (github-branch-source).
  2. New Item → Multibranch Pipeline → name app-pipeline.
  3. Branch Sources → Add source → GitHub → Credentials: github-pat (needs a PAT, not SSH — it uses the API) → Repository HTTPS URL → Validate.
  4. Behaviours → add: Discover branches (all or "only branches that are also filed as PRs"), Discover pull requests from origin (merging the PR with the target branch), Filter by name (with wildcards) → main develop feature/*.
  5. Build Configuration → Mode: by Jenkinsfile → Script Path: nodejs-app/Jenkinsfile.
  6. Scan Repository Triggers → tick Periodically if not otherwise run (e.g. 1 hour) and/or Scan by webhook with a trigger token.
  7. Orphaned Item Strategy → tick Discard old items → keep 5 (stops deleted branches leaving zombie jobs).
  8. Save → Scan Repository Now → watch each branch become a job.
⚠️Script Path is per-multibranch-job, not per branch Since both apps live in one repo, create two multibranch jobs: nodejs-pipeline with Script Path nodejs-app/Jenkinsfile, and python-pipeline with python-app/Jenkinsfile. (A Jenkinsfile in the repo root is the common default; we keep them beside their app on purpose.)

15.3 Branch-aware pipeline logic

groovyenv.BRANCH_NAME · when { branch } · changeRequest
pipeline {
    agent none

    stages {
        stage('Info') {
            agent { label 'linux' }
            steps {
                echo "Branch      : ${env.BRANCH_NAME}"     // 'main', 'feature/login', 'PR-42'
                echo "Is PR build : ${env.CHANGE_ID ? 'yes' : 'no'}"
                echo "PR target   : ${env.CHANGE_TARGET ?: 'n/a'}"
                echo "Job name    : ${env.JOB_NAME}"        // 'app-pipeline/feature%2Flogin'
            }
        }

        stage('Test') { /* always runs */ }

        stage('Push image') {
            when {
                anyOf {
                    branch 'main'
                    branch 'develop'
                }
            }
            steps { echo 'pushing image' }
        }

        stage('Deploy to prod') {
            when {
                allOf {
                    branch 'main'
                    not { changeRequest() }          // never deploy from a PR build
                }
            }
            steps { echo 'deploying' }
        }

        stage('PR feedback') {
            when { changeRequest() }                 // only for pull requests
            steps {
                echo "Validating PR #${env.CHANGE_ID} targeting ${env.CHANGE_TARGET}"
                // Block 20: post the result back to the PR
            }
        }
    }
}
VariableBranch buildPR build
env.BRANCH_NAMEfeature/loginPR-42
env.CHANGE_IDunset42
env.CHANGE_TARGETunsetmain
env.CHANGE_URLunsetPR URL

15.4 Automating the scan (webhook for multibranch)

bashGitHub webhook for branch indexing
# GitHub -> Settings -> Webhooks:
#   Payload URL : http://<JENKINS>:8080/github-webhook/
#   Content type: application/json
#   Events      : Push, Pull request          <-- PR needed for PR builds

# In the multibranch job: Scan Repository Triggers -> "Scan by webhook"
#   Trigger token: SCAN_ME   (or let the plugin generate one)

# Test the scan endpoint manually:
curl -X POST -u user:$TOKEN \
  "http://<JENKINS>:8080/job/nodejs-pipeline/build?token=SCAN_ME"
🔁Indexing vs building A scan (indexing) asks GitHub "which branches/PRs exist?" and creates/updates/removes sub-jobs. A build runs a Jenkinsfile. A push webhook triggers both: scan first, then build the affected branch.

15.5 Organization folders (enterprise scale)

New Item → GitHub Organization → point it at your GitHub org → Jenkins scans every repository, creates a multibranch job for each repo containing a Jenkinsfile, and keeps them in sync automatically. One configuration, hundreds of repos, zero manual job creation. This is what platform teams actually run.

15.6 ✅ Checklist

  • Two multibranch jobs exist and discover your branches.
  • Created a feature/test-ci branch, pushed, and saw a job appear automatically.
  • Deleted that branch, re-scanned, and watched the job disappear (orphaned strategy).
  • A PR build ran and printed env.CHANGE_ID.
  • The Push image stage is skipped on feature branches and runs on main.

15.7 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1What is a Multibranch Pipeline?
A job that scans a repository and automatically creates a sub-pipeline for each branch or pull request that contains a Jenkinsfile. Branch discovery, PR validation and cleanup of deleted branches are handled by the Branch Source plugin instead of by humans creating jobs.
Q2How do you run different steps on different branches?
With when { branch 'main' }, when { changeRequest() } and the environment variables BRANCH_NAME, CHANGE_ID and CHANGE_TARGET. Typically: tests on every branch, image push on main/develop, deployment only from main and never from a PR build.
Q3How do you stop deleted branches leaving dead jobs?
Configure the multibranch job's Orphaned Item Strategy to discard old items after N days (or immediately). Otherwise every short-lived feature branch leaves a job behind forever.
Q4Why does Multibranch need checkout scm instead of a hard-coded branch?
Because Jenkins injects the SCM configuration for whichever branch it is currently building. If I hard-code main in the Jenkinsfile, every branch build would check out main — so the feature branch would never actually be tested.
Q5Scanning found no branches — what do you check?
The credentials (Multibranch uses the GitHub API, so it needs a PAT with repo scope), the repository URL, the behaviours (is branch discovery actually enabled?), and the Script Path — if the Jenkinsfile isn't at the configured path, the branch is detected but not built.

15.8 Under the hood — scanning and lightweight checkout

A Multibranch job is a folder that holds child jobs. The scan (indexing) does three things:

  1. Enumerate branches and pull requests via the Git provider API — which is why it needs a personal access token, not just an SSH key.
  2. Lightweight checkout — fetch each candidate's Jenkinsfile straight from the API, with no workspace and no executor, just to see whether the branch is buildable.
  3. Reconcile — create a child job per qualifying branch, update existing ones, and apply the orphaned-item strategy to remove dead ones.
⚠️Lightweight checkout has a consequence Because the Jenkinsfile is read via the API, its environment is not a full workspace. Anything that needs a real clone during evaluation — for example loading another file from the repo — fails at scan time. If you need that, disable lightweight checkout in the job's Branch Source behaviours.

15.9 Behaviours reference (Branch Source)

BehaviourEffectUse it when
Discover branchesFind branches by strategy: all, excluding those filed as PRs, or only those filed as PRsAlways — pick "exclude PRs" if PRs are built separately
Discover pull requests from originBuild PR-<n> for open PRs, with a merge strategyYou want pre-merge validation
Discover pull requests from forksBuild fork PRs, with a trust policyOpen-source or inner-source repos
Filter by name (wildcards)Include/exclude by pattern, e.g. main develop feature/* release/*Keep the folder readable
Check out to matching local branchReal local branch instead of detached HEADPipelines that push tags back
Clean after / before checkoutgit clean -fdx behaviour per branchStale artifacts causing mystery failures
Orphaned item strategyDelete child jobs for deleted branches, now or after N daysAlways — otherwise zombie jobs accumulate

15.10 Lab — the full branch lifecycle

🧪Lab 15.1 · Branch in, branch out
bashDo this end to end and watch the folder change
git switch -c feature/multibranch-demo
echo "# demo" >> README.md
git commit -am "demo: multibranch branch"
git push -u origin feature/multibranch-demo

# In Jenkins: nodejs-pipeline -> Scan Repository Now
#   -> a child job "feature%2Fmultibranch-demo" appears and builds

git switch main
git branch -d feature/multibranch-demo
git push origin --delete feature/multibranch-demo

# Scan again -> the child job is removed (orphaned item strategy)
# Check: job -> Scan Repository Log  (shows exactly what was added/removed)
Then: open a pull request that changes only python-app/ and confirm the Node pipeline still runs — it should, because we don't filter by path. Now add a name filter or a path-based when condition and see the difference.

15.11 Jenkinsfile patterns for branches and PRs

groovyA complete branch-aware policy
pipeline {
    agent none

    options {
        // keep PR builds short-lived; keep release branches longer
        buildDiscarder(logRotator(numToKeepStr: '50'))
        timeout(time: 45, unit: 'MINUTES')
    }

    stages {
        stage('Build')  { agent { label 'linux' };  steps { sh './build.sh' } }

        stage('Test')   { agent { label 'linux' };  steps { sh './test.sh' } }

        stage('Full security scans') {
            when { not { changeRequest() } }      // full scans on branches, quick ones on PRs
            steps { sh './scan.sh --full' }
        }

        stage('Push image') {
            when { anyOf { branch 'main'; branch 'develop'; branch 'release/*' } }
            steps { sh './push.sh' }
        }

        stage('Deploy') {
            when {
                allOf {
                    branch 'main'
                    not { changeRequest() }
                }
            }
            steps { sh './deploy.sh' }
        }
    }

    post {
        always {
            script {
                // make the folder readable at a glance
                currentBuild.description = changeRequest()
                    ? "PR-${env.CHANGE_ID} -> ${env.CHANGE_TARGET}"
                    : "${env.BRANCH_NAME} - ${env.GIT_SHA}"
            }
        }
    }
}
📌Noteoptions { } is evaluated before the pipeline body, so calling changeRequest() inside it works only in recent Jenkins versions. If yours complains, move the conditional into the stages as shown above.

15.12 More interview Q&A

Q6How do you build pull requests safely?
Enable PR discovery with a trust policy: PRs from forks don't get credentials or write access, and I keep a separate, restricted credential scope for untrusted builds. PR builds run tests and scans but never deploy and never push images.
Q7What is the orphaned item strategy?
The policy for child jobs whose branch no longer exists — discard immediately or after N days, optionally keeping build records. Without it, every short-lived feature branch leaves a permanent job behind.
Q8How do you report CI status back to a pull request?
The GitHub Branch Source plugin reports build status to the PR automatically when the job is configured for it, and I add a post step to comment a summary. That status check is what lets branch protection require a green build before merge.
Q9Scanning is slow on a repo with 500 branches. What now?
Filter by name so only active prefixes are discovered, increase the scan interval, prefer webhook-driven scanning over periodic scans, and prune stale branches in the repository itself. Scans cost API calls, so they are also subject to provider rate limits.
Q10How do you deploy a preview environment per pull request?
Add a stage gated on when { changeRequest() } that deploys to a namespace or subdomain derived from CHANGE_ID, then tears it down in post { cleanup } or when the PR closes. Preview environments are the highest-value thing Multibranch enables.

15.8 Under the hood — scanning and lightweight checkout

A Multibranch job is a folder that holds child jobs. The scan (indexing) does three things:

  1. Enumerate branches and pull requests via the Git provider API — which is why it needs a personal access token, not just an SSH key.
  2. Lightweight checkout — fetch each candidate's Jenkinsfile straight from the API, with no workspace and no executor, just to see whether the branch is buildable.
  3. Reconcile — create a child job per qualifying branch, update existing ones, and apply the orphaned-item strategy to remove dead ones.
⚠️Lightweight checkout has a consequence Because the Jenkinsfile is read via the API, its environment is not a full workspace. Anything that needs a real clone during evaluation — for example loading another file from the repo — fails at scan time. If you need that, disable lightweight checkout in the job's Branch Source behaviours.

16
Block 16 · DevSecOps & scale

DevSecOps part 1 — SAST with SonarQube

Goal: automatically analyse code quality and security on every commit, and have a quality gate decide whether the build may proceed. This is the block that turns a CI engineer into a DevSecOps engineer.

16.1 What SAST actually is

🔬 SAST — Static Application Security Testing

Reads your source code without running it. Finds SQL injection patterns, hardcoded secrets, null dereferences, and code smells. Runs in seconds, early in CI.

🎯 Quality Gate

A pass/fail policy: "coverage ≥ 70%", "zero blocker issues", "duplication < 3%". The gate is what makes scanning matter — a report nobody enforces is decoration.

🧭Where the pieces sit in the pipeline SAST (Block 16) → source code · SCA (Block 17) → your dependencies · Container + DAST (Block 18) → the built image and the running app. Together they cover the three places bugs and vulnerabilities hide.

16.2 Run SonarQube locally (or on the EC2 box)

yamljenkins/sonarqube/docker-compose.yml
version: "3.8"
services:
  sonarqube:
    image: sonarqube:lts-community
    container_name: sonarqube
    restart: unless-stopped
    ports:
      - "9000:9000"
    environment:
      SONAR_ES_BOOTSTRAP_CHECKS_DISABLE: "true"
    volumes:
      - sonarqube_data:/opt/sonarqube/data
      - sonarqube_logs:/opt/sonarqube/logs
      - sonarqube_extensions:/opt/sonarqube/extensions
    ulimits:
      nofile: { soft: 65536, hard: 65536 }
volumes:
  sonarqube_data:
  sonarqube_logs:
  sonarqube_extensions:
bashStart it and do the first-time setup
cd jenkins/sonarqube
docker compose up -d
docker compose logs -f sonarqube        # wait for "SonarQube is operational"

# Browser: http://<EC2-IP>:9000   ->  login admin / admin  ->  CHANGE THE PASSWORD
  1. Create a project in SonarQube: Projects → Create Project → Manually → project key must match sonar.projectKey in your sonar-project.properties → nodejs-app / python-app.
  2. Generate a token: My Account → Security → name jenkins → Generate. Copy it once.
  3. Store it in Jenkins: Credentials → Secret text → ID sonar-token.
  4. Configure the server: Manage Jenkins → System → SonarQube servers → Add: Name sonar, Server URL http://<EC2-IP>:9000, Server authentication token → sonar-token.
  5. Configure the scanner tool: Manage Jenkins → Tools → SonarQube Scanner installations → Add sonar-scanner → Install automatically (latest).

16.3 The Jenkins pipeline steps

groovywithSonarQubeEnv + waitForQualityGate
stage('SonarQube Analysis') {
    agent { label 'linux' }
    steps {
        withSonarQubeEnv('sonar') {          // injects SONAR_HOST_URL + SONAR_AUTH_TOKEN
            sh '''
                sonar-scanner \
                  -Dsonar.projectKey=nodejs-app \
                  -Dsonar.sources=src \
                  -Dsonar.tests=tests \
                  -Dsonar.javascript.lcov.reportPaths=coverage/lcov.info
            '''
        }
    }
}

stage('Quality Gate') {
    agent { label 'linux' }
    steps {
        // SonarQube analyses asynchronously -> wait for the webhook callback
        timeout(time: 5, unit: 'MINUTES') {
            script {
                def qg = waitForQualityGate()
                echo "Quality gate status: ${qg.status}"
                if (qg.status != 'OK') {
                    error "SonarQube quality gate failed: ${qg.status}"
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
🚨The #1 SonarQube-in-Jenkins mistake waitForQualityGate() waits for SonarQube to call Jenkins back. If you don't create that webhook, the step hangs until your timeout expires. Add it in SonarQube:
uiSonarQube → Administration → Configuration → Webhooks
Name : jenkins
URL  : http://<JENKINS-IP>:8080/sonarqube-webhook/
Note the path is /sonarqube-webhook/ (provided by the SonarQube Scanner plugin), not /github-webhook/.

16.4 The config file both apps already have

propertiesnodejs-app/sonar-project.properties
sonar.projectKey=nodejs-app
sonar.projectName=nodejs-app
sonar.projectVersion=1.0.0

sonar.sources=src
sonar.tests=tests
sonar.sourceEncoding=UTF-8

# coverage produced by `npm run test:junit`
sonar.javascript.lcov.reportPaths=coverage/lcov.info

sonar.exclusions=node_modules/**,coverage/**,reports/**
propertiespython-app/sonar-project.properties
sonar.projectKey=python-app
sonar.projectName=python-app
sonar.projectVersion=1.0.0

sonar.sources=app.py
sonar.tests=tests
sonar.sourceEncoding=UTF-8

sonar.python.coverage.reportPaths=coverage.xml
sonar.python.flake8.reportPaths=flake8-report.txt
sonar.python.bandit.reportPaths=bandit-report.json

sonar.exclusions=venv/**,.venv/**,htmlcov/**,reports/**

With this file present you can run the scanner with no arguments at all — it reads the properties automatically:

groovyShortest possible analysis step
withSonarQubeEnv('sonar') {
    sh 'sonar-scanner'
}

16.5 Fail fast — or warn only?

ApproachCodeUse when
Hard gateerror "gate failed"Release branches, mature codebase
Soft gatecatchError(buildResult:'UNSTABLE', stageResult:'FAILURE')Introducing scanning to a legacy repo — don't block the team on day one
Report onlyNo gate step, dashboard onlyInitial rollout / visibility phase
📈How to roll out scanning without being hated Week 1: report only, publish the dashboard. Week 2: soft gate (unstable) so people see yellow. Week 3+: hard gate on new code only — SonarQube's "new code" period means you never inherit a decade of technical debt as a CI blocker, you just refuse to add to it. That nuance is a strong interview answer.

16.6 ✅ Checklist

  • SonarQube running at :9000 and both projects created.
  • sonar-token credential + sonar server configured in Jenkins.
  • /sonarqube-webhook/ registered in SonarQube.
  • Both pipelines show a Quality Gate stage that passes.
  • I broke the gate on purpose (dropped coverage) and watched the build fail.

16.7 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1How do you integrate SonarQube with Jenkins?
Install the SonarQube Scanner plugin, configure the server and its token credential, and add a stage wrapping sonar-scanner in withSonarQubeEnv('sonar'). Then a second stage calls waitForQualityGate() inside a timeout, which pauses until SonarQube posts the gate result back to /sonarqube-webhook/. Non-OK status fails the build.
Q2Why does waitForQualityGate hang?
Because SonarQube analyses asynchronously and notifies Jenkins via a webhook. If the webhook isn't configured (or Jenkins isn't reachable from SonarQube), the step waits until the timeout. Wrapping it in timeout() turns that into a clean failure instead of a stuck build.
Q3How do you introduce quality gates to a legacy codebase with thousands of issues?
Apply the gate to new code only — SonarQube's new-code period. Existing debt stays visible on the dashboard but doesn't block delivery, while every new line must be clean. Debt then shrinks naturally instead of blocking the team.
Q4SAST vs SCA vs DAST?
SAST analyses my own source without executing it (SonarQube, Bandit, ESLint security rules). SCA analyses third-party dependencies for known CVEs (npm audit, pip-audit, OWASP Dependency-Check, Trivy fs). DAST attacks the running application over HTTP (OWASP ZAP) and finds runtime issues like missing security headers or misconfigured cookies.

16.8 Under the hood — the analysis round trip

Because analysis happens on the SonarQube server, waitForQualityGate() must block until the callback arrives. If the webhook cannot reach Jenkins — wrong URL, firewall, self-signed certificate — the step waits for your timeout and then fails. That is why the timeout matters as much as the webhook itself.

16.9 What SonarQube actually measures

MetricWhat it isTypical gate
ReliabilityBugs — code that is demonstrably wrong0 new bugs
SecurityVulnerabilities (injection, weak crypto…)0 new vulnerabilities
Security HotspotsSecurity-relevant code needing human reviewReviewed, not necessarily fixed
MaintainabilityCode smells + technical debt ratioDebt ratio < 5%
CoverageLine/branch coverage from your reports≥ 70% on new code
DuplicationCopied blocks< 3% on new code
propertiesUseful extra SonarQube properties
# Analyse only the code you touched (great for legacy repos)
sonar.newCode.referenceBranch=main

# Monorepo: one project per app, each with its own key
sonar.projectKey=nodejs-app

# Exclude generated / third-party code
sonar.exclusions=**/node_modules/**,**/coverage/**,**/dist/**,**/*.min.js

# Treat tests as tests (affects coverage denominators)
sonar.tests=tests
sonar.test.inclusions=**/*.test.js,**/test_*.py

16.10 Lab — fail the gate on purpose

🧪Lab 16.1 · Prove the gate blocks 1. Add obvious problems to src/routes/items.js — an unused variable, an empty if, and a duplicated block. Push to main.
javascriptDeliberately bad code
router.get('/', (req, res) => {
  const unused = 'I am never read';           // code smell
  if (req.query.done === undefined) { }       // empty block -> maintainability issue
  const result = items;                        // duplication candidate
  const result2 = items;
  res.status(200).json({ count: result.length, items: result2 });
});
2. Watch the pipeline: SAST succeeds because the analysis was uploaded, then Quality Gate fails with ERROR.
3. Open the SonarQube project UI and read the issues — the pipeline links straight to it.
4. Revert, push, confirm green. You now know the gate really does block.

16.11 SonarQube vs SonarCloud vs alternatives

OptionHostingCostChoose when
SonarQube CommunitySelf-hosted (our docker-compose)FreeLearning, small teams — note: no branch/PR analysis in the free edition
SonarQube Developer+Self-hostedPaidYou need branch and PR analysis, portfolio views
SonarCloudSaaSFree for public reposOpen source, or you don't want to run a server
Semgrep / CodeQLCLI or SaaSFree tiersSecurity-rule-focused scanning and custom rules

16.12 More interview Q&A

Q5How is SonarQube different from a linter?
A linter catches style and simple bugs in one file. SonarQube maintains a project-wide model over time: cross-file duplication, coverage imported from your test reports, security hotspots, and — critically — a quality gate policy with trends and a new-code period, so you can enforce standards without inheriting all historical debt.
Q6Why analyse "new code" only?
Because applying today's standards to a ten-year-old codebase blocks delivery forever. The new-code period (since the last release, or a reference branch) means every change must be clean while existing debt stays visible and tracked separately — debt then shrinks naturally.
Q7Analysis succeeded but coverage shows 0% in SonarQube.
Either the report path is wrong, or the report was generated after the scanner ran. The order must be: tests first, producing coverage/lcov.info or coverage.xml, then sonar-scanner, with sonar.javascript.lcov.reportPaths or sonar.python.coverage.reportPaths pointing at the real file.
Q8How do you keep the SonarQube token safe?
It is stored as a secret-text credential in Jenkins and injected by withSonarQubeEnv, so it never appears in the Jenkinsfile or the console. I use a token per project or per team and rotate it on the same schedule as other CI credentials.
Q9How long should the quality gate wait?
Typically two to five minutes; I use timeout(time: 5, unit: 'MINUTES'). Very large projects can exceed that, in which case I raise the timeout rather than remove the gate — a silent pass is far worse than a slow one.

16.8 Under the hood — the analysis round trip

Because analysis happens on the SonarQube server, waitForQualityGate() must block until the callback arrives. If the webhook cannot reach Jenkins — wrong URL, firewall, self-signed certificate — the step waits for your timeout and then fails. That is why the timeout matters as much as the webhook itself.

MaintainabilityCode smells + technical debt ratioDebt ratio < 5%
CoverageLine/branch coverage from your reports≥ 70% on new code
DuplicationCopied blocks< 3% on new code

Q5How is SonarQube different from a linter?
A linter catches style and simple bugs in one file. SonarQube maintains a project-wide model over time: cross-file duplication, coverage imported from your test reports, security hotspots, and — critically — a quality gate policy with trends and a new-code period, so you can enforce standards without inheriting all historical debt.
Q6Why analyse "new code" only?
Because applying today's standards to a ten-year-old codebase blocks delivery forever. The new-code period (since the last release, or a reference branch) means every change must be clean while existing debt stays visible and tracked separately — debt then shrinks naturally.
Q7Analysis succeeded but coverage shows 0% in SonarQube.
Either the report path is wrong, or the report was generated after the scanner ran. The order must be: tests first, producing coverage/lcov.info or coverage.xml, then sonar-scanner, with sonar.javascript.lcov.reportPaths or sonar.python.coverage.reportPaths pointing at the real file.
Q8How do you keep the SonarQube token safe?
It is stored as a secret-text credential in Jenkins and injected by withSonarQubeEnv, so it never appears in the Jenkinsfile or the console. I use a token per project or per team and rotate it on the same schedule as other CI credentials.
Q9How long should the quality gate wait?
Typically two to five minutes; I use timeout(time: 5, unit: 'MINUTES'). Very large projects can exceed that, in which case I raise the timeout rather than remove the gate — a silent pass is far worse than a slow one.
17
Block 17 · DevSecOps & scale

DevSecOps part 2 — SCA, linting & secret scanning

Goal: catch vulnerable dependencies, insecure code patterns and leaked secrets before they are merged — the highest-value, lowest-effort security stage you can add.

17.1 Why dependencies are the real risk

Your code is a few thousand lines; your dependencies are a few hundred thousand. Most real-world breaches come from a vulnerable library you didn't write. SCA (Software Composition Analysis) compares your dependency tree against CVE databases.

17.2 The toolchain per ecosystem

Purpose🟩 Node.js🐍 Python🐳 Both
Dependency CVEsnpm audit, audit-cipip-audit, safetyTrivy fs, OWASP Dependency-Check
Static security linteslint-plugin-securitybanditSemgrep
Style / bug linteslintflake8, pylint—
Secret scanninggitleaks, Trivy fs --scanners secret, detect-secrets
Licence compliancelicense-checkerpip-licensesSyft + Grype

17.3 Installing the tools on the agent

bashOne-time install on the Jenkins agent (or bake into the agent image)
# Trivy (Aqua Security) - filesystem, container, IaC and secret scanning
sudo apt-get install -y wget apt-transport-https gnupg lsb-release
wget -qO - https://aquasecurity.github.io/trivy-repo/deb/public.key | sudo gpg --dearmor -o /usr/share/keyrings/trivy.gpg
echo "deb [signed-by=/usr/share/keyrings/trivy.gpg] https://aquasecurity.github.io/trivy-repo/deb $(lsb_release -sc) main" \
  | sudo tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/trivy.list
sudo apt-get update && sudo apt-get install -y trivy

# Gitleaks - secret scanning
GITLEAKS_VERSION=$(curl -s https://api.github.com/repos/gitleaks/gitleaks/releases/latest | jq -r .tag_name)
curl -sSfL "https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks/releases/download/${GITLEAKS_VERSION}/gitleaks_${GITLEAKS_VERSION#v}_linux_x64.tar.gz" \
  | sudo tar -xz -C /usr/local/bin gitleaks

# Python: tools come from requirements-dev.txt (pip-audit, bandit, flake8)
# Node:   npm audit is built in; add audit-ci with `npm i -g audit-ci`

trivy --version && gitleaks version

17.4 Pipeline stages for SCA (both apps)

groovyDependency + lint scanning for Node
stage('SCA · npm audit') {
    agent { docker { image 'node:20-alpine'; reuseNode true } }
    steps {
        dir('nodejs-app') {
            // JSON report for the record, then a blocking gate on high/critical
            sh '''
                npm audit --audit-level=high --json > npm-audit.json || true
                npm audit --audit-level=high            # non-zero exit => build fails
            '''
            archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'npm-audit.json', allowEmptyArchive: true
        }
    }
}

stage('Lint · ESLint') {
    agent { docker { image 'node:20-alpine'; reuseNode true } }
    steps {
        dir('nodejs-app') {
            // warnings-only: lint should not block a hotfix
            catchError(buildResult: 'UNSTABLE', stageResult: 'FAILURE') {
                sh 'npm run lint'
            }
        }
    }
}
groovyDependency + security + style scanning for Python
stage('SCA · pip-audit') {
    agent { docker { image 'python:3.12-slim'; reuseNode true } }
    steps {
        dir('python-app') {
            sh '''
                . venv/bin/activate
                pip-audit -r requirements.txt --format json --output pip-audit.json || true
                pip-audit -r requirements.txt                 # blocking gate
            '''
            archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'pip-audit.json', allowEmptyArchive: true
        }
    }
}

stage('SAST · Bandit') {
    agent { docker { image 'python:3.12-slim'; reuseNode true } }
    steps {
        dir('python-app') {
            sh '''
                . venv/bin/activate
                bandit -r app.py -f json -o bandit-report.json || true
                bandit -r app.py -ll            # -ll = fail on MEDIUM and above
            '''
            archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'bandit-report.json', allowEmptyArchive: true
        }
    }
}

stage('Lint · flake8') {
    agent { docker { image 'python:3.12-slim'; reuseNode true } }
    steps {
        dir('python-app') {
            catchError(buildResult: 'UNSTABLE', stageResult: 'FAILURE') {
                sh '. venv/bin/activate && flake8 app.py tests/'
            }
        }
    }
}
groovyTrivy filesystem + secret scan, and gitleaks
stage('SCA · Trivy filesystem') {
    agent { label 'linux' }
    steps {
        sh '''
            trivy fs --scanners vuln,secret \
                     --severity HIGH,CRITICAL \
                     --exit-code 1 \
                     --format table \
                     --output trivy-fs.txt . || true

            trivy fs --scanners vuln,secret \
                     --severity HIGH,CRITICAL \
                     --exit-code 1 \
                     --ignore-unfixed \
                     .                          # blocking gate
        '''
        archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'trivy-fs.txt', allowEmptyArchive: true
    }
}

stage('Secrets · gitleaks') {
    agent { label 'linux' }
    steps {
        sh '''
            # scan the whole history available in this clone
            gitleaks detect --source . --report-path gitleaks-report.json --redact || found=1
            if [ -n "${found:-}" ]; then
              echo "::SECRET DETECTED:: rotate it, then purge it from history"
              exit 1
            fi
        '''
    }
    post {
        always { archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'gitleaks-report.json', allowEmptyArchive: true }
    }
}

17.5 OWASP Dependency-Check (the enterprise option)

Common in regulated companies. Install the OWASP Dependency-Check plugin, configure the tool in Manage Jenkins → Tools, then:

groovyDependency-Check step + publisher
stage('OWASP Dependency-Check') {
    agent { label 'linux' }
    steps {
        dependencyCheck additionalArguments: '''
            --scan .
            --format HTML --format XML
            --out .
            --suppression suppression.xml
            --nvdApiKey $NVD_API_KEY
        ''', odcInstallation: 'dependency-check'

        dependencyCheckPublisher pattern: 'dependency-check-report.xml'
    }
}
💡Two practical notes Dependency-Check downloads the whole NVD database on first run (slow — run it nightly, not per-commit). Get a free NVD API key or downloads get throttled. Keep a suppression.xml for accepted risks, and always record why a finding is suppressed with an expiry date.

17.6 Publishing everything with Warnings NG

Install Warnings NG (warnings-ng) to trend issues across builds instead of dumping them into logs:

groovyrecordIssues — trend graphs for every scanner
post {
    always {
        recordIssues(
            enabledForFailure: true,
            tools: [
                eslint(pattern: 'nodejs-app/eslint-report.json'),
                flake8(pattern: 'python-app/flake8-report.txt'),
                pyLint(pattern: 'python-app/pylint-report.txt')
            ],
            qualityGates: [[threshold: 10, type: 'TOTAL', unstable: true]]
        )
    }
}

17.7 ✅ Checklist

  • Trivy and gitleaks installed on the agent.
  • npm audit / pip-audit stage runs and fails on HIGH/CRITICAL.
  • Bandit runs on the Python app; you can explain the B104 finding in app.py.
  • A secret I planted in a test file was caught by gitleaks.
  • Scan reports are archived and (ideally) trended with Warnings NG.

17.8 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1How do you scan dependencies in CI?
With SCA tools in a dedicated stage: npm audit --audit-level=high for Node, pip-audit for Python, plus Trivy in filesystem mode across the repo. They exit non-zero on high/critical CVEs, which fails the build. I archive the JSON report for the record and use a suppression file with justifications for accepted risks.
Q2How do you prevent secrets being committed?
Two layers: a pre-commit hook (gitleaks or detect-secrets) so it never leaves the developer's machine, and the same scan in CI as a blocking gate. If a secret is found I treat it as compromised — rotate first, then purge from history — because removing the commit does not un-leak it.
Q3Found a critical CVE in a transitive dependency with no fixed version. Now what?
Assess real exposure: is the vulnerable code path reachable from our usage? If yes, mitigate (upgrade the parent, patch, or remove the dependency); if no, record a documented, time-boxed suppression so the build stays green without hiding the risk. I'd also add an alert so we revisit it when a fix lands.
Q4What does Bandit check, and what's a finding you've seen?
Bandit is a Python AST-based SAST tool: hardcoded passwords, eval, weak crypto, shell injection, and binding to 0.0.0.0 (B104). In this repo it flags B104 in app.py where the dev server binds all interfaces — acceptable for a dev entrypoint because production runs gunicorn behind a proxy, so I'd suppress it with a comment explaining why.
Q5Reporting vs blocking — how do you choose?
Blocking gates for exploitable, high-confidence, fixable issues (critical CVEs, secrets, quality gate on new code). Reporting-only for advisory signals (style lint, low severity, informational) — those trend on a dashboard and can be promoted to gates later.

17.9 Under the hood — how SCA finds vulnerabilities

SCA tools build a dependency graph — your direct dependencies plus every transitive one — map each package to a PURL (package URL) or CPE identifier, and look it up in vulnerability databases (GitHub Advisory DB, OSV, NVD, npm and PyPI advisories). Three practical consequences follow:

  • Transitive dependencies matter most. You rarely chose the vulnerable package; a dependency of a dependency did. That is why "we only use three libraries" is not a security argument.
  • Accuracy depends on identification. Wrong identifiers produce false positives — which is what suppression files are for, always with a written reason and an expiry date.
  • Not every CVE is exploitable. A vulnerable function you never call is lower priority than one on a request path. Triage by reachability, not by severity alone.
bashSee the graph yourself
# Node: what depends on what
npm ls --all | head -40
npm ls <vulnerable-package>          # who pulls it in?
npm explain <vulnerable-package>

# Python
pip install pipdeptree
pipdeptree --reverse --packages flask

17.10 Tool comparison

ToolScopeSpeedOutputGate style
npm auditNode dependenciessecondstext / JSON--audit-level=high
pip-auditPython dependencies (OSV + PyPI)secondstext / JSONexit code
Trivy fsMulti-ecosystem + IaC + secretssecondstable / JSON / SARIF--exit-code 1 --severity
GrypeMulti-ecosystem + imagessecondstable / JSON--fail-on high
OWASP Dependency-CheckMulti (JVM-heavy)minutes — downloads the NVDHTML / XML / SARIFJenkins publisher thresholds
SnykMulti + licences + fix PRssecondsJSON / SARIFSaaS policy

17.11 Lab — find a real vulnerability

🧪Lab 17.1 · Add a vulnerable dependency, then gate it
bashDeliberately install something with a known advisory
# Node — install a package with a public advisory, e.g. an old lodash
npm install lodash@4.17.15 --save
npm audit --audit-level=high            # non-zero exit -> your gate fires
npm audit --json > npm-audit.json       # keep the evidence
npm uninstall lodash                    # clean up

# Python — same idea
pip install requests==2.19.1
pip-audit -r requirements.txt           # reports known CVEs
pip uninstall -y requests
Then add the SCA stage to the pipeline and confirm the build fails. Finally, add a suppression with a justification and watch it pass:
yaml.trivyignore (or Dependency-Check suppression.xml)
# .trivyignore — every entry needs an owner, a reason and an expiry
# Format: <vuln-id>  # reason  owner=@team  expires=YYYY-MM-DD
CVE-2020-8203  # transitive via old tooling, not on a request path  owner=@platform  expires=2026-06-30

17.12 Secret scanning in depth

bashgitleaks — configuration and pre-commit hook
# .gitleaks.toml — allow-list for known-safe strings
[extend]
useDefault = true

[[allowlist]]
description = "local test fixtures"
paths = ['''^tests/fixtures/.*''']

# .pre-commit-config.yaml
repos:
  - repo: https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks
    rev: v8.18.0
    hooks:
      - id: gitleaks
🚨If a secret reaches a remote branch, it is compromised Deleting the commit does not un-leak it — bots scrape public repositories continuously, often within seconds. The only correct response is: rotate first, then purge history, then add the prevention layer (pre-commit hook plus CI gate).

17.13 More interview Q&A

Q6Trivy vs OWASP Dependency-Check?
Trivy is a single fast binary covering dependencies, containers, IaC and secrets with no database bootstrap — ideal for pipelines. Dependency-Check is JVM-based, downloads the NVD (slow first run, needs an NVD API key) and is common in regulated enterprises with existing suppression files. I would use Trivy for speed and Dependency-Check where compliance requires it.
Q7How do you keep false positives from blocking delivery?
A version-controlled suppression file where every entry carries an owner, a reason and an expiry date, reviewed periodically. A suppression without justification is just a hidden risk.
Q8Should scans run on every commit?
Fast ones, yes — npm audit, pip-audit, Trivy filesystem and gitleaks all run in seconds. Slow or noisy scans (full Dependency-Check, active DAST) go nightly, with the fast subset blocking every commit.
Q9How do you report security findings to developers?
Publish machine-readable reports (SARIF or JSON) as build artifacts and trend them with Warnings NG, link the failing build in chat, and keep the finding next to the code that caused it. The goal is a thirty-second path from alert to offending line.
Q10What is SBOM-driven vulnerability management?
Generate an SBOM at build time and store it alongside the artifact. When a new CVE appears you query your SBOMs instead of re-scanning everything, so you know within minutes which services are affected — exactly the capability that was missing during Log4Shell.

17.9 Under the hood — how SCA finds vulnerabilities

SCA tools build a dependency graph — your direct dependencies plus every transitive one — map each package to a PURL (package URL) or CPE identifier, and look it up in vulnerability databases (GitHub Advisory DB, OSV, NVD, npm and PyPI advisories). Three practical consequences follow:

  • Transitive dependencies matter most. You rarely chose the vulnerable package; a dependency of a dependency did. That is why "we only use three libraries" is not a security argument.
  • Accuracy depends on identification. Wrong identifiers produce false positives — which is what suppression files are for, always with a written reason and an expiry date.
  • Not every CVE is exploitable. A vulnerable function you never call is lower priority than one on a request path. Triage by reachability, not by severity alone.

18
Block 18 · DevSecOps & scale

DevSecOps part 3 — image scanning, SBOM, signing & DAST

Goal: scan what you actually ship (the image), prove what's inside it (SBOM), sign it, and attack the running app (DAST). This completes the DevSecOps story end to end.

18.1 The full pipeline picture

18.2 Scanning the image (Trivy)

groovyImage vulnerability gate
stage('Scan image · Trivy') {
    agent { label 'docker' }
    steps {
        script {
            // 1. Always produce a readable report (never blocks)
            sh """
                trivy image --format table --output trivy-image.txt \
                            --severity HIGH,CRITICAL ${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA} || true
            """

            // 2. Blocking gate: fail on high/critical that HAVE a fix available
            sh """
                trivy image --exit-code 1 \
                            --severity HIGH,CRITICAL \
                            --ignore-unfixed \
                            ${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA}
            """

            // 3. Also fail on any misconfiguration in the Dockerfile
            sh "trivy config --exit-code 1 --severity HIGH,CRITICAL ${CONTEXT} || true"
        }
    }
    post {
        always {
            archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'trivy-image.txt', allowEmptyArchive: true
        }
    }
}
FlagWhy
--ignore-unfixedDon't fail on CVEs with no fix released yet — otherwise your pipeline is permanently red for things you cannot fix
--severity HIGH,CRITICALStart strict on the top two; add MEDIUM once the noise is manageable
--exit-code 1Turn the scan into a gate
trivy configScans Dockerfiles and IaC (Kubernetes YAML, Terraform) for misconfigurations

18.3 SBOM — prove what's inside

An SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) is a machine-readable inventory of everything in your image. When the next Log4Shell happens, you answer "are we affected?" in seconds instead of days.

groovyGenerate + attach an SBOM (Syft / CycloneDX)
stage('SBOM') {
    agent { label 'docker' }
    steps {
        sh """
            syft ${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA} -o cyclonedx-json=sbom.cdx.json
            # or, with Trivy alone:
            trivy image --format cyclonedx --output sbom.cdx.json ${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA}
        """
        archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'sbom.cdx.json', fingerprint: true
    }
}

stage('Sign image · cosign') {
    agent { label 'docker' }
    when { branch 'main' }
    steps {
        withCredentials([file(credentialsId: 'cosign-key', variable: 'COSIGN_KEY')]) {
            sh '''
                export COSIGN_PASSWORD=""
                cosign sign --key $COSIGN_KEY ${IMAGE}:${GIT_SHA}
            '''
        }
    }
}
🔐Verify on the other side Signing only matters if someone checks it. In Kubernetes: Kyverno or Sigstore policy-controller rejects any pod whose image isn't signed by your key. On a plain VM: cosign verify --key cosign.pub <image> before docker run.

18.4 DAST — attack the running app (OWASP ZAP)

groovyZAP baseline scan against the container we just built
stage('DAST · OWASP ZAP baseline') {
    agent { label 'docker' }
    steps {
        script {
            // 1. Run the app in a throwaway container
            sh "docker run -d --name app-${env.BUILD_NUMBER} -p 8099:3000 ${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA}"

            // 2. Wait for it to be ready
            retry(10) { sleep 3; sh 'curl -fsS http://localhost:8099/health || exit 1' }

            // 3. Attack it with ZAP (docker image, no install needed)
            sh """
                mkdir -p zap-reports && chmod 777 zap-reports
                docker run --rm --network host \
                  -v \$(pwd)/zap-reports:/zap/wrk:rw \
                  ghcr.io/zaproxy/zaproxy:stable \
                  zap-baseline.py -t http://localhost:8099 \
                                  -g gen.conf \
                                  -r zap-report.html \
                                  -w zap-report.md \
                                  -J zap-report.json || true
            """
        }
    }
    post {
        always {
            sh "docker rm -f app-${env.BUILD_NUMBER} || true"
            archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'zap-reports/**', allowEmptyArchive: true
        }
    }
}
⚠️Start with zap-baseline.py, not the full scan The baseline scan is passive and takes ~1 minute — safe for every commit. A full active scan (zap-full-scan.py) can take 30+ minutes and can be destructive; run it nightly against a staging environment, never against production.

18.5 Should security stages block the build?

ScanTimingGate
Secrets (gitleaks)Every commit🔴 Block always
SAST quality gate (new code)Every commit🔴 Block
SCA high/critical with fixEvery commit🔴 Block
Image scan high/critical fixableEvery build🔴 Block before push
Image scan, unfixed / mediumEvery build🟡 Report
ZAP baselineEvery commit🟡 Report (block later)
ZAP full scanNightly🟡 Report to security team
Lint / styleEvery commit🟡 Unstable at most

18.6 ✅ Checklist

  • Trivy image scan runs and blocks on fixable HIGH/CRITICAL.
  • An SBOM (sbom.cdx.json) is generated and archived per build.
  • ZAP baseline produces a report against the running container.
  • I know which scans block and which only report — and why.

18.7 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1Walk me through your DevSecOps pipeline.
Source stage: SonarQube SAST with a quality gate on new code. Dependency stage: npm audit / pip-audit plus Trivy filesystem and gitleaks for secrets. Build stage: multi-stage image, non-root, then Trivy image scan gating on fixable high/critical, an SBOM with Syft, and cosign signing on main. Runtime stage: the container is started, smoke-tested, and scanned with the OWASP ZAP baseline. Reports are archived and trended; only high-confidence, fixable issues block.
Q2What is an SBOM and why would a company want one?
A machine-readable inventory of every component in an artifact (CycloneDX or SPDX). It turns "are we affected by this CVE?" from a week of investigation into a query, and it's increasingly a compliance requirement (US executive orders, EU CRA, supply-chain questionnaires).
Q3Why sign container images?
To guarantee provenance: a signed image proves it was built by our pipeline and hasn't been tampered with between build and runtime. With cosign plus an admission controller like Kyverno, the cluster simply refuses to run unsigned images.
Q4How do you handle a critical CVE in a base image with no patched version yet?
First check exploitability — is the vulnerable package even invoked? Then reduce attack surface (slim/distroless base, remove the package), add a runtime control (WAF, seccomp, network policy), and record a time-boxed suppression with `--ignore-unfixed` so the build is not permanently red. When the upstream fix lands, the suppression expires and the gate re-arms.
Q5DAST in CI — how do you keep it fast?
Use the ZAP baseline (passive, ~1 minute) on every commit against a container started just for the scan, and reserve the full active scan for a nightly job against staging. Keep the scanner on the same network as the app with --network host so there's no external latency.

18.8 Under the hood — what an image scanner inspects

Trivy does not guess from image metadata; it unpacks the layers and reads the package databases inside the filesystem:

Source inside the imageEcosystem detected
/lib/apk/db/installedAlpine (apk)
/var/lib/dpkg/statusDebian / Ubuntu (dpkg)
/var/lib/rpmRHEL / CentOS (rpm)
**/node_modules/**/package.jsonNode (npm / yarn / pnpm)
**/*.dist-info/METADATAPython (pip)
**/Gemfile.lockRuby
**/go.mod and Go binary build infoGo
💡Why distroless images produce almost no findings There is no package manager database, so OS-level CVEs mostly vanish — what remains is your application dependencies (Node or Python), which the scanner still sees. That is a genuine security win, not a scanner blind spot.

18.9 Trivy in depth

bashA production-grade scan command
trivy image \
  --severity HIGH,CRITICAL \          # only what matters
  --ignore-unfixed \                  # no fix exists -> track, don't block
  --exit-code 1 \                     # gate
  --scanners vuln,secret \            # CVEs + embedded secrets
  --format table \                    # readable in the console
  --output trivy-image.txt \
  user/nodejs-app:abc1234

# Machine-readable for dashboards and the GitHub Security tab:
trivy image --format sarif     --output trivy.sarif     user/nodejs-app:abc1234
trivy image --format cyclonedx --output sbom.cdx.json   user/nodejs-app:abc1234

# Cache the database on the agent so scans start instantly
trivy image --cache-dir /var/lib/jenkins/.cache/trivy ...
FlagMeaning
--ignore-unfixedSkip CVEs with no released fix
--exit-code 1Turn the scan into a gate
--scanners vuln,secret,misconfigChoose what to look for
--pkg-types os,libraryOS packages and/or application dependencies
--ignore-policy trivy.policyRego-based, expiry-aware suppressions
--cache-dirPersist the vulnerability DB between builds

18.10 Signing and verification end to end

bashcosign: generate keys, sign, verify
# 1. Generate a key pair ONCE (store the private key as a Jenkins credential)
cosign generate-key-pair                     # -> cosign.key, cosign.pub

# 2. Sign the image DIGEST (never sign a mutable tag)
cosign sign --key cosign.key user/nodejs-app@sha256:9f8e7d...

# 3. Verify anywhere before running
cosign verify --key cosign.pub user/nodejs-app:abc1234

# 4. Keyless signing with OIDC (no long-lived key at all -- preferred)
cosign sign user/nodejs-app@sha256:9f8e7d...
cosign verify user/nodejs-app:abc1234 \
  --certificate-identity ci@example.com \
  --certificate-oidc-issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com
yamlKyverno policy — reject unsigned images in the cluster
apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata:
  name: verify-image-signature
spec:
  validationFailureAction: Enforce
  rules:
    - name: require-signature
      match:
        any:
          - resources: { kinds: ["Pod"] }
      verifyImages:
        - imageReferences: ["docker.io/*"]
          attestors:
            - entries:
                - keys:
                    publicKeys: |-
                      -----BEGIN PUBLIC KEY-----
                      MFkwEwYHKoZIzj0CAQYIKoZIzj0DAQcDQgAE...
                      -----END PUBLIC KEY-----

18.11 Lab — scan a deliberately insecure image

🧪Lab 18.1 · Watch the gate fire
bashBuild something old and vulnerable, then scan it
mkdir -p /tmp/lab18 && cd /tmp/lab18
cat > Dockerfile <<'EOF'
FROM node:14-alpine          # EOL Node with many known CVEs
RUN apk add --no-cache curl bash
USER root                     # running as root -> misconfiguration finding
CMD ["node", "-e", "console.log('hi')"]
EOF
docker build -t lab18/old-app:1 .
trivy image --severity HIGH,CRITICAL lab18/old-app:1        # lots of findings
trivy image --severity HIGH,CRITICAL --ignore-unfixed --exit-code 1 lab18/old-app:1
trivy config .                                              # flags USER root
Then repeat with FROM node:20-alpine and a non-root USER and watch the finding count collapse. That difference is the entire argument for multi-stage builds, current base images and non-root users.

18.12 DAST depth — what the ZAP baseline checks

CheckExample
Missing security headersContent-Security-Policy, X-Content-Type-Options, Strict-Transport-Security
Cookie flagsMissing Secure, HttpOnly, SameSite
Information disclosureStack traces, server version headers, directory listings
Insecure transportForms posting over HTTP, mixed content
Known vulnerable componentsOutdated JavaScript libraries (retire.js rules)
💡Two DAST practices that make it stick Run ZAP against a container started just for the scan (as in Block 18.4) so there is no shared state, and fail only on WARN severity or above once the baseline is clean — otherwise the first run floods you with informational noise and the team learns to ignore it.

18.13 More interview Q&A

Q6When should you scan: before or after pushing the image?
Scan before pushing so a vulnerable image never reaches the registry — that keeps the registry trustworthy and prevents accidental pulls. I still re-scan after push as a backstop, and schedule periodic rescans of stored images because new CVEs appear for images that were clean when built.
Q7Why rescan images that already passed?
Vulnerability databases change daily. An image that was clean in January can have a critical CVE in March without a single byte changing. Continuous rescanning of deployed digests is the only way to know your current exposure.
Q8What is a policy-as-code admission controller?
A Kubernetes component (Kyverno, OPA/Gatekeeper, Sigstore policy-controller) that evaluates rules before a pod is admitted — for example "image must be signed by our key", "no latest tags", "no privileged containers". It turns your pipeline's guarantees into enforced runtime policy.
Q9How do you convince management to invest in signing?
Frame it as supply-chain integrity: signing plus admission control means an attacker who compromises the registry or a developer laptop cannot get unsigned code into production. It is a small pipeline change that closes a whole class of attacks, and it is increasingly a compliance requirement.
Q10Which single security stage gives the best return?
Secret scanning with gitleaks — it takes seconds, has near-zero false positives, and prevents the most expensive incident class there is: a leaked credential. Then dependency scanning, because that is where the majority of real-world vulnerabilities live.

18.8 Under the hood — what an image scanner inspects

Trivy does not guess from image metadata; it unpacks the layers and reads the package databases inside the filesystem:

Source inside the imageEcosystem detected
/lib/apk/db/installedAlpine (apk)
/var/lib/dpkg/statusDebian / Ubuntu (dpkg)
/var/lib/rpmRHEL / CentOS (rpm)
**/node_modules/**/package.jsonNode (npm / yarn / pnpm)
**/*.dist-info/METADATAPython (pip)
**/Gemfile.lockRuby
**/go.mod and Go binary build infoGo
💡Why distroless images produce almost no findings There is no package manager database, so OS-level CVEs mostly vanish — what remains is your application dependencies (Node or Python), which the scanner still sees. That is a genuine security win, not a scanner blind spot.

18.9 Trivy in depth

bashA production-grade scan command
trivy image \
  --severity HIGH,CRITICAL \          # only what matters
  --ignore-unfixed \                  # no fix exists -> track, don't block
  --exit-code 1 \                     # gate
  --scanners vuln,secret \            # CVEs + embedded secrets
  --format table \                    # readable in the console
  --output trivy-image.txt \
  user/nodejs-app:abc1234

# Machine-readable for dashboards and the GitHub Security tab:
trivy image --format sarif     --output trivy.sarif     user/nodejs-app:abc1234
trivy image --format cyclonedx --output sbom.cdx.json   user/nodejs-app:abc1234

# Cache the database on the agent so scans start instantly
trivy image --cache-dir /var/lib/jenkins/.cache/trivy ...
FlagMeaning
--ignore-unfixedSkip CVEs with no released fix
--exit-code 1Turn the scan into a gate
--scanners vuln,secret,misconfigChoose what to look for
--pkg-types os,libraryOS packages and/or application dependencies
--ignore-policy trivy.policyRego-based, expiry-aware suppressions
--cache-dirPersist the vulnerability DB between builds
