19
Block 19 · DevSecOps & scale

Deploy stages — shipping the image

Goal: take the exact image you scanned and push it to a running environment, verify it, and be able to roll back in one click.

19.1 Deployment strategies (know these four)

♻️ Recreate

Stop old, start new. Simplest, has downtime. Fine for dev.

🔄 Rolling

Replace instances gradually. No downtime, both versions briefly live — your API must tolerate that.

🔵🟢 Blue-Green

Run the new stack beside the old, switch the proxy/router when healthy. Instant rollback by switching back.

🐤 Canary

Send 5–10% of traffic to the new version, watch metrics, then ramp to 100%. Best for risky changes.

19.2 The deploy target (Docker Compose on a VM)

yamljenkins/deploy/docker-compose.yml (lives on the target server)
version: "3.8"
services:
  nodejs-app:
    image: ${IMAGE_REPO}:${IMAGE_TAG}      # injected by the pipeline -- never edited by hand
    container_name: nodejs-app
    restart: unless-stopped
    ports:
      - "3000:3000"
    environment:
      PORT: 3000
      NODE_ENV: production
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD", "node", "-e", "require('http').get('http://127.0.0.1:3000/health',r=>process.exit(r.statusCode===200?0:1)).on('error',()=>process.exit(1))"]
      interval: 30s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 3
      start_period: 15s

  python-app:
    image: ${IMAGE_REPO}:${IMAGE_TAG}
    container_name: python-app
    restart: unless-stopped
    ports:
      - "5000:5000"
    environment:
      PORT: 5000
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD", "python", "-c", "import os,urllib.request;urllib.request.urlopen('http://127.0.0.1:'+os.environ['PORT']+'/health')"]
      interval: 30s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 3
      start_period: 15s
🎯The golden rule of deployment Deploy the same immutable artifact that passed tests and scans — referenced by digest or SHA tag, never rebuilt per environment. "Build once, deploy everywhere." Rebuilding for prod means prod is running code nobody tested.

19.3 Deploying over SSH

groovysshagent + docker compose pull/up + health check
stage('Deploy') {
    agent { label 'linux' }
    when { branch 'main' }
    steps {
        sshagent(credentials: ['deploy-key']) {          // SSH key credential, not a password
            sh """
                ssh -o StrictHostKeyChecking=no ${DEPLOY_USER}@${DEPLOY_HOST} <<'REMOTE'
                  set -e
                  cd ${DEPLOY_PATH}
                  export IMAGE_REPO=${IMAGE}
                  export IMAGE_TAG=${GIT_SHA}

                  docker compose pull                    # pull the exact scanned image
                  docker compose up -d --remove-orphans  # rolling replace
                  docker image prune -f

                  # wait for the healthcheck to go green
                  for i in \$(seq 1 30); do
                    if curl -fsS http://localhost:${APP_PORT}/health; then
                      echo "healthy after \${i}s"; exit 0
                    fi
                    sleep 2
                  done
                  echo "health check failed"; exit 1
REMOTE
            """
        }
    }
}
⚠️Two SSH gotchas
  • -o StrictHostKeyChecking=no skips host verification. Convenient, but it removes MITM protection — for production, pre-populate known_hosts for the deploy user instead.
  • The heredoc must be <<'REMOTE' (quoted) so the remote shell expands $IMAGE_TAG, not the local one. Unquoted heredocs are a classic source of "deployed the wrong tag".

19.4 Rollback

groovyOne-stage rollback using the previous successful build's tag
stage('Rollback on failure') {
    agent { label 'linux' }
    when { expression { currentBuild.currentResult == 'FAILURE' && params.AUTO_ROLLBACK } }
    steps {
        script {
            // lastSuccessfulBuild gives us the tag that was last known good
            def prev = currentBuild.previousSuccessfulBuild
            if (!prev) { error 'No previous successful build to roll back to' }
            def prevTag = prev.description?.trim() ?: prev.number.toString()
            echo "Rolling back to ${prevTag}"

            sshagent(credentials: ['deploy-key']) {
                sh """
                    ssh ${DEPLOY_USER}@${DEPLOY_HOST} \
                      "cd ${DEPLOY_PATH} && IMAGE_TAG=${prevTag} docker compose up -d --remove-orphans"
                """
            }
        }
        // let people know it happened
        currentBuild.description = "ROLLED BACK from ${env.GIT_SHA}"
    }
}

19.5 If you deploy to Kubernetes instead

groovyThe same stage, Kubernetes flavour
stage('Deploy to Kubernetes') {
    agent { label 'linux' }
    steps {
        withCredentials([file(credentialsId: 'kubeconfig', variable: 'KUBECONFIG')]) {
            sh """
                kubectl set image deployment/nodejs-app \
                        app=${IMAGE}:${GIT_SHA} --record -n ${params.ENV}
                kubectl rollout status deployment/nodejs-app -n ${params.ENV} --timeout=180s
            """
        }
    }
    post {
        failure {
            withCredentials([file(credentialsId: 'kubeconfig', variable: 'KUBECONFIG')]) {
                sh "kubectl rollout undo deployment/nodejs-app -n ${params.ENV}"
            }
        }
    }
}
💡Helm for real environments helm upgrade --install nodejs-app ./chart --set image.tag=${GIT_SHA} --namespace ${ENV} --atomic --timeout 5m — --atomic gives you automatic rollback on failure for free, which is why most teams prefer Helm over raw manifests.

19.6 ✅ Checklist

  • A deploy stage runs on main only and uses the SHA-tagged image.
  • The pipeline verifies /health after deployment and fails if it never becomes healthy.
  • Rollback path tested at least once (break the health check on purpose).
  • I can describe blue-green and canary, and when each is appropriate.

19.7 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1How does your pipeline deploy?
A deploy stage gated by when { branch 'main' } (and often an approval). It SSHes to the target with an SSH-agent credential, runs docker compose pull/up using the exact SHA tag that passed the scans, waits for the health endpoint, and on failure rolls back to the previous successful build's tag. The image is built once and promoted — never rebuilt per environment.
Q2How would you implement blue-green deployment in Jenkins?
Deploy the new version alongside the old on a different port or stack name, run health checks and smoke tests against it, then flip the load balancer or proxy target. Keep the old version running until the switch is verified, so rollback is just flipping back. In Kubernetes this is two deployments plus a service selector change; on VMs, two compose projects plus an nginx upstream swap.
Q3What's your rollback strategy?
Every deployment references an immutable tag, so rollback means redeploying the previous successful build's tag — I read it from currentBuild.previousSuccessfulBuild. On Kubernetes kubectl rollout undo or Helm's --atomic does it automatically. Crucially, database migrations must be backward compatible, otherwise rollback is impossible.
Q4Why build once and promote, instead of building per environment?
Building per environment means production runs an artifact that was never tested — a different dependency resolution, a different base image patch level. Promoting one immutable artifact makes "it worked in staging" meaningful and makes rollback deterministic.

19.8 Under the hood — the artifact lifecycle

⚠️Artifacts are not a package manager Jenkins stores artifacts on the controller's disk, forever, with no de-duplication. Using it as your long-term release store will fill $JENKINS_HOME and slow down backups. For real releases, push to a registry or artifact repository (Nexus, Artifactory, S3, GitHub Releases) and use Jenkins artifacts for build-scoped evidence only.
groovyFingerprints and cross-job artifact reuse
pipeline {
    agent { label 'linux' }
    options {
        // keep the controller healthy: only 20 builds' worth of artifacts
        buildDiscarder(logRotator(
            numToKeepStr: '40', artifactNumToKeepStr: '20'))
    }
    stages {
        stage('Build') {
            steps {
                sh 'npm ci && npm pack'
                archiveArtifacts(
                    artifacts: '*.tgz',
                    fingerprint: true,          // enable traceability
                    onlyIfSuccessful: true)
            }
        }
        stage('Promote') {
            when { branch 'main' }
            steps {
                // pull the exact artifact another job produced
                copyArtifacts(
                    projectName: 'nodejs-pipeline/main',
                    selector: lastSuccessful(),
                    filter: '*.tgz',
                    target: 'incoming/')
                sh 'ls -la incoming/'
            }
        }
    }
}

19.9 Stash vs archive vs external store

MechanismLives whereSurvives the build?CostUse for
stash/unstashCompressed on the controller, deleted with the buildNoVery cheapPassing files between stages/agents in one build
writeFile + workspaceAgent workspaceNoFreeTransient scratch files
archiveArtifacts$JENKINS_HOMEYesController diskReports, packages, evidence
Registry / Nexus / S3ExternalYesExternalRelease artifacts, images, long-term retention

19.10 Lab — promote a build through environments

🧪Lab 19.1 · Build once, promote the same artifact The discipline that separates real CI/CD from "rebuild per environment":
groovyOne artifact, three environments
pipeline {
    agent { label 'docker' }
    parameters {
        choice(name: 'TARGET_ENV', choices: ['staging', 'prod'], description: 'Where to deploy')
        string(name: 'BUILD_TO_PROMOTE', defaultValue: '', description: 'Empty = this build')
    }
    environment { REGISTRY = 'https://index.docker.io/v1/' }
    stages {
        stage('Resolve image') {
            steps {
                script {
                    if (params.BUILD_TO_PROMOTE?.trim()) {
                        copyArtifacts(
                            projectName: env.JOB_NAME,
                            selector: specific(params.BUILD_TO_PROMOTE),
                            filter: 'image-tag.txt')
                        env.IMAGE_TAG = readFile('image-tag.txt').trim()
                        echo "PROMOTING already-built image tag ${env.IMAGE_TAG}"
                    } else {
                        env.IMAGE_TAG = env.GIT_SHA
                        writeFile file: 'image-tag.txt', text: env.IMAGE_TAG
                        sh 'docker build -t $DOCKER_USER/app:$IMAGE_TAG .'
                        archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'image-tag.txt', fingerprint: true
                    }
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Deploy') { steps { sh "./deploy.sh ${params.TARGET_ENV} ${env.IMAGE_TAG}" } }
    }
    post { success { archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'deploy-*.log', allowEmptyArchive: true } }
}
Run it twice: once with an empty BUILD_TO_PROMOTE (builds and archives), then again with that build number (reuses the identical tag). Nothing is rebuilt — that is the whole point.

19.11 Retention policy that keeps the controller healthy

SettingRecommendedWhy
Builds to keep30–50Enough history for trends without unbounded growth
Artifacts to keep10–20Artifacts dominate disk usage
Keep builds with artifacts?Only for release jobsOtherwise it defeats the artifact limit
Days to keep90 for branches, forever for release pipelinesCompliance vs hygiene
groovyTiered retention: PR builds vs release builds
options {
    buildDiscarder(logRotator(
        numToKeepStr:         env.CHANGE_ID ? '10' : '50',   // PRs: short
        artifactNumToKeepStr: env.CHANGE_ID ? '2'  : '20',
        daysToKeepStr:        '90'))
}

19.12 More interview Q&A

Q6How do you promote the same artifact through environments?
Build once and record an immutable identifier (the git SHA or the image digest), then deploy that exact identifier to staging and production. Rebuilding per environment produces a different artifact, so what you tested is not what you shipped. Promotion reads the recorded identifier from the build's artifacts or from the registry.
Q7What is a fingerprint and why do you enable it?
A checksum Jenkins records for an archived file. It lets you answer "which build produced this file?" and "which builds consumed it?" — essential for traceability and for copyArtifacts relationships between upstream and downstream jobs.
Q8JENKINS_HOME is 90% full. What do you do?
Find the offenders with du -sh /var/lib/jenkins/jobs/* | sort -h, tighten the discard policies (especially artifact retention), clean old workspaces with cleanWs, and move real release artifacts to external storage. Then make it structural: a global discarder or JCasC defaults so new jobs inherit sane retention.
Q9How do you handle large binary artifacts (hundreds of MB)?
Don't put them in Jenkins at all. Push to S3, Nexus, Artifactory or the container registry, and archive only a small manifest or a URL file. Jenkins artifacts are for reports and small packages; anything large belongs in a system designed for it.
Q10How do you keep artifacts after deleting the build?
You can't — the archive lives under the build directory. If a build record is discarded, its artifacts go too. That's why anything with retention or compliance value must be copied to external storage inside the pipeline, in the same stage that archives it.
20
Block 20 · DevSecOps & scale

Notifications & human approvals

Goal: get the right message to the right channel, and put a deliberate human checkpoint in front of risky actions.

20.1 The input step — pausing for a human

groovyApproval gate with timeout, allowed approvers and captured values
stage('Approve production deploy') {
    when { branch 'main' }
    steps {
        script {
            timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES') {          // never wait forever
                def approval = input(
                    id: 'ProdDeploy',
                    message: "Deploy ${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA} to PRODUCTION?",
                    ok: 'Ship it',
                    submitter: 'release-managers,admins',   // Jenkins users/groups allowed to approve
                    submitterParameter: 'APPROVER',         // who approved -> approval.APPROVER
                    parameters: [
                        choice(name: 'STRATEGY', choices: ['rolling','blue-green'], description: 'Deploy strategy'),
                        booleanParam(name: 'RUN_SMOKE', defaultValue: true, description: 'Run smoke tests after')
                    ]
                )
                env.APPROVER      = approval['APPROVER']
                env.DEPLOY_STRATEGY = approval['STRATEGY']
                echo "Approved by ${env.APPROVER} using ${env.DEPLOY_STRATEGY}"
            }
        }
    }
}
OptionPurpose
messageShown in the UI prompt — include what is being deployed
okLabel for the confirm button ("Deploy", not "Proceed")
submitterComma-separated users/groups allowed to approve (pairs with RBAC)
submitterParameterCaptures who approved, for the audit trail
parametersCollect extra decisions at approval time
idStable identifier — required if you use the API to approve
⚠️Always wrap input in timeout An input with no timeout holds an executor and a workspace indefinitely — the classic "why is Jenkins out of executors?" outage. Wrapping it in timeout() also gives you a defined result: timeouts abort the build, which you can handle in post { aborted { } }.
groovyApproval outside a stage's agent (best practice)
stage('Approve') {
    agent none                 // don't hold an agent while waiting for a human!
    when { beforeAgent true; branch 'main' }
    steps {
        timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES') {
            input message: 'Deploy to production?', ok: 'Deploy'
        }
    }
}

beforeAgent true evaluates the when condition before an agent is allocated — combined with agent none this means no executor is consumed while waiting.

20.2 Slack notifications

uiOne-time Slack setup
1. Install the "Slack Notification" plugin
2. Manage Jenkins -> System -> Slack
     Workspace      : your-workspace
     Credential     : add a "Secret text" credential with the bot token (ID: slack-bot)
     Default channel: #ci
3. Test Connection -> "Success" before you rely on it
groovyslackSend in post conditions
post {
    success {
        slackSend channel: '#ci', color: 'good',
            message: "✅ *${env.JOB_NAME}* #${env.BUILD_NUMBER} succeeded\n" +
                     "Image: \`${IMAGE}:${env.GIT_SHA}\`\n<${env.BUILD_URL}|Open build>"
    }
    failure {
        slackSend channel: '#ci', color: 'danger',
            message: "❌ *${env.JOB_NAME}* #${env.BUILD_NUMBER} FAILED (<${env.BUILD_URL}|logs>)"
    }
    unstable {
        slackSend channel: '#ci', color: 'warning',
            message: "⚠️ *${env.JOB_NAME}* #${env.BUILD_NUMBER} unstable — quality gates not met"
    }
    fixed {
        slackSend channel: '#ci', color: 'good', message: "🎉 Back to green!"
    }
}

20.3 Email (Extended E-mail Notification)

groovyemailext with an HTML body and a log excerpt
post {
    failure {
        script {
            def culprits = currentBuild.changeSets
                    .collectMany { it.items.collect { it.author.email ?: it.author.fullName } }
                    .unique().join(',')
            def log = currentBuild.rawBuild.getLog(60).join('\n')     // last 60 lines

            emailext(
                subject: "FAILED: ${env.JOB_NAME} #${env.BUILD_NUMBER}",
                to: culprits ?: 'devops@example.com',
                mimeType: 'text/html',
                body: """
                    <h3 style="color:#e11d48">Build FAILED</h3>
                    <p><b>Job:</b> ${env.JOB_NAME} #${env.BUILD_NUMBER}<br/>
                       <b>Branch:</b> ${env.BRANCH_NAME ?: 'n/a'}<br/>
                       <b>Commit:</b> ${env.GIT_SHA}<br/>
                       <a href="${env.BUILD_URL}">Open in Jenkins</a></p>
                    <pre style="background:#0d1526;color:#dce6f5;padding:12px">${log}</pre>
                """,
                attachLog: true,
                compressLog: true
            )
        }
    }
}

20.4 Any other system (generic webhook)

groovyTeams / Discord / PagerDuty via httpRequest
// Requires the "HTTP Request" plugin
def response = httpRequest(
    httpMode: 'POST',
    url: 'https://events.pagerduty.com/v2/enqueue',
    contentType: 'APPLICATION_JSON',
    requestBody: groovy.json.JsonOutput.toJson([
        routing_key: PD_KEY, event_action: 'trigger',
        payload: [summary: "Jenkins ${env.JOB_NAME} failed", severity: 'error', source: env.NODE_NAME]
    ])
)
echo "PagerDuty responded ${response.status}"

20.5 Notification design principles

PrincipleWhy
Notify on state change, not every build200 "build passed" messages a day = nobody reads any of them
Failures go to the person who broke itcurrentBuild.changeSets gives you the commit authors
Include a deep link and the artifact identity${BUILD_URL} and the SHA tag — no context switching
Page only for regressions on release branchesUse post { regression { } } and branch conditions
Never let notifications fail the buildWrap in try/catch or catchError — a Slack outage must not break CI

20.6 ✅ Checklist

  • An approval gate pauses the prod deploy and times out after 30 minutes.
  • Slack (or email) receives failure and success messages with a build link.
  • Notification failures cannot break the build.
  • Only changed-state events notify (no spam on every green build).

20.7 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1How do you add a manual approval to a pipeline?
With the input step inside a timeout(), restricted by submitter to a release group and captured with submitterParameter for the audit trail. I put it in a stage with agent none and when { beforeAgent true } so no executor is held while waiting for a human.
Q2Why is an un-timeout'd input dangerous?
It occupies an executor and a workspace indefinitely. Enough of them and the queue backs up until no build can run at all. A timeout turns that into a controlled abort I can handle in post { aborted { } }.
Q3How do you avoid notification fatigue?
Notify on state transitions — failure, fixed, regression — rather than on every success, route failures to the commit authors from currentBuild.changeSets, and reserve paging for regressions on release branches.
Q4Slack is down. Your build fails?
It must not. Notification steps are wrapped so their failure is logged, not propagated — losing a Slack message is acceptable, losing a build because the chat provider had an outage is not.

20.8 Under the hood — how approval actually works

💡The mental model While a pipeline is paused at input, the build is still occupying an executor and its state is persisted to disk. That's why it survives a controller restart. It's also why a long input inside node {} is expensive — prefer agent none plus a lightweight approval stage.
Where the approval comes fromMechanism
A human clicks in the UIJenkins renders the input form with your parameters and submitters
A script or chat bot approvesPOST to /job/<job>/<n>/input/<id>/submit with proceed= and the parameter JSON
A timeout firesThe step throws FlowInterruptedException → the build is aborted unless you wrap it
bashApprove from the CLI / chat bot
# List pending inputs
curl -sS -u "user:$API_TOKEN" \
  "$JENKINS_URL/job/nodejs-pipeline/job/main/42/wfapi/pendingInputActions" | jq

# Approve (parameter name must match the input parameter exactly)
curl -sS -u "user:$API_TOKEN" -X POST \
  "$JENKINS_URL/job/nodejs-pipeline/job/main/42/input/ProdDeploy/submit" \
  --data-urlencode 'json={"parameter":[{"name":"APPROVE","value":"true"}]}' \
  --data 'proceed=Approve'

20.9 Input patterns worth memorising

groovyPattern catalogue
// 1. Simple go/no-go restricted to a group
input message: 'Deploy to production?', ok: 'Deploy',
      submitter: 'release-managers',
      submitterParameter: 'APPROVED_BY'

// 2. Capture a value the pipeline then uses
def target = input(id: 'EnvChoice', message: 'Where?',
      parameters: [choice(name: 'ENV', choices: ['staging','prod'],
                          description: 'Target environment')])
echo "Deploying to ${target['ENV']}"

// 3. Timeout that FAILS FAST rather than aborting silently
timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES') {
    input message: 'Approve?'
}

// 4. Timeout that degrades gracefully (skip instead of fail)
def approved = false
try {
    timeout(time: 10, unit: 'MINUTES') {
        input message: 'Deploy now? (skips after 10 min)'
    }
    approved = true
} catch (err) {
    echo "No approval in time (${err}) - continuing without deploy"
}

// 5. Only ask when the change is risky
if (env.BRANCH_NAME == 'main' && currentBuild.number % 5 != 0) {
    input message: 'main branch - confirm'
}

20.10 Lab — approvals under pressure

🧪Lab 20.1 · Break the gate three ways 1. No submitter restriction. Remove submitter:, run the pipeline, and confirm any authenticated user can approve. That is a real audit finding.
2. Timeout with no handler. Set timeout(time: 1, unit: 'MINUTES') around the input, walk away, and observe the build result (ABORTED, not FAILED) and what post conditions fire.
3. Restart mid-approval. Start a build, wait for the prompt, then sudo systemctl restart jenkins. Log back in: the prompt is still there. This is durable pipeline state, and it's worth being able to describe it from memory.
bashWatch it survive
sudo systemctl restart jenkins
sleep 20 && curl -sSI http://localhost:8080/login | head -1

20.11 Governance: who may approve what

Change typeApproval
Feature branch buildNone — automatic
Deploy to dev / stagingNone — automatic, self-service
Deploy to production (normal)One approval from the on-call engineer
Deploy to production (out of hours / Friday)Two approvals, one from release management
Schema migration or rollbackEngineering manager + DBA
HotfixAutomatic to staging, retro-approval logged
🚨Two approval anti-patterns
  • Approval on every commit. It becomes reflex clicking and provides no safety. Gate only consequential actions.
  • Self-approval. Enable Prevent self-approval or use a submitter group the author isn't in, otherwise the control is decorative.

20.12 More interview Q&A

Q6What happens to resources while waiting for input?
The build keeps its executor and its state is persisted, so it survives a controller restart. To avoid blocking an executor for hours, put the approval in a stage with a lightweight agent (or agent none at top level with a tiny agent for that stage), and set a timeout.
Q7Approval timed out — what build result do you get and how do you handle it?
ABORTED, via a FlowInterruptedException. If I want a different outcome I wrap the input in try/catch and decide: skip the deployment and continue, fail the build, or notify. I make sure the post block treats ABORTED explicitly rather than lumping it in with failure.
Q8How do you restrict who can approve a production deploy?
With the submitter parameter naming a user or group, plus submitterParameter to record who approved into the build record. I also enable "prevent self-approval" so the person who authored the change cannot be the one who approves it.
Q9How do you make approvals auditable?
Record the approver (submitterParameter), the timestamp, the artifact identifier and the environment in the build description, and ship that to the audit log or chat channel. The build record must be able to answer who deployed what, when, and on whose authority.
Q10Should production deploys be manual at all?
Mature teams gate on evidence rather than on a human: canary or blue-green with automated health checks and automatic rollback. Manual approval is right while that safety net is immature, and for genuinely irreversible actions like destructive schema changes. The goal is to earn the right to remove the gate.
21
Block 21 · DevSecOps & scale

Parallel, matrix, timeouts, retries & locks

Goal: make pipelines fast (parallelism, matrix) and safe under concurrency (timeouts, retries, locks, milestones).

21.1 Parallel stages

groovyDeclarative parallel block
pipeline {
    agent none

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            agent { label 'linux' }
            steps { checkout scm }
        }

        stage('Quality & Security') {
            failFast true                       // one failure cancels the siblings
            parallel {
                stage('Unit tests') {
                    agent { label 'linux' }
                    steps { sh 'npm run test:junit' }
                }
                stage('Lint') {
                    agent { label 'linux' }
                    steps { sh 'npm run lint' }
                }
                stage('Dependency scan') {
                    agent { label 'linux' }
                    steps { sh 'trivy fs --exit-code 1 --severity HIGH,CRITICAL .' }
                }
                stage('Secret scan') {
                    agent { label 'linux' }
                    steps { sh 'gitleaks detect --source . --redact' }
                }
            }
        }

        stage('Package') {
            agent { label 'docker' }
            steps { sh 'docker build -t app:$GIT_SHA .' }
        }
    }
}
⚠️Parallel needs executors Four parallel stages need four free executors (or four agents). With two executors they simply queue and you gain nothing — parallelism is bought with infrastructure, not syntax. Check Manage Jenkins → Nodes before promising a 4× speed-up.

21.2 Parallel in scripted style (when you need dynamic branches)

groovyBuild a parallel map at runtime
stage('Test both apps') {
    steps {
        script {
            def apps = ['nodejs-app': 'node', 'python-app': 'python']
            def branches = apps.collectEntries { name, type ->
                ["${name}": {
                    node('linux') {
                        dir(name) {
                            runTests(type: type, minCoverage: 70)   // shared library step (Block 14)
                        }
                    }
                }]
            }
            parallel branches
        }
    }
}

21.3 Matrix — the same job across many combinations

groovyMatrix with axes, excludes and per-cell behaviour
pipeline {
    agent none

    stages {
        stage('Cross-platform tests') {
            matrix {
                axes {
                    axis { name 'NODE_VERSION'; values '18', '20', '22' }
                    axis { name 'OS';           values 'linux', 'alpine' }
                }
                excludes {
                    exclude {   // Node 18 on alpine isn't supported by this project
                        axis { name 'NODE_VERSION'; values '18' }
                        axis { name 'OS';           values 'alpine' }
                    }
                }
                agent { docker { image "node:${NODE_VERSION}-${OS}"; reuseNode true } }
                stages {
                    stage('Test') {
                        steps {
                            echo "Testing on Node ${NODE_VERSION} / ${OS}"
                            sh 'node -v && npm ci && npm run test:junit'
                        }
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
🧪Real-world matrix uses Test across Node/Python versions, OSes and architectures; run the same smoke suite against multiple target environments; build multi-arch images (linux/amd64, linux/arm64) in parallel and combine them into one manifest.

21.4 Timeouts, retries and waiting

groovyThe resilience toolkit
// Pipeline-wide safety net
options { timeout(time: 45, unit: 'MINUTES') }

// Per-stage override
stage('Long scan') {
    options { timeout(time: 15, unit: 'MINUTES') }
    steps { sh 'trivy image --exit-code 1 app:latest' }
}

steps {
    retry(3) {                                   // retry the exact same block, 3 times
        sh 'docker pull node:20-alpine'
    }

    retry(count: 5, conditions: [               // only retry on specific causes
        agent(), nonresumable()
    ]) {
        node('flaky-agent') { sh 'true' }
    }

    waitUntil {                                  // poll until the block returns true
        def ok = sh(script: 'curl -fsS http://localhost:3000/health', returnStatus: true) == 0
        sleep(time: 3, unit: 'SECONDS')
        return ok
    }

    timeout(time: 2, unit: 'MINUTES') {          // hard cap on any of the above
        waitUntil { sh(script: 'curl -fsS http://localhost:3000/health', returnStatus: true) == 0 }
    }

    sleep(time: 10, unit: 'SECONDS')             // simple pause
}

21.5 Locks — serialising shared resources

groovyLockable Resources plugin
// Define resources: Manage Jenkins -> System -> Lockable Resources
//   Name: deploy-prod     (quantity 1)
//   Name: test-database   (quantity 3)

stage('Deploy to production') {
    steps {
        lock(resource: 'deploy-prod') {          // only one prod deploy at a time
            sh './deploy.sh prod'
        }
    }
}

stage('Integration tests') {
    steps {
        lock(label: 'test-database', quantity: 1, variable: 'DB_RESOURCE') {
            echo "Using database resource: ${env.DB_RESOURCE}"
            sh 'pytest tests/integration'
        }
    }
}

// Inverse: block concurrent runs of THIS job
options { disableConcurrentBuilds() }

// Or: kill the older build when a new one starts
options {
    disableConcurrentBuilds(abortPrevious: true)
}

21.6 Milestones — correct ordering of concurrent builds

groovyMilestone + lock for sane deploy ordering
stage('Deploy') {
    steps {
        milestone 1                       // build #5 reaches this first
        lock(resource: 'deploy-prod') {
            milestone 2                   // older builds that reach here are cancelled
            sh './deploy.sh'
        }
    }
}

Two pushes land close together. Without milestones, the older build could deploy after the newer one and silently roll you back. milestone cancels any build that is behind — exactly the semantics you want for a deploy stage.

21.7 ✅ Checklist

  • A parallel block runs 3+ checks simultaneously and failFast behaves as expected.
  • A matrix runs the same tests across multiple Node/Python versions.
  • Every long-running stage has a timeout; flaky network steps have retry.
  • Prod deploy is protected by a lock (and ideally a milestone).

21.8 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1How do you speed up a slow Jenkins pipeline?
Run independent work in a parallel block (tests, lint, scans), put the fastest and most-likely-to-fail checks first, use a matrix instead of sequential loops, cache dependencies and Docker layers, and make sure there are enough executors to actually run things concurrently.
Q2How do you handle flaky steps?
retry(n) for genuinely transient operations like registry pulls or network calls, and waitUntil for polling a service until it's ready. I do not use retry to hide flaky tests — those get quarantined and fixed, because retry only delays the failure.
Q3How do you prevent two deployments running at once?
lock(resource: 'deploy-prod') from the Lockable Resources plugin so only one build holds the deploy slot. Combined with milestone, an older build that arrives late is cancelled instead of rolling the environment backwards.
Q4What does disableConcurrentBuilds do, and what's the alternative?
It queues new runs while one is in progress. With abortPrevious: true the older run is cancelled instead — usually what you want on a fast-moving main branch, while for deployments you'd prefer queuing so every release is actually attempted.
Q5When would you use matrix over parallel?
When the branches are the same work across different dimensions — Node 18/20/22 × linux/alpine. Matrix gives me a compact declaration, automatic naming, exclusions for unsupported combinations, and per-cell results in one view, instead of me hand-writing six near-identical parallel stages.

21.8 Under the hood — the master/agent file path contract

⚠️The single most expensive mistake in Jenkins pipelines Using File, new FileOutputStream, Paths.get or Files.* in pipeline code operates on the controller's filesystem, not the agent's workspace. It appears to work when you test on a single-machine install, then silently reads or writes the wrong machine in production — and can corrupt $JENKINS_HOME. Always use pipeline steps: readFile, writeFile, sh, findFiles, archiveArtifacts.
Instead of this (Java/Groovy)Use this (pipeline step)
new File(path).textreadFile(path)
new File(path).write(...)writeFile(file:…, text:…) or sh 'cat > …'
file.eachLine {}readFile(...).split('\n') or sh '…' returning text
new File(p).exists()fileExists(p)
dir.listFiles()findFiles(glob: '**/*.json')
new File(p).delete()sh 'rm -f …' or deleteDir()
new XmlSlurper().parse(f)readJSON / readMavenPom / parse with sh 'jq'
groovyfindFiles — the agent-safe way to enumerate
steps {
    script {
        def reports = findFiles(glob: '**/coverage/*.xml')
        echo "Found ${reports.length} coverage reports"
        reports.each { f ->
            echo "${f.path} (${f.length} bytes, dir=${f.directory})"
        }
        if (reports.length == 0) { error 'No coverage report - failing fast' }
    }
}

21.9 CPS deep dive — what breaks and why

ConstructStatus in CPSWhat to do
for (i in list), each {}SafePreferred
Closures like { it -> ... }SafePreferred
Java streams / .parallelStream()UnsupportedUse a loop or @NonCPS
while(true) without body checkpointRisky (infinite loop blocks CPS)Add a bounded counter
RecursionRisky (stack depth)Prefer iteration
Anonymous inner classesNot supportedMove to src/ or @NonCPS
try/catch/finallySafeUse it
Exceptions crossing nodeMust be serializableCatch inside the same node block
groovyThe @NonCPS escape hatch, used correctly
// Runs as plain Groovy on the controller -- NO pipeline steps allowed inside.
// Perfect for string/JSON munging that would otherwise confuse CPS.
@NonCPS
String renderMarkdownTable(String json) {
    def rows = new groovy.json.JsonSlurper().parseText(json)
    def sb = new StringBuilder()
    sb.append('| Service | Version |\n|---|---|\n')
    rows.each { r -> sb.append("| ${r.name} | ${r.version} |\n") }
    return sb.toString()                       // return a plain String
}

pipeline {
    agent any
    stages {
        stage('Report') {
            steps {
                script {
                    def raw = sh(script: 'cat versions.json', returnStdout: true).trim()
                    def table = renderMarkdownTable(raw)     // call it from CPS code
                    writeFile file: 'report.md', text: table
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
🚨Rule Inside an @NonCPS method you may not call sh, echo, readFile or any other pipeline step — they will throw or silently do nothing. Keep @NonCPS for pure computation, and do the I/O in CPS code.

21.10 Lab — reproduce a real production failure

🧪Lab 21.2 · The file that isn't there Run this on a controller with a separate agent:
groovyDeliberately wrong, then corrected
pipeline {
    agent { label 'linux' }                 // runs on the AGENT
    stages {
        stage('Broken') {
            steps {
                script {
                    // WRONG: touches the CONTROLLER filesystem
                    def f = new File('/tmp/agent-proof.txt')
                    f.text = 'written from the pipeline'
                    echo "exists? ${f.exists()}"       // true -- on the controller!
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Fixed') {
            steps {
                // RIGHT: runs where the workspace is
                sh 'echo "written from the pipeline" > /tmp/agent-proof.txt && cat /tmp/agent-proof.txt'
                writeFile file: 'proof.txt', text: 'written via writeFile'
                echo readFile('proof.txt')
            }
        }
    }
}
Then SSH to the agent: /tmp/agent-proof.txt exists there. SSH to the controller: it does not. Meanwhile the new File version created the file on the controller. Once you have seen this once, you will never use new File in a pipeline again.

21.11 Performance: make pipelines fast

TechniqueTypical gainHow
Parallelise independent stages2–5×parallel for build/lint/SCA/SAST as we do
Cache dependencies outside the workspace50–80% of install time/.npm and /.cache/pip mounts outside the workspace
Skip unchanged workLarge on monoreposwhen { changeset 'nodejs-app/**' }
Keep agents warmRemoves cold-startLong-lived agents or a warm image pool
Avoid agent none thrashSeconds per stageGroup steps that can share one agent
Shrink Docker contextSeconds to minutes.dockerignore
Fail fastHuge on broken commitsCheap stages first: lint, then test, then scans
groovyCheapest-first ordering — fail in 40 seconds, not 12 minutes
stages {
    stage('Lint')  { steps { sh 'npm run lint' } }        // ~20s
    stage('Unit')  { steps { sh 'npm test' } }            // ~60s
    stage('Build') { steps { sh 'npm run build' } }       // ~90s
    stage('Scans') { steps { sh 'trivy fs .' } }          // ~120s
    stage('Image') { steps { sh 'docker build ...' } }    // ~180s
}

21.12 More interview Q&A

Q6Why must pipeline variables be serializable?
Because durable pipelines persist state to disk at every checkpoint so a build can survive a controller restart. Any object captured in the CPS state — including exceptions you let escape a node block — must be serializable. Non-serializable objects cause "java.io.NotSerializableException" at exactly the worst moment.
Q7When do you reach for @NonCPS?
Only for pure computation that CPS cannot express — Java streams, regex-heavy string work, JSON/XML parsing — and never for anything that calls pipeline steps. It runs as ordinary Groovy on the controller, which also means it must be cheap and must not touch the filesystem.
Q8How do you debug a pipeline that only fails on the agent?
Reproduce inside the same container with docker run --rm -it -v $PWD:/w -w /w node:20-alpine sh, dump the environment with sh 'env | sort', compare users and paths (id, pwd), and check that anything the build needs is actually installed in the image rather than on the long-lived host.
Q9How do you make a slow pipeline faster without adding risk?
Measure first — the Pipeline Timing or Blue Ocean view shows where the minutes go. Then parallelise independent stages, cache dependencies outside the workspace, order stages cheapest-first so failures surface in under a minute, and skip work that the changed paths cannot affect.
Q10What is the risk of running builds on the controller?
Everything the build does runs with controller privileges, has access to $JENKINS_HOME and all credentials, and competes with the UI for CPU and memory — a runaway build can take CI down for everyone. I set the built-in node to zero executors and run all builds on agents.
22
Block 22 · Operations & interviews

Backup, upgrade & maintenance

Goal: be the person who can restore Jenkins at 3 a.m. — and who can upgrade it without an outage. Operations knowledge is what turns a pipeline author into a platform engineer.

22.1 What exactly to back up

ItemPathCritical?Notes
Job configuration$JENKINS_HOME/jobs/**/config.xml🔴 CriticalAlso in Git if you use JCasC
Global config$JENKINS_HOME/config.xml🔴 CriticalSecurity settings, tool config
Credentials + master key$JENKINS_HOME/secrets/🔴 CriticalBack up master.key separately
Plugin listplugins.txt (yours)🟡 ImportantReproducible rebuild
JenkinsfilesYour Git repos🔴 CriticalAlready versioned — the point of Pipeline as Code
Build historyjobs/*/builds/⚪ NiceHuge; exclude from frequent backups
Workspacesworkspace/⚪ NoRecreated on next build — exclude

22.2 A backup script you can cron

bashscripts/jenkins-backup.sh
#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Backs up the parts of JENKINS_HOME that matter, and nothing else.
set -euo pipefail

JENKINS_HOME="${JENKINS_HOME:-/var/lib/jenkins}"
BACKUP_DIR="${BACKUP_DIR:-/var/backups/jenkins}"
RETENTION_DAYS="${RETENTION_DAYS:-14}"
STAMP=$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S)
OUT="${BACKUP_DIR}/jenkins-${STAMP}.tar.gz"

mkdir -p "$BACKUP_DIR"

# Quiesce: stop new builds while we copy, so nothing changes mid-backup
sudo systemctl stop jenkins

tar --exclude='jobs/*/builds/*/archive' \
    --exclude='jobs/*/workspace*' \
    --exclude='workspace/*' \
    --exclude='caches/*' \
    --exclude='logs/*' \
    --exclude='tools/*' \
    -czf "$OUT" -C "$JENKINS_HOME" .

sudo systemctl start jenkins

# The master key goes to a SEPARATE, more restricted location
sudo cp "$JENKINS_HOME/secrets/master.key" "${BACKUP_DIR}/master.key-${STAMP}"
sudo chmod 600 "${BACKUP_DIR}/master.key-${STAMP}"

find "$BACKUP_DIR" -name 'jenkins-*.tar.gz' -mtime +$RETENTION_DAYS -delete
echo "Backup written: $OUT ($(du -h "$OUT" | cut -f1))"
cronNightly at 02:15
15 2 * * * /home/ubuntu/jenkins-zero-to-hero/scripts/jenkins-backup.sh >> /var/log/jenkins-backup.log 2>&1
☁️Off-box copies A backup on the same disk as Jenkins is not a backup. Sync to S3 afterwards: aws s3 sync /var/backups/jenkins s3://your-bucket/jenkins/ --storage-class STANDARD_IA, and turn on versioning + a lifecycle rule. The ThinBackup plugin can do all of this declaratively if you prefer UI-driven backups.

22.3 Configuration as Code (JCasC) — the modern approach

Instead of backing up XML and hoping, describe your controller in YAML. Install Configuration as Code (configuration-as-code), then Manage Jenkins → System → Configuration as Code:

yamljenkins/jenkins.yaml (JCasC)
jenkins:
  systemMessage: "Managed by JCasC - do not edit by hand"
  numExecutors: 0                     # controller runs no builds
  securityRealm:
    local:
      users:
        - id: "admin"
          password: "${JENKINS_ADMIN_PASSWORD}"     # from an env var / secret
  authorizationStrategy:
    roleBased:
      roles:
        global:
          - name: "admin"
            permissions: ["Overall/Administer"]
            assignments: ["admin"]
  nodes:
    - permanent:
        name: "linux-1"
        remoteFS: "/home/jenkins/agent"
        numExecutors: 2
        labelString: "linux docker node20"

tool:
  git:
    installations:
      - name: "Default"
        home: "git"

credentials:
  system:
    domainCredentials:
      - credentials:
          - usernamePassword:
              id: "dockerhub-creds"
              username: "your-user"
              password: "${DOCKERHUB_TOKEN}"        # injected, never in Git

unclassified:
  sonarGlobalConfiguration:
    installations:
      - name: "sonar"
        serverUrl: "http://localhost:9000"
🚀Why JCasC is a career multiplier Your controller becomes rebuildable from a file: new server, apply YAML, done. It's reviewable in PRs, testable, and the modern standard (often combined with the Jenkins Operator on Kubernetes). Mentioning "I manage Jenkins with JCasC + a plugin manifest" instantly signals platform-level thinking.

22.4 Keeping the controller healthy

TaskHowFrequency
Discard old buildsbuildDiscarder(logRotator(numToKeepStr:'30')) per pipelineAutomatic

Prune Dockerdocker image/container/builder prune -fNightly cron
Rotate logslogrotate on /var/log/jenkins/Automatic

Review users & rolesManage Jenkins → Manage and Assign RolesQuarterly

22.5 Maintenance with the Script Console

groovyManage Jenkins → Script Console (run with care)
// 1. Delete builds older than 30 days from every job
import jenkins.model.Jenkins
import java.util.Calendar

def cutoff = Calendar.getInstance()
cutoff.add(Calendar.DAY_OF_MONTH, -30)

Jenkins.instance.allItems(hudson.model.Job).each { job ->
  job.builds.findAll { it.time < cutoff.time }.each { build ->
    println "Deleting ${job.fullName} #${build.number}"
    build.delete()
  }
}

// 2. Find jobs that have never been built
Jenkins.instance.allItems(hudson.model.Job).findAll { it.lastBuild == null }
        .each { println "Never built: ${it.fullName}" }

// 3. Which jobs are taking the longest?
Jenkins.instance.allItems(hudson.model.Job).collect { job ->
  def b = job.lastBuild
  [name: job.fullName, avg: job.builds.collect { it.duration }.with { it ? it.sum()/it.size() : 0 }]
}.sort { -it.avg }.take(20).each { println "${it.name}: ${(it.avg/1000 as int)}s" }

// 4. Reset a forgotten admin password
def u = hudson.model.User.get('admin')
u.addProperty(hudson.security.HudsonPrivateSecurityRealm.Details.fromPlainPassword('NewTempPass123!'))
u.save()
🚨The Script Console is root on Jenkins It executes Groovy with full controller privileges — including reading every credential. Restrict it with RBAC, never leave it open to non-admins, and always test scripts on a non-production controller first. Everything you run here is permanent.

22.6 The upgrade procedure

  1. Read the changelog and the upgrade guide for your LTS line (e.g. 2.440 → 2.452). Note Java version requirements.
  2. Back up JENKINS_HOME and master.key. Verify the archive opens.
  3. Update plugins first, on the old Jenkins version, and restart. Plugin incompatibilities are far more common than core problems.
  4. Upgrade core: sudo apt update && sudo apt install jenkins (or swap the WAR / container image).
  5. Restart and verify: plugins loaded, agents reconnected, one pipeline from each app runs green.
  6. Rollback plan: keep the previous WAR/package and the backup; restore = stop, restore files, start.
bashFind your version and check what's installed
java -jar /usr/share/jenkins/jenkins.war --version     # core version
# or: Manage Jenkins -> About Jenkins

# List installed plugins with versions (great for diffs)
curl -s -u user:$TOKEN "http://localhost:8080/pluginManager/api/json?depth=1" \
  | jq -r '.plugins[] | "\(.shortName) \(.version)"' | sort

22.7 ✅ Checklist

  • Nightly backup job runs and you have restored from it at least once (untested backups don't count).
  • master.key is backed up separately.
  • Discard-old-builds + workspace cleanup + Docker pruning are all in place.
  • You can describe the six-step upgrade procedure without notes.
  • You have written (or at least read) a JCasC file.

22.8 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1How do you back up Jenkins?
I back up JENKINS_HOME excluding workspaces, build archives and caches, with Jenkins stopped briefly for consistency. secrets/master.key is copied separately and tightly restricted, because without it credentials can never be decrypted. Copies go off-box to S3 with versioning, and I test the restore regularly — an untested backup isn't a backup.
Q2What's your upgrade process?
Read the changelog and Java requirements, verify the backup, update plugins first (they cause most breakage), then upgrade core, restart, and smoke-test one pipeline per project. I keep the previous package and the backup so rollback is a file restore away.
Q3Jenkins is slow. Where do you look?
Disk first (workspaces, Docker layers, build history), then the queue and executor usage, then memory and GC on the controller, then slow plugins (especially SCM scanning and heavy publishers). I add buildDiscarder, per-stage timeouts and the Timestamper plugin to find the specific slow stage.
Q4What is JCasC and why use it?
Jenkins Configuration as Code: the entire controller configuration — security, tools, agents, credentials — described in YAML and loaded at startup. It makes the controller reproducible, reviewable in Git, and trivial to recreate after a disaster, which is a big step up from restoring XML blobs.
Q5How would you rebuild Jenkins from scratch after losing the server?
Provision a box with the install script, restore the JENKINS_HOME backup (or apply the JCasC YAML plus the plugin manifest), restore master.key, start Jenkins, verify agents reconnect, and run one pipeline per project. Because Jenkinsfiles live in Git, no pipeline logic was ever lost.

22.8 Under the hood — JCasC and immutability

Jenkins Configuration as Code reads a YAML file at start-up and reconciles it with the live configuration. Its value is not "config in a file" — it's that configuration becomes reviewable, diffable and reproducible.

yamljenkins.yaml — a compact but real baseline
jenkins:
  systemMessage: "Managed by JCasC - manual changes will be reverted on restart"
  numExecutors: 0                       # never build on the controller
  labelString: "built-in"
  mode: EXCLUSIVE
  securityRealm:
    local:
      allowsSignup: false
      users:
        - id: "admin"
          password: "${ADMIN_PASSWORD}"   # injected from the environment
  authorizationStrategy:
    roleBased:
      roles:
        global:
          - name: "admin"
            permissions:
              - "Overall/Administer"
            assignments:
              - "admin"
  nodes:
    - permanent:
        name: "linux-1"
        remoteFS: "/home/jenkins/agent"
        numExecutors: 4
        labelString: "linux docker"
        launcher:
          ssh:
            host: "10.0.1.20"
            credentialsId: "agent-ssh"
  tool:
    git:
      installations:
        - name: "Default"
          home: "git"
security:
  queueItemAuthenticator:
    authenticators:
      - global:
          strategy: "triggeringUsersAuthorizationStrategy"
credentials:
  system:
    domainCredentials:
      - credentials:
          - usernamePassword:
              id: "dockerhub-creds"
              username: "${DOCKER_USER}"
              password: "${DOCKER_TOKEN}"
              description: "Docker Hub push account"
💡Secrets in JCasC Never commit real secrets: reference environment variables or files with ${VAR} and supply them from your secret manager at boot. Manage Credentials is for runtime use; JCasC bootstraps the config around them.

22.9 Backup and disaster recovery

WhatWhy it mattersHow often
$JENKINS_HOME job configsThe jobs themselvesDaily
credentials.xml + master.key / hudson.util.SecretWithout the key, credentials cannot be decrypted — the backup is uselessDaily, encrypted, stored separately
Plugin listReproducible installOn change
Build history & artifactsCompliance, debuggingWeekly (or external storage only)
🚨The backup trap Most teams back up $JENKINS_HOME and forget master.key, then discover during a restore that every credential is unreadable. Back up the key, encrypted, in a different location, and test a restore at least once.
bashMinimal, correct backup (ThinBackup-style, tar based)
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail
STAMP=$(date +%F-%H%M)
DEST=/backup/jenkins/$STAMP
mkdir -p "$DEST"

systemctl stop jenkins
tar --exclude='*/workspace/*' \
    --exclude='*/builds/*/archive/*' \
    --exclude='caches' --exclude='logs' \
    -czf "$DEST/jenkins-home.tgz" /var/lib/jenkins

# The decryption key -- without it the backup CANNOT restore credentials
cp /var/lib/jenkins/secrets/master.key "$DEST/" 2>/dev/null || true
cp /var/lib/jenkins/secrets/hudson.util.Secret "$DEST/" 2>/dev/null || true
systemctl start jenkins

# Retention
find /backup/jenkins -maxdepth 1 -mtime +14 -type d -exec rm -rf {} +
echo "backup complete: $DEST"

22.10 Lab — rebuild Jenkins from nothing

🧪Lab 22.1 · Prove you can recover This is the exercise that turns "I configured Jenkins" into "I can operate Jenkins". Do it once, on a spare EC2 instance.
  1. sudo systemctl stop jenkins and move /var/lib/jenkins aside.
  2. Install Jenkins fresh with jenkins/ec2-install.sh.
  3. Restore jenkins.yaml → start → confirm the controller comes back with your agents, users and roles.
  4. Restore credentials.xml plus the key files → confirm a pipeline can still push to Docker Hub.
  5. Point a Multibranch job at your GitHub repo and press Scan Repository Now — all branches reappear without a single manual click.
Time it. If recovery takes more than about 30 minutes, automate more of it.

22.11 Observability for CI

SignalHow to get itWhy you care
Build duration trendPipeline Timing plugin, or currentBuild.duration to a metrics sinkSlow creep is invisible day to day
Success rate / flakinessBuild history + test trendsFlaky tests destroy trust in CI
Queue depth and wait timeMonitoring plugin, PrometheusCapacity problems show up here first
Agent availabilityNode monitors, PrometheusOffline agents silently halve capacity
Disk usage of $JENKINS_HOMENode exporter / CloudWatchFull disk = dead controller
Security findings over timeWarnings NG trends, SonarQube historyShows whether DevSecOps is working

22.12 More interview Q&A

Q6How do you manage Jenkins at scale?
Configuration as Code for the controller, a pinned plugin list (or a pre-baked image), agents provisioned from images or Kubernetes, and pipelines plus shared libraries in Git. No manual configuration, no snowflake controller — I should be able to delete it and have it back within the hour.
Q7How do you test a Jenkins upgrade safely?
Take a backup, restore it onto a staging controller, run a representative subset of jobs against it, and only then upgrade production — with a rollback path (AMI/snapshot) and a maintenance window. I read the plugin compatibility notes first, because plugin breakage is the usual failure mode, not Jenkins itself.
Q8How do you back up and restore Jenkins?
ThinBackup or a scripted tar of $JENKINS_HOME excluding workspaces and archived artifacts, including master.key so credentials remain decryptable, stored off the controller with retention. Restore is rehearsed, not assumed.
Q9How do you secure the controller itself?
HTTPS with a real certificate, no anonymous access, matrix or role-based authorisation, CSRF protection on, agent-to-controller access control, zero executors on the built-in node, secrets only in the credential store, and the controller isolated so build agents can't reach it except through the remoting channel.
Q10How do you monitor Jenkins health?
Prometheus metrics for queue depth, executor usage and build durations; alerts on agent offline, disk usage and queue backlog; plus trends for flaky tests and security findings. The goal is to notice degradation before developers start complaining.
23
Block 23 · Operations & interviews

Troubleshooting playbook

Goal: stop guessing. A repeatable debugging method plus a reference of the errors that actually happen — 90% of "Jenkins is broken" is one of these.

23.1 The five-step debugging method

  1. Read the first error, not the last. Scroll the console log to the first ERROR or failing + command. Java stack traces list 50 lines of context but the cause is usually in the first 5.
  2. Reproduce as the Jenkins user. sudo -iu jenkins <command>. If it fails there too, it's not a Jenkins problem — it's permissions, PATH, or the environment.
  3. Compare environments. Add sh 'env | sort', sh 'whoami && pwd && node -v'. Differences in PATH, HOME and tool versions explain most "works locally" bugs.
  4. Isolate with Replay. Edit the Jenkinsfile in the browser, reduce it to just the failing step, iterate in seconds.
  5. Inspect the workspace. Job → Workspace. Are the files actually there? Did the checkout happen? Is a stale artifact breaking you? (Then cleanWs().)

23.2 Diagnostic snippets to keep in your pocket

groovyDrop-in debug stage
stage('Debug') {
    steps {
        sh '''
            set +e                     # keep going so we see everything
            echo "--- identity ---";  whoami; id
            echo "--- location ---";  pwd; ls -la
            echo "--- tools ---";     node -v 2>/dev/null; npm -v 2>/dev/null
                                      python3 -V 2>/dev/null; docker -v 2>/dev/null
                                      git --version; java -version 2>&1 | head -1
            echo "--- env ---";       env | sort
            echo "--- disk ---";      df -h . ; du -sh . 2>/dev/null
            echo "--- network ---";   curl -sS -m 5 -o /dev/null -w "github:%{http_code}\\n" https://github.com
                                      curl -sS -m 5 -o /dev/null -w "dockerhub:%{http_code}\\n" https://hub.docker.com
        '''
        echo "Jenkins env: BUILD=${env.BUILD_NUMBER} NODE=${env.NODE_NAME} LABELS=${env.NODE_LABELS}"
        echo "Params: ${params}"
        echo "Result so far: ${currentBuild.currentResult}"
    }
}

23.3 Error reference — agents & execution

Error / symptomRoot causeFix
Pending — waiting for next available executorAll executors busy, or no agent matches the labelAdd executors/agents; check the label spelling matches Nodes
No nodes with the label 'docker' foundAgent offline or mislabelledNodes → verify labels are space-separated and the agent is online
Required context class hudson.FilePath is missingFile step outside steps, or post with agent noneWrap in steps { } or node('label') { }
Agent connects then disconnects repeatedlyClock skew, disk full, Java crashCheck NTP, disk in the remote root dir, agent journalctl
hudson.remoting.ChannelClosedExceptionRemoting channel died (OOM, network)Raise agent heap, check network stability, look for OOM killer in dmesg
Build hangs foreverNo timeout, or a step waiting on stdinoptions { timeout(...) }; add < /dev/null to interactive commands

23.4 Error reference — Git, Docker & permissions

ErrorRoot causeFix
Permission denied (publickey)Public key absent on GitHub, or wrong key offeredVerify with ssh -T git@github.com as the jenkins user; re-add the deploy key
Host key verification failedHost not in known_hostssudo -iu jenkins ssh-keyscan github.com >> ~/.ssh/known_hosts
Could not resolve credentialsIdTypo, or the credential is in a folder the job can't seeCopy the exact ID from Credentials; check folder scope
Cannot connect to the Docker daemonjenkins not in docker group / daemon downusermod -aG docker jenkins + restart; systemctl status docker
denied: requested access to the resource is deniedWrong namespace or token lacks write scopeMatch DOCKER_USER to your Docker Hub namespace; use an access token
permission denied while trying to connect to the Docker daemon socketSocket owned by root:dockerAdd jenkins to the docker group and restart Jenkins (group loads at login)
no space left on deviceWorkspaces, images, build recordsdocker system prune -a; buildDiscarder; cleanWs()
Command not found for a tool that existsPATH differs for the jenkins userUse absolute paths, or tools { } / withEnv

23.5 Error reference — pipeline syntax & plugins

ErrorRoot causeFix
No such DSL method 'xxx' found among stepsPlugin missing, step outside steps, or a typoInstall the plugin; move the call inside steps { }; check spelling
Missing required parameter: "agent"agent declared with no valueagent any / agent none / agent { label '…' }
WorkflowScript: 12: expecting '}'Unbalanced brace/comma earlier in the fileRead ~10 lines above; validate with the pipeline-model-converter/validate endpoint
Scripts not permitted to use method …Groovy sandbox blocked a signatureApprove it in In-process Script Approval, or move the logic into a shared library
Variable prints literally as ${VAR}Single quotes usedDouble quotes for interpolation
Could not find or load main class after upgradeJava version mismatchInstall a supported JDK (17/21) and set JAVA_HOME
Plugins fail to load after updateDependency conflictsRestore the backup, or update the dependency plugins first, one at a time
java.lang.OutOfMemoryErrorController heap too smallSet JAVA_OPTS=-Xmx2g in /etc/default/jenkins; reduce concurrent builds

23.6 Where to look — the log map

SymptomLog location
Job failedJob → Build → Console Output
Controller won't start / crashesjournalctl -u jenkins -f and /var/log/jenkins/jenkins.log
Webhook didn't triggerGitHub → Webhooks → Recent Deliveries; then Jenkins log
Agent won't connectNodes → agent → Log (launch log)
Plugin misbehavingManage Jenkins → System Log → add a logger
SCM polling not detecting changesJob → Git Polling Log
Pipeline internals / Groovy errorsConsole output + Replay

23.7 ✅ Checklist

  • I have a reusable "Debug" stage I can paste into any pipeline.
  • I can name the first three things to check for any failing build.
  • I know where the controller log, the launch log and the polling log live.
  • I've deliberately reproduced at least three errors from the tables above and fixed them myself.

23.8 💬 Interview Q&A

Q1A build fails in Jenkins but works on my machine. Walk me through it.
Reproduce as the Jenkins user with sudo -iu jenkins — that alone catches most of it. Then compare: tool versions, PATH, working directory, lockfile/commit status, network access to registries, and disk. Then read the first error in the console log rather than the last, and isolate the failing step with Replay.
Q2A build is stuck in the queue. Why?
Either all executors are busy or no online agent matches the label expression. I check the queue's "why" text, then Nodes for offline agents and label spelling. Long term: more agents with those labels, and per-stage agents so a long stage doesn't hold a slot for the whole run.
Q3You see "Scripts not permitted to use method…". What do you do?
It's the Groovy sandbox blocking a signature. For a one-off I approve it in In-process Script Approval after reviewing what it does — it grants that permission controller-wide. For anything recurring I move the logic into a shared library, where it runs outside the sandbox deliberately and is code-reviewed instead of click-approved.
Q4How do you diagnose a slow pipeline?
Timestamper plus the Stage View shows exactly which stage is slow. From there: dependency download (add caching), Docker layer cache (--cache-from), sequential work that could be parallel, or an agent that's simply underpowered. I also check whether slowness correlates with concurrency — that's usually executors or I/O.

23.8 Under the hood — the plugin architecture

Every Jenkins feature you use is a plugin. Understanding four extension points explains most of what you see:

ConceptWhat it isExample
ExtensionA class annotated @Extension that contributes behaviour to an extension pointA new build step, a new SCM, a new credential type
Describable + DescriptorA configurable object with metadata (display name, help, validation)A step with a UI form in the Snippet Generator
Publisher / RecorderRuns in the post phase to consume build outputsjunit, archiveArtifacts, recordIssues
Builder / SimpleBuildStepDoes work during the buildsh, docker build steps
💡Why this helps you in an interview When someone says "we solved it with a custom plugin", being able to say "that would be a SimpleBuildStep with a Descriptor, or honestly a shared-library step if it's just orchestration" shows architectural judgement rather than tool ignorance.

23.9 The plugin selection checklist

CheckWhere to lookRed flag
Actively maintainedplugins.jenkins.io — last release date, changelogNo release in 2+ years
Install baseUsage statistics on the plugin pageVery low, with no sponsor
Open issues / security advisoriesJira, GitHub, Jenkins security advisoriesOpen unfixed advisory
CompatibilityRequired core versionForces a core downgrade
OverlapCompare with what you already haveThree plugins doing the same job
Exit costCan the logic live in the pipeline instead?Config only exists in the plugin
⚠️Prefer pipeline code over plugins Anything expressed in the Jenkinsfile is visible in code review, versioned with the app, and portable. Plugin configuration lives in the controller's XML. So: use plugins for capability (Kubernetes agents, SonarQube integration), but keep policy — thresholds, ordering, gating — in the pipeline.

23.10 Lab — install, audit, remove

🧪Lab 23.1 · Plugin hygiene in practice
bashInventory and dependency audit
# What is installed, with versions -- keep this in Git
JENKINS_URL=http://localhost:8080
curl -sS "$JENKINS_URL/pluginManager/api/json?depth=1" \
  | jq -r '.plugins[] | "\(.shortName):\(.version)"' | sort > plugins-installed.txt

# Which plugins are pinned / have updates / are deprecated
curl -sS "$JENKINS_URL/pluginManager/api/json?depth=1" \
  | jq -r '.plugins[] | select(.hasUpdate or .pinned or .deleted)
           | "\(.shortName) update=\(.hasUpdate) pinned=\(.pinned) deleted=\(.deleted)"'

# Who depends on a plugin you want to remove? (dependency graph)
curl -sS "$JENKINS_URL/pluginManager/api/json?depth=2" \
  | jq -r '.plugins[] | select(.shortName=="credentials") | .dependencies[] | .shortName'
Then: uninstall one plugin you added early but no longer use, restart, and confirm nothing breaks. Always check the dependency list first — removing a shared dependency can disable several others at once.

23.12 More interview Q&A

Q6How do you manage plugin risk?
Keep the installed set minimal and pinned, record it in Git (or a plugins.txt used to build the image), subscribe to Jenkins security advisories, and test upgrades on a staging controller. Every plugin is code running with controller privileges, so the set is an attack surface, not a feature list.
Q7A plugin breaks after an upgrade. What now?
Identify the culprit from the update history, roll back that plugin (keep the previous .jpi), or restore the controller from the pre-upgrade snapshot. If it's a longer fix, check whether the functionality can move into the pipeline or a shared library so the dependency disappears.
Q8Configuration as Code vs Job DSL vs manual setup?
JCasC configures the controller (security realm, agents, tools, credentials) from YAML at boot. Job DSL creates jobs from Groovy seed scripts. Manual setup is for exploration only. In a mature setup everything is one of the first two, and Multibranch plus Jenkinsfiles means most job definitions live in Git anyway.
Q9How do you introduce a new plugin to a team?
Add it in a staging controller, prove the value in one pipeline, document the integration steps, then promote it to the shared plugin list with a pinned version — and tell the team what it replaces. Never install directly on production first.
Q10When would you write your own plugin?
Only when the behaviour genuinely cannot be expressed in a pipeline or shared library — deep integration with an internal system, a new credential type, or a custom UI. Otherwise a shared-library step is cheaper to write, test and review, and it's portable.
